## IV Andrew Amos / 四 安德鲁·埃莫斯
===Original===

I took the train three days later from King’s Cross to Edinburgh. I went to the Pentland Hotel in Princes Street and left there a suitcase containing some clean linen and a change of clothes. I had been thinking the thing out, and had come to the conclusion that I must have a base somewhere and a fresh outfit. Then in well-worn tweeds and with no more luggage than a small trench kit-bag, I descended upon the city of Glasgow.
I walked from the station to the address which Blenkiron had given me. It was a hot summer evening, and the streets were filled with bareheaded women and weary-looking artisans. As I made my way down the Dumbarton Road I was amazed at the number of able-bodied fellows about, considering that you couldn’t stir a mile on any British front without bumping up against a Glasgow battalion. Then I realized that there were such things as munitions and ships, and I wondered no more.
A stout and dishevelled lady at a close-mouth directed me to Mr. Amos’s dwelling. “Twa stairs up. Andra will be in noo, havin’ his tea. He’s no yin for overtime. He’s generally hame on the chap of six.” I ascended the stairs with a sinking heart, for like all South Africans I have a horror of dirt. The place was pretty filthy, but at each landing there were two doors with well-polished handles and brass plates. On one I read the name of Andrew Amos.
A man in his shirtsleeves opened to me, a little man, without a collar, and with an unbuttoned waistcoat. That was all I saw of him in the dim light, but he held out a paw like a gorilla’s and drew me in.
The sitting-room, which looked over many chimneys to a pale yellow sky against which two factory stalks stood out sharply, gave me light enough to observe him fully. He was about five feet four, broad-shouldered, and with a great towsy head of grizzled hair. He wore spectacles, and his face was like some old-fashioned Scots minister’s, for he had heavy eyebrows and whiskers which joined each other under his jaw, while his chin and enormous upper lip were clean-shaven. His eyes were steely grey and very solemn, but full of smouldering energy. His voice was enormous and would have shaken the walls if he had not had the habit of speaking with half-closed lips. He had not a sound tooth in his head.
A saucer full of tea and a plate which had once contained ham and eggs were on the table.

===Chinese===

三天以后，我从国王十字车站坐火车去爱丁堡。我在王子街的彭特兰旅馆存下一只手提箱，里头是几件换洗的内衣同一身替换衣裳。这件事我前前后后想过了，得出的结论是：我总得有个落脚的基地，换一身全新的行头。随后我穿上磨旧的粗花呢衣裳，只带一只小行囊，杀到格拉斯哥城。

我从车站步行到布伦基伦给我的那个地址。那是个炎热的夏夜，街上满是光着头的女人同面带倦色的工匠。顺着敦巴顿路走下去，我一路惊讶：怎么有这么多健全的汉子满街晃——要知道，在英国任何一条战线上，走不了一英里，准撞上一支格拉斯哥营。后来我想起世上还有军火同造船这两宗事，就不奇怪了。

一个巷口，一位胖墩墩、蓬头散发的太太给我指了埃莫斯先生的住处。“上两层。安德拉这会儿该到家了，正吃茶呢。他不是个肯加班的人。一般六点整就到家。”我心头一沉地往楼上走，因为像所有南非人一样，我怕脏怕得要命。这地方相当脏，可每层楼梯口都有两扇门，门把手擦得雪亮，钉着铜牌。一块铜牌上，我读到了安德鲁·埃莫斯的名字。

一个只穿衬衫的男人给我开了门，小个子，没戴领子，马甲敞着。昏暗的光线里我就看见这一些；可他伸出一只大猩猩似的手掌，把我拽了进去。

起居室的窗外是一片烟囱的海洋，衬着淡黄的天空，两根工厂烟囱高高戳出。屋里的光够我看清他了。他大约五英尺四，肩膀宽阔，一头浓密蓬乱的灰白头发。他戴着眼镜，那张脸像哪位老派苏格兰牧师：浓眉连着络腮胡子，在下巴底下会师，下巴同那片肥大的上唇剃得精光。他的眼睛是钢灰色，非常严肃，可闷着火。他的嗓门大得吓人，要不是养成了抿着半边嘴唇说话的习惯，准能把墙震塌。他满嘴没有一颗囫囵牙。

桌上一碟子茶，一只盘子，里头原先盛过火腿煎蛋。

===Original===

He nodded towards them and asked me if I had fed.
“Ye’ll no eat onything? Well, some would offer ye a dram, but this house is staunch teetotal. I door ye’ll have to try the nearest public if ye’re thirsty.”
I disclaimed any bodily wants, and produced my pipe, at which he started to fill an old clay. “Mr. Brand’s your name?” he asked in his gusty voice. “I was expectin’ ye, but Dod! man ye’re late!”
He extricated from his trousers pocket an ancient silver watch, and regarded it with disfavour. “The dashed thing has stoppit. What do ye make the time, Mr. Brand?”
He proceeded to prise open the lid of his watch with the knife he had used to cut his tobacco, and, as he examined the works, he turned the back of the case towards me. On the inside I saw pasted Mary Lamington’s purple-and-white wafer.
I held my watch so that he could see the same token. His keen eyes, raised for a second, noted it, and he shut his own with a snap and returned it to his pocket. His manner lost its wariness and became almost genial.
“Ye’ve come up to see Glasgow, Mr. Brand? Well, it’s a steerin’ bit, and there’s honest folk bides in it, and some not so honest. They tell me ye’re from South Africa. That’s a long gait away, but I ken something aboot South Africa, for I had a cousin’s son oot there for his lungs. He was in a shop in Main Street, Bloomfountain. They called him Peter Dobson. Ye would maybe mind of him.”
Then he discoursed of the Clyde. He was an incomer, he told me, from the Borders, his native place being the town of Galashiels, or, as he called it, “Gawly.” “I began as a powerloom tuner in Stavert’s mill. Then my father dee’d and I took up his trade of jiner. But it’s no world nowadays for the sma’ independent business, so I cam to the Clyde and learned a shipwright’s job. I may say I’ve become a leader in the trade, for though I’m no an official of the Union, and not likely to be, there’s no man’s word carries more weight than mine. And the Goavernment kens that, for they’ve sent me on commissions up and down the land to look at wuds and report on the nature of the timber. Bribery, they think it is, but Andrew Amos is not to be bribit. He’ll have his say about any Goavernment on earth, and tell them to their face what he thinks of them.

===Chinese===

他朝盘子点点头，问我要不要垫垫。

“你不吃点啥？得，换个人准要请你喝一盅，可这家人是铁杆的戒酒户。你要渴了，只好劳驾自己上最近的酒馆。”

我说什么也不需要，掏出烟斗；他见了，也装上一斗旧陶土烟斗。“你叫布兰德先生，是吧？”他用那阵风似的嗓门问。“我在等你，可是老天！伙计，你可来晚了。”

他从裤兜里掏出一只古旧的银表，不满地看着它。“这该死的东西停了。布兰德先生，你看几点了？”

他拿方才切烟丝的刀撬开表盖，查看机芯的时候，把表壳的背面转向我。表壳内侧，我看见贴着玛丽·拉明顿那枚紫白两色的封缄帖。

我把我的表举起来，好叫他看见同样的记号。他敏锐的眼睛抬了一秒，认下了，啪的一声合上表，放回口袋。他那份戒备松了，几乎变得和气起来。

“你是上来逛格拉斯哥的，布兰德先生？呃，这地方是个热闹码头，住着老实人，也住着不怎么老实的人。他们告诉我，你是南非来的。那可是老远的路；不过南非的事我也懂一点，我有个表侄在那边养肺。他在布隆方丹主街上的一家铺子里做事。人家叫他彼得·多布森。你也许记得他。”

随后他大谈起克莱德河。他告诉我，他是外来的，老家在边区，是加拉希尔斯镇——照他的叫法，“加利”。“我起先在斯塔弗特的厂里做力织机调机工。后来我爹死了，我接了他的木匠行当。可如今这世道，小本独立生意做不成，我就到了克莱德，学了一门造船的活。不妨说，我在这一行算个头领；我虽不是工会的官，往后多半也不会是，可全城没有谁的话比我的话更压秤。政府也知道这个，他们派我坐着车满国跑，去看林子，报告木料的成色。他们当这是收买。可安德鲁·埃莫斯是收买不动的。天底下随便哪个政府，他都要说上两句，当着他们的面，把对他的看法一五一十讲给他们听。”

===Original===

Ay, and he’ll fight the case of the workingman against his oppressor, should it be the Goavernment or the fatted calves they ca’ Labour Members. Ye’ll have heard tell o’ the shop stewards, Mr. Brand?”
I admitted I had, for I had been well coached by Blenkiron in the current history of industrial disputes.
“Well, I’m a shop steward. We represent the rank and file against office-bearers that have lost the confidence o’ the workingman. But I’m no socialist, and I would have ye keep mind of that. I’m yin o’ the old Border radicals, and I’m not like to change. I’m for individual liberty and equal rights and chances for all men. I’ll no more bow down before a Dagon of a Goavernment official than before the Baal of a feckless Tweedside laird. I’ve to keep my views to mysel’, for thae young lads are all drucken-daft with their wee books about Cawpital and Collectivism and a wheen long senseless words I wouldna fyle my tongue with. Them and their socialism! There’s more gumption in a page of John Stuart Mill than in all that foreign trash. But, as I say, I’ve got to keep a quiet sough, for the world is gettin’ socialism now like the measles. It all comes of a defective eddication.”
“And what does a Border radical say about the war?” I asked.
He took off his spectacles and cocked his shaggy brows at me. “I’ll tell ye, Mr. Brand. All that was bad in all that I’ve ever wrestled with since I cam to years o’ discretion﻿—Tories and lairds and manufacturers and publicans and the Auld Kirk﻿—all that was bad, I say, for there were orra bits of decency, ye’ll find in the Germans full measure pressed down and running over. When the war started, I considered the subject calmly for three days, and then I said: ‘Andra Amos, ye’ve found the enemy at last. The ones ye fought before were in a manner o’ speakin’ just misguided friends. It’s either you or the Kaiser this time, my man!’ ”
His eyes had lost their gravity and had taken on a sombre ferocity. “Ay, and I’ve not wavered. I got a word early in the business as to the way I could serve my country best. It’s not been an easy job, and there’s plenty of honest folk the day will give me a bad name. They think I’m stirrin’ up the men at home and desertin’ the cause o’ the lads at the front. Man, I’m keepin’ them straight.

===Chinese===

是啊，工人受了欺负，他还要替工人打官司，哪怕欺负人的是政府，或者是他们唤作工党议员的那群养肥了的牛犊。工场代表这个词，你总听说过吧，布兰德先生？

我承认听说过——布伦基伦把当前工潮的近代史给我恶补过一番。

“呃，我就是个工场代表。我们代表普通兄弟，对付那些失了工人信任的工会官。可我不是社会主义者，这一点你记牢。我是老边区急进派里的一个，不打算改。我主张个人自由，人人平等的权利同机会。政府官儿是尊大衮神像，特威德河边没出息的地主是尊巴力神像，两样的神我都不拜。这些话我只得闷在自己肚里，因为如今的小伙子们全叫那几本讲‘资本’呀‘集体主义’呀的小册子灌醉了，还有一大串又长又没意思的词，我脏了舌头都不肯说。社会主义，哼！约翰·斯图尔特·密尔一页书里的见识，也比那一堆外国破烂多。可是我说了，我只得装个闷葫芦，如今这世道得社会主义，就跟出麻疹似的普遍。说到底，都怪教育有毛病。”

“那么，一位边区急进派怎么看待这场战争呢？”我问。

他摘下眼镜，乱蓬蓬的眉毛朝我一竖。“我告诉你，布兰德先生。我懂事以来同它摔跤的一切东西——托利党呀，地主呀，厂主呀，酒馆老板呀，国教呀——里头一切坏的东西——我说的是坏的东西，里头也零零碎碎有几片好处——你在德国人身上全能找到，还装得满满的、冒了尖。开战那阵，我把这桩事冷静盘算了三天，然后我说：‘安德拉·埃莫斯，你可算找着真正的对头了。从前你打的那些，说起来不过是走岔了道的朋友。这一回，不是你死，就是德皇！’”

他眼睛里的严肃褪了，换上一副阴沉的凶劲。“是啊，我从没动摇过。事情一开头，就有人捎话给我，指点我怎样替国家出力最顶用。这差事不好干，如今多少老实人要给我安个恶名。他们当我是在后方煽动工人，扔下前线弟兄的事业不管。伙计，我是在把他们扶正。

===Original===

If I didna fight their battles on a sound economic isshue, they would take the dorts and be at the mercy of the first blagyird that preached revolution. Me and my like are safety-valves, if ye follow me. And dinna you make ony mistake, Mr. Brand. The men that are agitating for a rise in wages are not for peace. They’re fighting for the lads overseas as much as for themselves. There’s not yin in a thousand that wouldna sweat himself blind to beat the Germans. The Goavernment has made mistakes, and maun be made to pay for them. If it were not so, the men would feel like a moose in a trap, for they would have no way to make their grievance felt. What for should the big man double his profits and the small man be ill set to get his ham and egg on Sabbath mornin’? That’s the meaning o’ Labour unrest, as they call it, and it’s a good thing, says I, for if Labour didna get its leg over the traces now and then, the spunk o’ the land would be dead in it, and Hindenburg could squeeze it like a rotten aipple.”
I asked if he spoke for the bulk of the men.
“For ninety percent in ony ballot. I don’t say that there’s not plenty of riffraff﻿—the pint-and-a-dram gentry and the soft-heads that are aye reading bits of newspapers, and muddlin’ their wits with foreign whigmaleeries. But the average man on the Clyde, like the average man in ither places, hates just three things, and that’s the Germans, the profiteers, as they call them, and the Irish. But he hates the Germans first.”
“The Irish!” I exclaimed in astonishment.
“Ay, the Irish,” cried the last of the old Border radicals. “Glasgow’s stinkin’ nowadays with two things, money and Irish. I mind the day when I followed Mr. Gladstone’s Home Rule policy, and used to threep about the noble, generous, warmhearted sister nation held in a foreign bondage. My Goad! I’m not speakin’ about Ulster, which is a dour, ill-natured den, but our own folk all the same. But the men that will not do a hand’s turn to help the war and take the chance of our necessities to set up a bawbee rebellion are hateful to Goad and man. We treated them like pet lambs and that’s the thanks we get. They’re coming over here in thousands to tak the jobs of the lads that are doing their duty. I was speakin’ last week to a widow woman that keeps a wee dairy down the Dalmarnock Road.

===Chinese===

这些仗要是我不在正当的经济题目上替他们打，他们就要闹小性子，随随便便叫头一个鼓吹革命的痞子牵着鼻子走。我同我这样的人，是安全阀，你明白吧。还有，你可别弄错，布兰德先生。那些闹着要涨工钱的人，不是要和平。他们既为自己，也一样为海外的弟兄们打仗。一千个人里头，挑不出一个不肯累瞎了眼也要打赢德国人的。政府办错了事，就得逼它付出代价。不这样，工人们就像掉进夹子的老鼠，有冤没处诉。凭什么大人物利润翻番，小人物连安息日早晨的一顿火腿蛋都难吃上？他们所谓的‘工潮’，根子就在这儿；依我说，这是好事。劳工要不时时刻刻把腿从辕里跨出来一趟，这地方的血性就死绝了，兴登堡就能像捏一只烂苹果似的把它捏扁。”

我问他能不能代表大多数工人。

“投票的话，九成。我不说没有渣滓——一品脱带一盅的爷们，还有那些专爱捡报纸片看、拿外国的歪道理糊自己脑子的软脑壳。可是克莱德的平常人，同别处的平常人一样，恨三样东西：德国人，还有他们叫做‘发战争财的’，还有爱尔兰人。不过恨德国人排第一。”

“爱尔兰人！”我吃了一惊，喊出来。

“是啊，爱尔兰人，”这位老边区急进派的末代传人喊道。“如今的格拉斯哥，叫两样东西熏臭了：钱，同爱尔兰人。我记得从前的日子，我追随格莱斯顿先生的地方自治政策，逢人就讲什么高尚、慷慨、热心肠的姊妹民族，被外国枷锁锁着。我的天！阿尔斯特我不说，那是个又倔又坏的窝，可到底是自家人。可是那种连一个手指头都不肯帮战争一把、倒趁着我们急难起一场小便宜叛乱的人，神和人恨的他们全占了。我们拿他们当心肝羊羔待，得的就是这么一声谢。他们成千上万地涌过来，抢那些在前方尽本分的弟兄的饭碗。上礼拜我还同一个寡妇说话，她在达尔马诺克路那边开着一家小奶铺。

===Original===

She has two sons, and both in the airmy, one in the Cameronians and one a prisoner in Germany. She was telling me that she could not keep goin’ any more, lacking the help of the boys, though she had worked her fingers to the bone. ‘Surely it’s a crool job, Mr. Amos,’ she says, ‘that the Goavernment should tak baith my laddies, and I’ll maybe never see them again, and let the Irish gang free and tak the bread frae our mouth. At the gasworks across the road they took on a hundred Irish last week, and every yin o’ them as young and well set up as you would ask to see. And my wee Davie, him that’s in Germany, had aye a weak chest, and Jimmy was troubled wi’ a bowel complaint. That’s surely no justice!’﻿ ﻿…”
He broke off and lit a match by drawing it across the seat of his trousers. “It’s time I got the gas lichtit. There’s some men coming here at half-ten.”
As the gas squealed and flickered in the lighting, he sketched for me the coming guests. “There’s Macnab and Niven, two o’ my colleagues. And there’s Gilkison of the Boiler-fitters, and a lad Wilkie﻿—he’s got consumption, and writes wee bits in the papers. And there’s a queer chap o’ the name o’ Tombs﻿—they tell me he comes frae Cambridge, and is a kind of a professor there﻿—anyway he’s more stuffed wi’ havers than an egg wi’ meat. He telled me he was here to get at the heart o’ the workingman, and I said to him that he would hae to look a bit further than the sleeve o’ the workin’-man’s jaicket. There’s no muckle in his head, poor soul. Then there’ll be Tam Norie, him that edits our weekly paper﻿—*Justice for All*. Tam’s a humorist and great on Robert Burns, but he hasna the balance o’ a dwinin’ teetotum﻿ ﻿… Ye’ll understand, Mr. Brand, that I keep my mouth shut in such company, and don’t express my own views more than is absolutely necessary. I criticize whiles, and that gives me a name of whunstane common sense, but I never let my tongue wag. The feck o’ the lads comin’ the night are not the real workingman﻿—they’re just the froth on the pot, but it’s the froth that will be useful to you. Remember they’ve heard tell o’ ye already, and ye’ve some sort o’ reputation to keep up.”
“Will Mr. Abel Gresson be here?” I asked.
“No,” he said. “Not yet. Him and me havena yet got to the point o’ payin’ visits. But the men that come will be Gresson’s friends and they’ll speak of ye to him.

===Chinese===

她有两个儿子，都在军队里，一个在卡梅伦人营，一个在德国做俘虏。她告诉我，两个帮手一走，她就是把手指出力到骨头，也撑不下去了。‘这真是狠心的事啊，埃莫斯先生，’她说，‘政府把我两个小子都拿了去，兴许我再也见不着他们了，倒放爱尔兰人自由自在，来抢我们嘴里的面包。路对面煤气厂上礼拜进了一百个爱尔兰人，个个都齐整得没得挑。我那小戴维——在德国的那个——胸口从小就弱，吉米又闹肚子。这总不是公道吧！’……”

他顿住，拿火柴在裤裆上一划，点着了。“该点煤气灯了。十点半有人来。”

煤气灯点着的时候吱吱尖叫、忽闪，他趁着光给我挨个介绍今晚要来的客人。“有麦克纳布同尼文，我的两个同事。有锅炉装修工会的吉尔基森，还有个小伙子威尔基——他得了痨病，常在报纸上写点小文章。还有一个姓汤姆斯的怪家伙——听说他是剑桥来的，在那边算个教授——反正他肚子里的废话比鸡蛋里的肉还满。他对我说，他来是要摸到工人的心坎；我对他说，那他可得看得比工人夹克的袖子远一点。他脑子里没多少东西，可怜的家伙。再有就是塔姆·诺里，编我们那份周报《人人正义》的。塔姆是个滑稽人，迷罗伯特·彭斯迷得厉害，可他那点判断力，还不如一只转歪了的陀螺……你要明白，布兰德先生，这种场合我闭紧嘴巴，非绝对必要不亮自己的观点。我时不时挑点错，落下个花岗岩常识的名声，可我从不多嘴。今晚来的小伙子们，多数不是真正的工人——不过是锅面上那层沫子，可对你有用的正是这层沫子。记住，他们已经听说过你了，你那点名声，还得撑住。”

“埃布尔·格雷森先生会来么？”我问。

“不，”他说。“眼下还不会。他同我还没走到互相登门的地步。可来的都是格雷森的朋友，他们会把我今晚的话捎到他耳朵里。

===Original===

It’s the best kind of introduction ye could seek.”
The knocker sounded, and Mr. Amos hastened to admit the first comers. These were Macnab and Wilkie: the one a decent middle-aged man with a fresh-washed face and a celluloid collar, the other a round-shouldered youth, with lank hair and the large eyes and luminous skin which are the marks of phthisis. “This is Mr. Brand boys, from South Africa,” was Amos’s presentation. Presently came Niven, a bearded giant, and Mr. Norie, the editor, a fat dirty fellow smoking a rank cigar. Gilkison of the Boiler-fitters, when he arrived, proved to be a pleasant young man in spectacles who spoke with an educated voice and clearly belonged to a slightly different social scale. Last came Tombs, the Cambridge professor, a lean youth with a sour mouth and eyes that reminded me of Launcelot Wake.
“Ye’ll no be a mawgnate, Mr. Brand, though ye come from South Africa,” said Mr. Norie with a great guffaw.
“Not me. I’m a working engineer,” I said. “My father was from Scotland, and this is my first visit to my native country, as my friend Mr. Amos was telling you.”
The consumptive looked at me suspiciously. “We’ve got two﻿—three of the comrades here that the cawpitalist Government expelled from the Transvaal. If ye’re our way of thinking, ye will maybe ken them.”
I said I would be overjoyed to meet them, but that at the time of the outrage in question I had been working on a mine a thousand miles further north.
Then ensued an hour of extraordinary talk. Tombs in his singsong namby-pamby University voice was concerned to get information. He asked endless questions, chiefly of Gilkison, who was the only one who really understood his language. I thought I had never seen anyone quite so fluent and so futile, and yet there was a kind of feeble violence in him like a demented sheep. He was engaged in venting some private academic spite against society, and I thought that in a revolution he would be the class of lad I would personally conduct to the nearest lamppost. And all the while Amos and Macnab and Niven carried on their own conversation about the affairs of their society, wholly impervious to the tornado raging around them.
It was Mr. Norie, the editor, who brought me into the discussion.
“Our South African friend is very blate,” he said in his boisterous way.

===Chinese===

这是你能求到的顶好的引荐。”

门环响了，埃莫斯赶去迎头一批客人。是麦克纳布同威尔基：一个体面的中年人，脸刚洗过，戴赛珞珞领子；另一个是驼背的青年，头发稀软，一双大眼睛、一副痨病发光的皮肤。“这是布兰德先生，小伙子们，南非来的，”埃莫斯这样引见。跟着来的是尼文，一个大胡子巨人；还有诺里先生，那位编辑，一个肥胖的脏汉子，抽一支呛人的雪茄。锅炉装修工会的吉尔基森到的时候，看来是位和气的年轻人，戴眼镜，说话是受过教育的口音，分明出身于略高一等的社会。最后到的是汤姆斯，那位剑桥教授，一个瘦青年，嘴角刻薄，那双眼睛叫我想起朗斯洛·韦克。

“你从南非来，该不会是个‘马格奈特’（大亨）吧，布兰德先生？”诺里先生哈哈大笑着说。

“我不是。我是个干活的机务工程师，”我说。“我父亲是苏格兰人，这是我头一回到我自己的祖国，我朋友埃莫斯先生方才告诉过你们。”

那痨病青年狐疑地打量我。“我们这儿有两三位——三位同志，是资本家政府从德兰士瓦驱逐出来的。你要是同我们一条心，兴许认识他们。”

我说，同他们会面我高兴还来不及，只是那桩暴行发生的时候，我正在北边一千英里外的一座矿上做工。

接着是一个钟头妙不可言的谈话。汤姆斯用他那种大学里咿咿呀呀的娘娘腔缠着人打听。他问个没完，多半问吉尔基森，因为满屋只有吉尔基森真听得懂他的话。我从没见过这样能说又这样无用的人，可他身上有一种软塌塌的凶劲，像一只疯羊。他是在对社会发泄哪一桩私人的学院冤气；我心想，真要闹起革命，这种小子，我要亲手押他到最近的路灯杆底下。这一晚上，埃莫斯、麦克纳布同尼文自顾自谈他们工会的事务，对身边这场龙卷风全然无觉。

把我拉进讨论的，是那位编辑诺里先生。“咱们这位南非朋友脸皮太薄，”他大嗓门说。

===Original===

“Andra, if this place of yours wasn’t so damned teetotal and we had a dram apiece, we might get his tongue loosened. I want to hear what he’s got to say about the war. You told me this morning he was sound in the faith.”
“I said no such thing,” said Mr. Amos. “As ye ken well, Tam Norie, I don’t judge soundness on that matter as you judge it. I’m for the war myself, subject to certain conditions that I’ve often stated. I know nothing of Mr. Brand’s opinions, except that he’s a good democrat, which is more than I can say of some o’ your friends.”
“Hear to Andra,” laughed Mr. Norie. “He’s thinkin’ the inspector in the Socialist State would be a waur kind of awristocrat then the Duke of Buccleuch. Weel, there’s maybe something in that. But about the war he’s wrong. Ye ken my views, boys. This war was made by the cawpitalists, and it has been fought by the workers, and it’s the workers that maun have the ending of it. That day’s comin’ very near. There are those that want to spin it out till Labour is that weak it can be pit in chains for the rest o’ time. That’s the manoeuvre we’re out to prevent. We’ve got to beat the Germans, but it’s the workers that has the right to judge when the enemy’s beaten and not the cawpitalists. What do you say, Mr. Brand?”
Mr. Norie had obviously pinned his colours to the fence, but he gave me the chance I had been looking for. I let them have my views with a vengeance, and these views were that for the sake of democracy the war must be ended. I flatter myself I put my case well, for I had got up every rotten argument and I borrowed largely from Launcelot Wake’s armoury. But I didn’t put it too well, for I had a very exact notion of the impression I wanted to produce. I must seem to be honest and in earnest, just a bit of a fanatic, but principally a hardheaded businessman who knew when the time had come to make a deal. Tombs kept interrupting me with imbecile questions, and I had to sit on him. At the end Mr. Norie hammered with his pipe on the table.
“That’ll sort ye, Andra. Ye’re entertain’ an angel unawares. What do ye say to that, my man?”
Mr. Amos shook his head. “I’ll no deny there’s something in it, but I’m not convinced that the Germans have got enough of a wheepin’.” Macnab agreed with him; the others were with me.

===Chinese===

“安德拉，你这地方要不是该死的戒酒，一人来上一盅，咱们兴许能把他舌头松开。我要听听他对战争的看法。今儿早上你告诉我，他信仰端正。”

“我可没那么说，”埃莫斯先生说。“你清楚得很，塔姆·诺里，信仰端不端正，我的量法同你的不一样。我自己拥护战争，附几个条件，这我常讲。布兰德先生的见解我一点不知道，只知道他是个好民主派——比你的哪一位朋友都强，这话我可是说了。”

“听听安德拉这话，”诺里先生笑。“他心里想，社会主义国家里的稽查官，要比巴克卢公爵还坏上一等的老爷。呃，这话兴许有点道理。可对战争，他错了。小伙子们，我的观点你们知道。这场战争是资本家打出来的，打仗出力的是工人，收尾也必须由工人来收。那一天近得很了。有人想把战争拖啊拖，拖到劳工弱得可以拿链子锁起来锁到世界末日。我们要防的就是这一手。德国人要打垮，可敌人算不算打垮了，有权下判断的是工人，不是资本家。你说呢，布兰德先生？”

诺里先生显然把旗子插在了篱笆上，可他给了我正等的机会。我把我的观点狠狠倒了出来：为了民主，这场战争必须了结。我自夸那番话说得不坏——那些烂透的论点我全都背熟了，还从朗斯洛·韦克的武库里大量借用。可我也没有说得太好，因为我要造成什么印象，心里有一本极精确的账。我必须显得诚实、认真，带那么一点狂热，但主要是一个精明的生意人，知道成交的时机到了。汤姆斯不住拿些蠢问题打岔，我只好压他一压。末了，诺里先生拿烟斗敲桌子。

“这总该治了你了吧，安德拉。你这是不留神招待了一位天使。这话你怎么说，伙计？”

埃莫斯先生摇头。“我不否认这里头有点东西，可要我信德国人挨的鞭子够数了，还早。”麦克纳布赞成他；其余的人站在我这边。

===Original===

Norie was for getting me to write an article for his paper, and the consumptive wanted me to address a meeting.
“Wull ye say a’ that over again the morn’s night down at our hall in Newmilns Street? We’ve got a lodge meeting o’ the I.W.B., and I’ll make them pit ye in the programme.” He kept his luminous eyes, like a sick dog’s, fixed on me, and I saw that I had made one ally. I told him I had come to Glasgow to learn and not to teach, but I would miss no chance of testifying to my faith.
“Now, boys, I’m for my bed,” said Amos, shaking the dottle from his pipe. “Mr. Tombs, I’ll conduct ye the morn over the Brigend works, but I’ve had enough clavers for one evening. I’m a man that wants his eight hours’ sleep.”
The old fellow saw them to the door, and came back to me with the ghost of a grin in his face.
“A queer crowd, Mr. Brand! Macnab didna like what ye said. He had a laddie killed in Gallypoly, and he’s no lookin’ for peace this side the grave. He’s my best friend in Glasgow. He’s an elder in the Gaelic kirk in the Cowcaddens, and I’m what ye call a freethinker, but we’re wonderful agreed on the fundamentals. Ye spoke your bit verra well, I must admit. Gresson will hear tell of ye as a promising recruit.”
“It’s a rotten job,” I said.
“Ay, it’s a rotten job. I often feel like vomiting over it mysel’. But it’s no for us to complain. There’s waur jobs oot in France for better men﻿ ﻿… A word in your ear, Mr. Brand. Could ye not look a bit more sheepish? Ye stare folk ower straight in the een, like a Hieland sergeant-major up at Maryhill Barracks.” And he winked slowly and grotesquely with his left eye.
He marched to a cupboard and produced a black bottle and glass. “I’m blue-ribbon myself, but ye’ll be the better of something to tak the taste out of your mouth. There’s Loch Katrine water at the pipe there﻿ ﻿… As I was saying, there’s not much ill in that lot. Tombs is a black offence, but a dominie’s a dominie all the world over. They may crack about their Industrial Workers and the braw things they’re going to do, but there’s a wholesome dampness about the tinder on Clydeside. They should try Ireland.”
“Supposing,” I said, “there was a really clever man who wanted to help the enemy. You think he could do little good by stirring up trouble in the shops here?”
“I’m positive.”
“And if he were a shrewd fellow, he’d soon tumble to that?”
“Ay.”

===Chinese===

诺里要我为他的报纸写一篇文章，那痨病青年要我去看大会上演讲。

“明儿晚上，你肯不肯把这一套再原样说一遍？在纽密尔恩斯街我们的会堂。我们工场代表有个集会，我把你排进节目单里。”他一双发亮的大眼睛，像病狗的眼睛似的，定定望着我；我明白我赚到了一个同盟。我告诉他，我来格拉斯哥是来学，不是来教，可凡是能见证我信仰的机会，我一个也不放过。

“好了，小伙子们，我要睡了，”埃莫斯说，磕净烟斗里的灰。“汤姆斯先生，明儿我带你逛布里根德厂子；今晚这些闲扯我受够了。我是个要睡足八个钟头的人。”

老头子把众人送到门口，回来时脸上带着一丝笑影。

“一伙怪人，布兰德先生！麦克纳布不爱听你那套。他一个小子死在加利波利了，不到进坟墓，他不想要和平。他是我在格拉斯哥最好的朋友。他是考卡登斯盖尔语教会的长老，我是你们所说的自由思想者，可在根本的大事上，我们合得得出奇。你那一段说得很好，我认账。格雷森会听说你是个有出息的新兵。”

“这是份烂差事，”我说。

“是啊，烂差事。我自己也常想吐。可轮不到咱们抱怨。法兰西还有更烂的差事，派给比咱们强的人……附耳过来，布兰德先生。你能不能显得再窝囊一点？你看人瞪得太直，像玛丽希尔兵营里的高地团士官长。”他冲我慢慢眨了眨左眼，样子怪诞。

他大步走到柜子跟前，拿出一瓶黑酒同一只杯子。“我自己是系蓝丝带的戒酒人，可你嘴里总得压点什么味儿。水龙头里有卡特林湖的水……我说到哪儿了——那一伙里没有多少坏心眼。汤姆斯是桩黑罪过，可教书匠走到哪儿都是教书匠。他们尽管去吹他们的产业工人，同他们要干的那一番漂亮事吧，克莱德河边的火种总有股健康的潮气。他们该去爱尔兰试试。”

“假定，”我说，“有个顶聪明的人，存心帮敌人。你认为他在这里的厂子里煽风点火，办不成什么好事？”

“我拿得稳。”

“那么他要是个精明人，很快也会看破这一层？”

“是啊。”

===Original===

“Then if he still stayed on here he would be after bigger game﻿—something really dangerous and damnable?”
Amos drew down his brows and looked me in the face. “I see what ye’re ettlin’ at. Ay! That would be my conclusion. I came to it weeks syne about the man ye’ll maybe meet the morn’s night.”
Then from below the bed he pulled a box from which he drew a handsome flute. “Ye’ll forgive me, Mr. Brand, but I aye like a tune before I go to my bed. Macnab says his prayers, and I have a tune on the flute, and the principle is just the same.”
So that singular evening closed with music﻿—very sweet and true renderings of old Border melodies like “My Peggy is a young thing,” and “When the kye come hame.” I fell asleep with a vision of Amos, his face all puckered up at the mouth and a wandering sentiment in his eye, recapturing in his dingy world the emotions of a boy.
---
The widow-woman from next door, who acted as housekeeper, cook, and general factotum to the establishment, brought me shaving water next morning, but I had to go without a bath. When I entered the kitchen I found no one there, but while I consumed the inevitable ham and egg, Amos arrived back for breakfast. He brought with him the morning’s paper.
“The *Herald* says there’s been a big battle at Eepers,” he announced.
I tore open the sheet and read of the great attack of 31 July which was spoiled by the weather. “My God!” I cried. “They’ve got St. Julien and that dirty Frezenberg ridge﻿ ﻿… and Hooge﻿ ﻿… and Sanctuary Wood. I know every inch of the damned place.﻿ ﻿…”
“Mr. Brand,” said a warning voice, “that’ll never do. If our friends last night heard ye talk like that ye might as well tak the train back to London﻿ ﻿… They’re speakin’ about ye in the yards this morning. Ye’ll get a good turnout at your meeting the night, but they’re sayin’ that the polis will interfere. That mightna be a bad thing, but I trust ye to show discretion, for ye’ll not be muckle use to onybody if they jyle ye in Duke Street. I hear Gresson will be there with a fraternal message from his lunatics in America﻿ ﻿… I’ve arranged that ye go down to Tam Norie this afternoon and give him a hand with his bit paper. Tam will tell ye the whole clash o’ the West country, and I look to ye to keep him off the drink.

===Chinese===

“那么，他要是还赖在这儿不走，他要钓的就是更大的鱼——一样真正危险、真正缺德的东西？”

埃莫斯把眉毛压下来，直看我的脸。“我明白你往哪儿引。是啊！我也会得出这个结论。几个礼拜以前我就得出来了——为的是你明晚兴许要见的那个人。”

他从床底下拖出一只匣子，从里头取出一支漂亮的长笛。“你担待，布兰德先生，我睡前总要来一支曲子。麦克纳布做他的祷告，我吹我的笛子，道理原是一样的。”

那个奇特的夜晚就这样在音乐里收了尾——老边区调子吹得又甜又准，像《我的佩吉正年轻》，像《牛儿回家了》。我在睡意里还望见埃莫斯：嘴边皱成一团，眼睛里游着一点缠绵，在他那间昏暗的屋子里，重新捉回了一个少年的种种心情。

---

第二天早晨，隔壁那位寡妇——她在这户人家一身兼任管家、厨娘同一切杂役——给我端来刮脸水，澡却是洗不成了。我进厨房时屋里没人；我吃着躲不掉的火腿煎蛋，埃莫斯回来吃早饭，手里拿着当天的报纸。

“《先驱报》说伊普尔打了一场大仗。”他宣布。

我扯开报纸，读到七月三十一日那场被天气毁了的大攻势。“我的天！”我喊。“他们拿下了圣朱利安，还有弗雷岑贝格那条肮脏的山梁……还有胡赫……还有圣所林。那鬼地方的每一寸土我都认得。……”

“布兰德先生，”一个警告的声音说，“这可万万使不得。昨晚那伙朋友要听见你这么讲话，你干脆坐火车回伦敦得了……今儿早上厂子里都在讲你。今晚你那个会人到得不会少，可他们说警察要来搅局。那兴许不是坏事，可你放机灵些；他们要把你关进杜克街监狱，你就谁也帮不上了。我听说格雷森今晚要去，还带着他那群美国疯子的一封兄弟贺词……我已经安排好了，今儿下午你下塔姆·诺里那儿去，帮他弄他那份小报。塔姆会把西边一带着实给你讲个透，我还指望你看住他，别叫他喝酒。

===Original===

He’s aye arguin’ that writin’ and drinkin’ gang thegither, and quotin’ Robert Burns, but the creature has a wife and five bairns dependin’ on him.”
I spent a fantastic day. For two hours I sat in Norie’s dirty den, while he smoked and orated, and, when he remembered his business, took down in shorthand my impressions of the Labour situation in South Africa for his rag. They were fine breezy impressions, based on the most wholehearted ignorance, and if they ever reached the Rand I wonder what my friends there made of Cornelius Brand, their author. I stood him dinner in an indifferent eating-house in a street off the Broomielaw, and thereafter had a drink with him in a public-house, and was introduced to some of his less reputable friends.
About teatime I went back to Amos’s lodgings, and spent an hour or so writing a long letter to Mr. Ivery. I described to him everybody I had met, I gave highly coloured views of the explosive material on the Clyde, and I deplored the lack of clearheadedness in the progressive forces. I drew an elaborate picture of Amos, and deduced from it that the Radicals were likely to be a bar to true progress. “They have switched their old militancy,” I wrote, “on to another track, for with them it is a matter of conscience to be always militant.” I finished up with some very crude remarks on economics culled from the table-talk of the egregious Tombs. It was the kind of letter which I hoped would establish my character in his mind as an industrious innocent.
Seven o’clock found me in Newmilns Street, where I was seized upon by Wilkie. He had put on a clean collar for the occasion and had partially washed his thin face. The poor fellow had a cough that shook him like the walls of a powerhouse when the dynamos are going.
He was very apologetic about Amos. “Andra belongs to a past worrld,” he said. “He has a big reputation in his society, and he’s a fine fighter, but he has no kind of Vision, if ye understand me. He’s an auld Gladstonian, and that’s done and damned in Scotland. He’s not a Modern, Mr. Brand, like you and me. But tonight ye’ll meet one or two chaps that’ll be worth your while to ken. Ye’ll maybe no go quite as far as them, but ye’re on the same road. I’m hoping for the day when we’ll have oor Councils of Workmen and Soldiers like the Russians all over the land and dictate our terms to the pawrasites in Pawrliament.

===Chinese===

他一天到晚论证写文章同喝酒是一码事，还引罗伯特·彭斯作证。可那可怜虫家里有老婆，还有五个娃张着嘴等他。”

我过了一天光怪陆离的日子。有两个钟头，我坐在诺里那间肮脏的小屋里，他抽着烟高谈阔论，偶尔记起自己的正事，就用速记记下我对南非劳工情形的印象，给他那张破报。那些印象写得又痛快又豪爽，根子上是一无所知；它们真要传到兰德去，我不知那边的朋友们要把它们的作者科尼利厄斯·布兰德当成什么人。我在布鲁米洛旁边一条街上的一家马马虎虎的饭馆请他吃了饭，随后又同他进酒馆喝了一盅，经他引见，认识了他几位不大体面的朋友。

傍晚茶点时分，我回埃莫斯的住处，花了一个来钟头，给艾弗里先生写了一封长信。我把见过的人挨个描给他听，把克莱德河上这一堆火药绘声绘色渲染了一通，又对进步力量里头脑不清深表痛惜。我细细画了一幅埃莫斯的像，从里头推出结论：急进派恐怕要变成真正进步的绊脚石。“他们把旧日的好斗性换了轨道，”我写道，“因为对这些人来说，好斗永远关乎良心。”结尾我又从那位了不得的汤姆斯的席间读话里捡了几段政治经济学，糟蹋得不成样子。我要的就是这样一封信——好在他心里给我立起一个勤勤恳恳的傻子的形象。

七点钟，我到了纽密尔恩斯街，威尔基一把逮住了我。他为了这场合换上了干净领子，那张瘦脸也马马虎虎洗过了一半。这可怜人咳嗽起来，抖得像开动发电机的厂房墙壁。

他对埃莫斯满怀歉意。“安德拉属于一个过去了的世界，”他说。“他在他的行会里名声很大，也是一员好斗士，可他没有一点儿‘远见’，你懂我的意思。他是老格莱斯顿派，在苏格兰这一派早完蛋、早定了罪了。他不是个‘现代人’，布兰德先生，不像你同我。可今晚你会见到一两位值得结识的人物。你兴许跟不上他们那么远，可你们走的是同一条路。我盼着有那么一天，咱们全城遍地都是俄国人那样的工兵代表会，对议会里那群寄生虫发号施令。

===Original===

They tell me, too, the boys in the trenches are comin’ round to our side.”
We entered the hall by a back door, and in a little waiting-room I was introduced to some of the speakers. They were a scratch lot as seen in that dingy place. The chairman was a shop-steward in one of the Societies, a fierce little rat of a man, who spoke with a cockney accent and addressed me as “Comrade.” But one of them roused my liveliest interest. I heard the name of Gresson, and turned to find a fellow of about thirty-five, rather sprucely dressed, with a flower in his buttonhole. “Mr. Brand,” he said, in a rich American voice which recalled Blenkiron’s. “Very pleased to meet you, sir. We have come from remote parts of the globe to be present at this gathering.” I noticed that he had reddish hair, and small bright eyes, and a nose with a droop like a Polish Jew’s.
As soon as we reached the platform I saw that there was going to be trouble. The hall was packed to the door, and in all the front half there was the kind of audience I expected to see﻿—workingmen of the political type who before the war would have thronged to party meetings. But not all the crowd at the back had come to listen. Some were scallawags, some looked like better-class clerks out for a spree, and there was a fair quantity of khaki. There were also one or two gentlemen not strictly sober.
The chairman began by putting his foot in it. He said we were there tonight to protest against the continuation of the war and to form a branch of the new British Council of Workmen and Soldiers. He told them with a fine mixture of metaphors that we had got to take the reins into our own hands, for the men who were running the war had their own axes to grind and were marching to oligarchy through the blood of the workers. He added that we had no quarrel with Germany half as bad as we had with our own capitalists. He looked forward to the day when British soldiers would leap from their trenches and extend the hand of friendship to their German comrades.
“No me!” said a solemn voice. “I’m not seekin’ a bullet in my wame,”﻿—at which there was laughter and catcalls.
Tombs followed and made a worse hash of it. He was determined to speak, as he would have put it, to democracy in its own language, so he said “hell” several times, loudly but without conviction.

===Chinese===

他们还告诉我，战壕里的小伙子们也在朝我们这边转。”

我们从后门进了会堂，在一间小候场室里，有人给我引见了几位演讲人。在那间昏暗屋子的光线下，他们是凑起来的一支杂牌军。主席是某工会的一位工场代表，一个凶巴巴的小个子，活像一只耗子，说话带伦敦东城腔，管我叫“同志”。可其中一位引了我最大的兴趣。我听见格雷森这个名字，转过身，见一个三十五岁上下的汉子，穿得相当齐整，扣眼里插一朵花。“布兰德先生，”他说，一口浓重的美国腔，叫我想起布伦基伦。“幸会，先生。我们从天南地北的角落赶来，专诚出席今晚这个大会。”我注意到他一头红棕头发，一双亮得出奇的小眼睛，鼻子带一点波兰犹太人式的下勾。

我们一上讲台，我就看出要出乱子。会堂挤到了门口，前半场是我料想的那种听众——政治型的工人，战前挤满政党大会的那种人。可后半场并非全是来听的。有的是流氓，有的像出来找乐子的体面些的职员，还有相当一批卡其军装。也有一两位喝得不大清醒的先生。

主席一开场就砸了锅。他说，我们今晚聚在这里，抗议战争延续，并成立新的不列颠工兵代表会的一个支部。他拿一手漂亮的混搭比喻告诉大家，缰绳必须抓到我们自己手里，因为主持战争的那伙人各揣一把私斧，正踩着工人的血走向寡头统治。他又说，我们同德国的过节，还不及同本国资本家的一半深。他期待着这么一天：英国大兵从战壕里跳出去，向他们的德国同志伸出友谊之手。

“俺可不！”一个庄重的嗓音说。“俺可不想肚子上挨枪子儿。”——一阵哄笑，一阵嘘。

汤姆斯跟着上台，砸得更彻底。他打定主意要——照他的说法——用民主自己的语言对民主讲话，于是他大声说了好几遍“见鬼”，响亮，却没有底气。

===Original===

Presently he slipped into the manner of the lecturer, and the audience grew restless. “I propose to ask myself a question﻿—” he began, and from the back of the hall came﻿—“And a damned sully answer ye’ll get.” After that there was no more Tombs.
I followed with extreme nervousness, and to my surprise got a fair hearing. I felt as mean as a mangy dog on a cold morning, for I hated to talk rot before soldiers﻿—especially before a couple of Royal Scots Fusiliers, who, for all I knew, might have been in my own brigade. My line was the plain, practical, patriotic man, just come from the colonies, who looked at things with fresh eyes, and called for a new deal. I was very moderate, but to justify my appearance there I had to put in a wild patch or two, and I got these by impassioned attacks on the Ministry of Munitions. I mixed up a little mild praise of the Germans, whom I said I had known all over the world for decent fellows. I received little applause, but no marked dissent, and sat down with deep thankfulness.
The next speaker put the lid on it. I believe he was a noted agitator, who had already been deported. Towards him there was no lukewarmness, for one half of the audience cheered wildly when he rose, and the other half hissed and groaned. He began with whirlwind abuse of the idle rich, then of the middle-classes (he called them the “rich man’s flunkeys”), and finally of the Government. All that was fairly well received, for it is the fashion of the Briton to run down every Government and yet to be very averse to parting from it. Then he started on the soldiers and slanged the officers (“gentry pups” was his name for them), and the generals, whom he accused of idleness, of cowardice, and of habitual intoxication. He told us that our own kith and kin were sacrificed in every battle by leaders who had not the guts to share their risks. The Scots Fusiliers looked perturbed, as if they were in doubt of his meaning. Then he put it more plainly. “Will any soldier deny that the men are the barrage to keep the officers’ skins whole?”
“That’s a bloody lee,” said one of the Fusilier Jocks.
The man took no notice of the interruption, being carried away by the torrent of his own rhetoric, but he had not allowed for the persistence of the interrupter. The jock got slowly to his feet, and announced that he wanted satisfaction.

===Chinese===

不久他就滑回了讲师的腔调，听众坐不住了。“我打算向自己提一个问题——”他开了头；会堂后部应声飞来一句——“那你准得到一个见鬼的蠢答案。”此后就再没有汤姆斯的戏了。

我怀着极度的心虚跟着上台，出乎意料，居然听得还算客气。我觉得自己贱得像大冷天一条癞皮狗，因为我最恨在当兵的面前胡说八道——尤其眼前就有两个苏格兰皇家燧发枪团的兵，说不定就是我自家旅里的。我走的是直截了当、讲究实际、爱国的平民路线：刚从殖民地来，眼睛新鲜，要求来一个新局面。我说得极有分寸，可为对得起今晚这个场面，又不得不掺进两段发疯的话——这我是拿对军需部的猛烈攻击弄来的。我又夹进一点对德国人不痛不痒的夸奖，说他们满世界都是体面人，这话我到处讲过。掌声没有多少，明显的嘘声也没有，我如释重负地坐下来。

下一个演讲人把棺材钉敲死了。我想他是位有名的鼓动家，早被驱逐过一回。对他，听众没有不冷不热的：他一站起来，一半人疯狂喝彩，另一半人嘶嘶地倒彩。他开头把闲散的阔人劈头盖脸骂一顿，再骂中产阶级（他管他们叫“阔人的跟班”），最后骂政府。这些都还受欢迎，因为骂政府是英国人的时兴，虽说真要他们离开政府，他们又万分不肯。随后他骂到军队，把军官（他称之为“贵崽子”）同将军们痛骂，骂将军们怠惰、怯懦、永远烂醉。他告诉我们，每一仗里，我们自己的骨肉至亲都被那些没胆子分担危险的官长牺牲掉了。那两个燧发枪兵神色不安，像拿不准他的意思。他于是说得更露骨了。“哪个当兵的敢否认：士兵就是弹幕，专给军官挡皮肉的？”

“那是他妈的血口喷人，”一个燧发枪团的苏格兰兵说。

那人没理会这声打岔，正被自己的雄辩洪流冲得昏了头；可他没算到打岔的人那股韧劲。那苏格兰兵慢慢站起来，宣布他要讨个说法。

===Original===

“If ye open your dirty gab to blagyird honest men, I’ll come up on the platform and wring your neck.”
At that there was a fine old row, some crying out “Order,” some “Fair play,” and some applauding. A Canadian at the back of the hall started a song, and there was an ugly press forward. The hall seemed to be moving up from the back, and already men were standing in all the passages and right to the edge of the platform. I did not like the look in the eyes of these newcomers, and among the crowd I saw several who were obviously plainclothes policemen.
The chairman whispered a word to the speaker, who continued when the noise had temporarily died down. He kept off the army and returned to the Government, and for a little sluiced out pure anarchism. But he got his foot in it again, for he pointed to the Sinn Feiners as examples of manly independence. At that, pandemonium broke loose, and he never had another look in. There were several fights going on in the hall between the public and courageous supporters of the orator.
Then Gresson advanced to the edge of the platform in a vain endeavour to retrieve the day. I must say he did it uncommonly well. He was clearly a practised speaker, and for a moment his appeal “Now, boys, let’s cool down a bit and talk sense,” had an effect. But the mischief had been done, and the crowd was surging round the lonely redoubt where we sat. Besides, I could see that for all his clever talk the meeting did not like the look of him. He was as mild as a turtle dove, but they wouldn’t stand for it. A missile hurtled past my nose, and I saw a rotten cabbage envelop the baldish head of the ex-deportee. Someone reached out a long arm and grabbed a chair, and with it took the legs from Gresson. Then the lights suddenly went out, and we retreated in good order by the platform door with a yelling crowd at our heels.
It was here that the plainclothes men came in handy. They held the door while the ex-deportee was smuggled out by some side entrance. That class of lad would soon cease to exist but for the protection of the law which he would abolish. The rest of us, having less to fear, were suffered to leak into Newmilns Street. I found myself next to Gresson, and took his arm. There was something hard in his coat pocket.
Unfortunately there was a big lamp at the point where we emerged, and there for our confusion were the Fusilier Jocks.

===Chinese===

“你再张开你那张脏嘴骂正经人，俺就上台拧断你的脖子。”

顿时掀起一场好乱子，有喊“秩序”的，有喊“公平”的，还有拍手叫好的。会堂后部一个加拿大人起了个头唱起来，人群凶狠地往前挤。整个会堂仿佛从后头往前涌，过道里已站满了人，一直挤到台口。我不喜欢这些新面孔眼里的神色；人群里我还看见几个分明是便衣警察。

主席对演讲人咬了个耳朵，等嘈杂暂歇，那人接着讲。他避开军队，回头骂政府，有一阵干脆放起纯粹的无政府主义来。可他又踩了雷：他把新芬党人举成男子汉气概、独立自主的模范。这一下炸了锅，他再没有翻身的机会。会堂里好几处动起手来，是听众同那位演说家的勇敢支持者们之间开的打。

于是格雷森走上台口，妄图挽回这一天。我得说，他干得出奇地漂亮。他分明是个老练的演说家；他那一声“好了，小伙子们，咱们冷静点，讲点道理”，一时真有了效果。可祸根已经种下，人群围拢来，涌向我们坐着的这座孤垒。再说，我看得出，任凭他口齿伶俐，听众不喜欢他那张脸。他温和得像斑鸠，可他们不买账。一发导弹擦着我鼻子飞过去，我看见一只烂白菜扣上了那位前被驱逐者半秃的脑袋。有人伸长胳膊抓过一把椅子，照着格雷森的腿就扫。随后灯突然灭了，我们秩序井然地从舞台门撤退，身后一群人嚇嚇直叫。

便衣这时候派上了用场。他们把住门，掩护前被驱逐者从什么边门溜了出去。要不是他巴不得废除的法律护着他，这种小子早绝种了。我们其余几个目标小些，被放过，渗进纽密尔恩斯街的夜色。我挨着格雷森，挽住他的胳膊。他外衣口袋里有个硬东西。

不巧，我们出来的路口正立着一盏大灯，灯下，为叫我们难堪，站着那两个燧发枪团的苏格兰兵。

===Original===

Both were strung to fighting pitch, and were determined to have someone’s blood. Of me they took no notice, but Gresson had spoken after their ire had been roused, and was marked out as a victim. With a howl of joy they rushed for him.
I felt his hand steal to his side-pocket. “Let that alone, you fool,” I growled in his ear.
“Sure, mister,” he said, and the next second we were in the thick of it.
It was like so many street fights I have seen﻿—an immense crowd which surged up around us, and yet left a clear ring. Gresson and I got against the wall on the sidewalk, and faced the furious soldiery. My intention was to do as little as possible, but the first minute convinced me that my companion had no idea how to use his fists, and I was mortally afraid that he would get busy with the gun in his pocket. It was that fear that brought me into the scrap. The jocks were sportsmen every bit of them, and only one advanced to the combat. He hit Gresson a clip on the jaw with his left, and but for the wall would have laid him out. I saw in the lamplight the vicious gleam in the American’s eye and the twitch of his hand to his pocket. That decided me to interfere and I got in front of him.
This brought the second jock into the fray. He was a broad, thickset fellow, of the adorable bandy-legged stocky type that I had seen go through the Railway Triangle at Arras as though it were blotting-paper. He had some notion of fighting, too, and gave me a rough time, for I had to keep edging the other fellow off Gresson.
“Go home, you fool,” I shouted. “Let this gentleman alone. I don’t want to hurt you.”
The only answer was a hook-hit which I just managed to guard, followed by a mighty drive with his right which I dodged so that he barked his knuckles on the wall. I heard a yell of rage, and observed that Gresson seemed to have kicked his assailant on the shin. I began to long for the police.
Then there was that swaying of the crowd which betokens the approach of the forces of law and order. But they were too late to prevent trouble. In self-defence I had to take my jock seriously, and got in my blow when he had overreached himself and lost his balance. I never hit anyone so unwillingly in my life. He went over like a poled ox, and measured his length on the causeway.
I found myself explaining things politely to the constables.

===Chinese===

两个都绷到了动手的火候，横下心要见血。我，他们不看在眼里；格雷森是在他们火头上开过口的，被点作了牺牲品。一声欢呼，他们朝他扑过来。

我觉出他的手悄悄摸向侧袋。“别动那个，你这不识好歹的，”我在他耳朵边低吼。

“是了，先生，”他说；下一秒，我们已经卷进了乱阵。

这同我见过的许多场街头斗殴一模一样——一大群人围着我们涌动，可中间让出一个空圈。格雷森同我退到人行道的墙根，面对狂怒的大兵。我的本意是能不动手就不动手；可头一分钟就叫我明白，我这位同伴压根不会使拳头，而我又怕得要死，怕他掏出口袋里那把枪。正是这个怕，把我拽进了战团。那两个苏格兰兵个个是堂堂正正的运动家，只有一个上来交手。他左手照着格雷森的下巴就是一记，要不是有墙挡着，早把他放倒了。灯光下我看见那美国人眼里凶光一闪，那只手朝口袋一抽。这一下我拿定了主意挤到他前面去。

这一下把第二个苏格兰兵也卷了进来。他是个宽肩膀的矮壮汉子，那副可爱的罗圈腿、敦实个儿的种——这种汉子我见过穿过阿拉斯的铁路三角地，跟踩吸墨纸似的。他也懂些拳脚，叫我吃足了苦头，因为我还得分神把另一个从格雷森身上别开。

“回家去吧，你这糊涂虫，”我喊。“放这位先生过去。我不想伤你。”

回答我的是一记勾拳——好容易我格开了——紧跟着他右手的重磅一击，我一闪，他一拳砸在墙上蹭破了指节。我听见一声怒吼，看见格雷森似乎朝他的对头小腿上踢了一脚。我开始盼警察来了。

随后人群起了那种摇晃——那是法律同秩序的部队临近的兆头。可他们来得太晚，架已经打起来了。出于自卫，我只好拿出真功夫对付我的苏格兰兵，趁他扑过了头失去平衡，一拳递了上去。我一生里从没这么不情愿地打过人。他像一头被放倒的牛直挺挺横过去，在石板路上量开了身长。

再跟着，我就客客气气地向巡警们解释起来。

===Original===

“These men objected to this gentleman’s speech at the meeting, and I had to interfere to protect him. No, no! I don’t want to charge anybody. It was all a misunderstanding.” I helped the stricken jock to rise and offered him ten bob for consolation.
He looked at me sullenly and spat on the ground. “Keep your dirty money,” he said. “I’ll be even with ye yet, my man﻿—you and that redheaded scab. I’ll mind the looks of ye the next time I see ye.”
Gresson was wiping the blood from his cheek with a silk handkerchief. “I guess I’m in your debt, Mr. Brand,” he said. “You may bet I won’t forget it.”
---
I returned to an anxious Amos. He heard my story in silence and his only comment was﻿—“Well done the Fusiliers!”
“It might have been worse, I’ll not deny,” he went on. “Ye’ve established some kind of a claim upon Gresson, which may come in handy﻿ ﻿… Speaking about Gresson, I’ve news for ye. He’s sailing on Friday as purser in the *Tobermory*. The *Tobermory*’s a boat that wanders every month up the West Highlands as far as Stornoway. I’ve arranged for ye to take a trip on that boat, Mr. Brand.”
I nodded. “How did you find out that?” I asked.
“It took me some finding,” he said dryly, “but I’ve ways and means. Now I’ll not trouble ye with advice, for ye ken your job as well as me. But I’m going north myself the morn to look after some of the Ross-shire wuds, and I’ll be in the way of getting telegrams at the Kyle. Ye’ll keep that in mind. Keep in mind, too, that I’m a great reader of the *Pilgrim’s Progress* and that I’ve a cousin of the name of Ochterlony.”

===Chinese===

“这两位不赞成这位先生在会上的演讲，我只好插手保护他。不，不！我不告任何人。都是一场误会。”我把打倒的苏格兰兵扶起来，掏出十先令给他压惊。

他阴沉地看看我，朝地上啐了一口。“留着你的脏钱吧，”他说。“这笔账我早晚同你算，伙计——同你，还有你那个红头发的工贼。下回再让我撞见，我认得你们的模样。”

格雷森正用真丝手帕擦脸上的血。“布兰德先生，我想我欠了你一份情，”他说。“你放心，我不会忘的。”

---

我回到住处，埃莫斯正着急。他一言不发听我讲完，只评了一句——“燧发枪团干得好！”

“兴许还可能更糟，我不赖账，”他接着说。“你在格雷森身上立下了几分人情，往后兴许用得着……说起格雷森，我有消息给你。他礼拜五上船，当‘托伯莫里’号的乘务长。‘托伯莫里’是一条每月在西边高地沿海转悠一趟的船，一直开到斯托诺韦。我已经给你订好了位子，布兰德先生，你搭那条船走一趟。”

我点点头。“这个你是怎么打听出来的？”我问。

“费了点事才打听出来，”他干巴巴地说，“可我有我的路子。如今我也不拿忠告烦你了，你的行当你同我一样在行。只是明儿我自己要北上，照看罗斯郡的几处林子，凯尔那边有电报我能收到。这话你记下。还有一句也记下：我是《天路历程》的大读者，我有个表亲，姓奥克特洛尼。”

===Original===

To That Most Gallant Company

the Officers and Men of the

South African Infantry Brigade

on the Western Front

===Chinese===

献给那支最英勇的部队

南非步兵旅的官兵们

于西线

## XXI How an Exile Returned to His Own People / 二十一 流亡者如何归返本族
===Original===

Next morning I found the Army Commander on his way to Doullens.
“Take over the division?” he said. “Certainly. I’m afraid there isn’t much left of it. I’ll tell Carr to get through to the Corps Headquarters, when he can find them. You’ll have to nurse the remnants, for they can’t be pulled out yet﻿—not for a day or two. Bless me, Hannay, there are parts of our line which we’re holding with a man and a boy. You’ve got to stick it out till the French take over. We’re not hanging on by our eyelids﻿—it’s our eyelashes now.”
“What about positions to fall back on, sir?” I asked.
“We’re doing our best, but we haven’t enough men to prepare them.” He plucked open a map. “There we’re digging a line﻿—and there. If we can hold that bit for two days we shall have a fair line resting on the river. But we mayn’t have time.”
Then I told him about Blenkiron, whom of course he had heard of. “He was one of the biggest engineers in the States, and he’s got a nailing fine eye for country. He’ll make good somehow if you let him help in the job.”
“The very fellow,” he said, and he wrote an order. “Take this to Jacks and he’ll fix up a temporary commission. Your man can find a uniform somewhere in Amiens.”
After that I went to the detail camp and found that Ivery had duly arrived.
“The prisoner has given no trouble, sirr,” Hamilton reported. “But he’s a wee thing peevish. They’re saying that the Gairmans is gettin’ on fine, and I was tellin’ him that he should be proud of his ain folk. But he wasn’t verra weel pleased.”
Three days had wrought a transformation in Ivery. That face, once so cool and capable, was now sharpened like a hunted beast’s. His imagination was preying on him and I could picture its torture. He, who had been always at the top directing the machine, was now only a cog in it. He had never in his life been anything but powerful; now he was impotent. He was in a hard, unfamiliar world, in the grip of something which he feared and didn’t understand, in the charge of men who were in no way amenable to his persuasiveness. It was like a proud and bullying manager suddenly forced to labour in a squad of navvies, and worse, for there was the gnawing physical fear of what was coming.
He made an appeal to me.
“Do the English torture their prisoners?” he asked. “You have beaten me. I own it, and I plead for mercy. I will go on my knees if you like.

===Chinese===

第二天早晨，我在去杜朗的路上找到了集团军司令。
“接管那个师？”他说。“当然可以。只怕它也没剩下多少了。我让卡尔设法接通军部，等他找得到他们的话。你得看护好那些残余，眼下还撤不下来——一两天内不行。天哪，汉内（Hannay），咱们有些地段，是一个大人带一个娃娃在守。你们得撑到法国人来接防。咱们已经不是拿眼皮吊着了——是拿睫毛吊着了。”

“有可以退守的阵地么，长官？”我问。

“我们尽力在搞，可没有足够的人手去构筑。”他扯开一张地图。“这里我们在挖一条线——还有那里。那一小段要是能守两天，咱们就有一条依托河流的像样的防线了。可未必来得及。”

随后我跟他讲了布伦基伦（Blenkiron）的事，他当然听说过这个人。“他是美国数得着的大工程师之一，看地形的眼力毒得很。你要是让他参与这活儿，他怎么也干得出色。”

“正对路，”他说着写了一道命令。“拿这个给杰克，他会给他办一个临时委任状。你那位在亚眠总能找到一身军装。”

之后我去了补充兵营营地，发现艾维里（Ivery）果然押到了。

“这囚犯没添麻烦，长官，”汉密尔顿（Hamilton）报告。“就是有点儿躁。外头都传说德国佬打得顺手，我跟他讲，他该为自己人得意呢。他听了不大受用。”

三天工夫，艾维里换了一个人。那张脸，从前那样冷静、干练，如今磨尖了，像一头被围猎的野兽。想象在啃他，我描得出那刑罚。他，从来都在顶上操纵机器的人，如今只是机器里的一个齿。他生平从来只做过强者；如今他无能为力。他落在一个又硬又生的世界里，擑在一样他害怕又不理解的东西手里，交在一群怎么也听不进他劝说的人手上。像一个又骄又横的经理，忽然被罚下去同挖土的苦力一起做活，还不止——底下还垫着那啮咬人心的、对下场的肉体恐惧。

他向我告了饶。

“英国人折磨俘虏么？”他问。“你们打败了我。我认。我求你们发发慈悲。你们要我跪下也行。

===Original===

I am not afraid of death﻿—in my own way.”
“Few people are afraid of death﻿—in their own way.”
“Why do you degrade me? I am a gentleman.”
“Not as we define the thing,” I said.
His jaw dropped. “What are you going to do with me?” he quavered.
“You have been a soldier,” I said. “You are going to see a little fighting﻿—from the ranks. There will be no brutality, you will be armed if you want to defend yourself, you will have the same chance of survival as the men around you. You may have heard that your countrymen are doing well. It is even possible that they may win the battle. What was your forecast to me? Amiens in two days, Abbeville in three. Well, you are a little behind scheduled time, but still you are prospering. You told me that you were the chief architect of all this, and you are going to be given the chance of seeing it, perhaps of sharing in it﻿—from the other side. Does it not appeal to your sense of justice?”
He groaned and turned away. I had no more pity for him than I would have had for a black mamba that had killed my friend and was now caught to a cleft tree. Nor, oddly enough, had Wake. If we had shot Ivery outright at St. Anton, I am certain that Wake would have called us murderers. Now he was in complete agreement. His passionate hatred of war made him rejoice that a chief contriver of war should be made to share in its terrors.
“He tried to talk me over this morning,” he told me. “Claimed he was on my side and said the kind of thing I used to say last year. It made me rather ashamed of some of my past performances to hear that scoundrel imitating them﻿ ﻿… By the way, Hannay, what are you going to do with me?”
“You’re coming on my staff. You’re a stout fellow and I can’t do without you.”
“Remember I won’t fight.”
“You won’t be asked to. We’re trying to stem the tide which wants to roll to the sea. You know how the Boche behaves in occupied country, and Mary’s in Amiens.”
At that news he shut his lips.
“Still﻿—” he began.
“Still,” I said. “I don’t ask you to forfeit one of your blessed principles. You needn’t fire a shot. But I want a man to carry orders for me, for we haven’t a line any more, only a lot of blobs like quicksilver. I want a clever man for the job and a brave one, and I know that you’re not afraid.”
“No,” he said. “I don’t think I am﻿—much. Well. I’m content!”

===Chinese===

我不怕死——按我自己的那一种怕法。”

“谁也不怕死——按各自的那一种怕法。”

“你为什么羞辱我？我是绅士。”

“按我们对这个词的定义，不是，”我说。

他的下巴塌了。“你们要把我怎么办？”他颤声问。

“你当过兵，”我说。“你要去看一看打仗了——从列兵的位子看。不会有暴行，你若想自卫，会给你武器，你同你身边的人有一样的活命机会。你也许听说了，你的同胞打得不错。他们甚至可能打赢这一仗。你当初给我下的断语是什么来着？两天到亚眠，三天到阿布维尔。唔，你比预定时刻慢了一点，可你终究在得胜。你告诉过我，这一切的总设计师是你。现在给你机会亲眼看一看，说不定还亲身体验一下——从另一边看。这岂不正合你的正义感？”

他呻吟一声，扭过脸去。我对他没有半点怜悯，比对一条咬死了我朋友、如今夹在树杠上的黑曼巴蛇多不出一分。说来也怪，韦克（Wake）也没有。当初在圣安东，我们若当场毙了艾维里，韦克准会说我们是凶手。如今他完全赞成。他对战争的切齿痛恨，叫他庆幸一个战争的罪魁祸首得以亲身尝一尝战争的恐怖。

“他今天早上还想说动我，”他告诉我。“自称同我是一边儿的，说的正是我去年说惯的那一套。听那无赖模仿我那些话，叫我为自己过去的某些表演怪害臊的……对了，汉内，你打算把我怎么办？”

“上我的参谋班子。你是条汉子，我离不了你。”

“记着，我不打仗。”

“不会叫你打。咱们在拦那股要滚到海里去的潮水。你知道德国佬在占领区干些什么，而玛丽在亚眠。”

听到这消息，他闭紧了嘴唇。

“可是——”他开口。

“可是，”我说。“我不叫你牺牲你那些宝贝原则里的一条。你可以一枪不放。可我要一个人替我传令，因为咱们已经没有战线了，只剩一摊摊水银似的斑点。这差事我要个聪明人，也要个勇敢的人，而我知道你不怕。”

“不怕，”他说。“我想是——不大怕。好。我愿意！”

===Original===

I started Blenkiron off in a car for Corps Headquarters, and in the afternoon took the road myself. I knew every inch of the country﻿—the lift of the hill east of Amiens, the Roman highway that ran straight as an arrow to St. Quentin, the marshy lagoons of the Somme, and that broad strip of land wasted by battle between Dompierre and Peronne. I had come to Amiens through it in January, for I had been up to the line before I left for Paris, and then it had been a peaceful place, with peasants tilling their fields, and new buildings going up on the old battlefield, and carpenters busy at cottage roofs, and scarcely a transport wagon on the road to remind one of war. Now the main route was choked like the Albert road when the Somme battle first began﻿—troops going up and troops coming down, the latter in the last stage of weariness; a ceaseless traffic of ambulances one way and ammunition wagons the other; busy staff cars trying to worm a way through the mass; strings of gun horses, oddments of cavalry, and here and there blue French uniforms. All that I had seen before; but one thing was new to me. Little country carts with sad-faced women and mystified children in them and piles of household plenishing were creeping westward, or stood waiting at village doors. Beside these tramped old men and boys, mostly in their Sunday best as if they were going to church. I had never seen the sight before, for I had never seen the British Army falling back. The dam which held up the waters had broken and the dwellers in the valley were trying to save their pitiful little treasures. And over everything, horse and man, cart and wheelbarrow, road and tillage, lay the white March dust, the sky was blue as June, small birds were busy in the copses, and in the corners of abandoned gardens I had a glimpse of the first violets.
Presently as we topped a rise we came within full noise of the guns. That, too, was new to me, for it was no ordinary bombardment. There was a special quality in the sound, something ragged, straggling, intermittent, which I had never heard before. It was the sign of open warfare and a moving battle.
At Peronne, from which the newly returned inhabitants had a second time fled, the battle seemed to be at the doors. There I had news of my division. It was farther south towards St. Christ.

===Chinese===

我打发布伦基伦坐汽车去军部，下午自己也上了路。这一带我熟得每一寸都认得——亚眠东边那道隆起的高地，那条直得像箭一样通到圣康坦的罗马大道，索姆河的沼泽泻湖，还有东皮埃尔和佩罗讷之间那片叫战争荒废了的宽阔地带。一月里我是穿过它来亚眠的，因为去巴黎之前我上过前线，那时它还是个太平地方，庄稼人种着地，旧战场上新房子一栋栋立起来，木匠忙着修农舍的屋顶，路上几乎见不到一辆运输车叫人想起战争。如今大路堵死了，像索姆河大战刚开场时的阿尔贝大道——队伍往前开，队伍往回撤，撤下来的已经疲乏到最后一层；救护车一路不停往西，弹药车一路不停往东；忙碌的参谋车想在人流里钻出一条缝；一串串炮兵马，零零星星的骑兵，还有东一处西一处的法国蓝军装。这些我从前都见过；可有一样是新的。一辆辆乡下小车，车里坐着愁苦的女人和发懵的孩子、一堆堆家当，正朝西慢慢挪，或是停在村口等着。车边走着老人和半大孩子，多半穿着礼拜天的好衣裳，像要上教堂去。这景象我从前没见过，因为我从没见过英国军队撤退。拦住大水的堤坝破了，谷里的人家在抢救他们可怜的一点点家当。而一切之上——马和人，车和独轮车，道路和耕地——铺着三月的白尘，天蓝得像六月，小鸟在树丛里忙，废弃花园的角落里，我瞥见了头一批紫罗兰。

不久我们上了一道坡，进到了炮声的全程里。那声音对我也新鲜，因为那不是寻常的炮击。那声响里有一种特别的质，一种破破烂烂、断断续续、不成阵势的东西，我从前没听见过。那是运动战和流动战斗的记号。

在佩罗讷——刚回来的居民已经第二次出逃——战斗像已经到了门口。在那里我得到了我师的消息。它在南边，朝圣克里斯特去。

===Original===

We groped our way among bad roads to where its headquarters were believed to be, while the voice of the guns grew louder. They turned out to be those of another division, which was busy getting ready to cross the river. Then the dark fell, and while airplanes flew west into the sunset there was a redder sunset in the east, where the unceasing flashes of gunfire were pale against the angry glow of burning dumps. The sight of the bonnet-badge of a Scots Fusilier made me halt, and the man turned out to belong to my division. Half an hour later I was taking over from the much-relieved Masterton in the ruins of what had once been a sugar-beet factory.
There to my surprise I found Lefroy. The Boche had held him prisoner for precisely eight hours. During that time he had been so interested in watching the way the enemy handled an attack that he had forgotten the miseries of his position. He described with blasphemous admiration the endless wheel by which supplies and reserve troops move up, the silence, the smoothness, the perfect discipline. Then he had realized that he was a captive and unwounded, and had gone mad. Being a heavyweight boxer of note, he had sent his two guards spinning into a ditch, dodged the ensuing shots, and found shelter in the lee of a blazing ammunition dump where his pursuers hesitated to follow. Then he had spent an anxious hour trying to get through an outpost line, which he thought was Boche. Only by overhearing an exchange of oaths in the accents of Dundee did he realize that it was our own﻿ ﻿… It was a comfort to have Lefroy back, for he was both stouthearted and resourceful. But I found that I had a division only on paper. It was about the strength of a brigade, the brigades battalions, and the battalions companies.
---
This is not the place to write the story of the week that followed. I could not write it even if I wanted to, for I don’t know it. There was a plan somewhere, which you will find in the history books, but with me it was blank chaos. Orders came, but long before they arrived the situation had changed, and I could no more obey them than fly to the moon. Often I had lost touch with the divisions on both flanks. Intelligence arrived erratically out of the void, and for the most part we worried along without it. I heard we were under the French﻿—first it was said to be Foch, and then Fayolle, whom I had met in Paris.

===Chinese===

我们在坏路上摸索着往据信是师部的地方走，炮声越来越响。到头来那是另一个师的炮，正忙着准备过河。随后天黑了，飞机朝西飞进落日时，东边有一片更红的落日——那里炮火不灭的闪光，在燃烧的物资堆的怒火前发着白。一个苏格兰燧发枪兵的帽徽叫我停了车，那人果然是我师的。半小时后，我在一座曾经是甜菜制糖厂废墟里，从如释重负的马斯特顿（Masterton）手里接了防。

在那儿我意外地发现了勒弗罗伊（Lefroy）。德国佬把他俘虏了整整八个钟头。那八个钟头里，他看敌人怎么组织进攻看得入了迷，忘了自己处境的苦。他带着渎神的赞叹，给我描述补给和后备队上前的那道没有头尾的传送轮，那安静，那顺滑，那严整的纪律。随后他想起自己是俘虏、又没受伤，就疯了。他是名气不小的重量级拳手，两拳把两个看守抡进沟里，躲开随后的一阵枪，藏到一座烧着的弹药堆背风处，追兵迟疑着不敢跟。接着他提心吊胆花了半个钟头想穿过一道哨兵线，还当是德国佬的。直到偷听见一顿邓迪口音的对骂，才明白那是咱们自己的……勒弗罗伊回来是一大安慰，他又有胆子又有主意。可我发现，我的师只存在于纸面上。它大约一个旅的实力，旅等于营，营等于连。
---
这里不是写随后那一星期战史的地方。想写也写不了，因为我不知道。某处有一个方略，史书里能找到，可对我而言只有一片空白的混沌。命令来是来了，可等它到，态势早变了，我照办还不如飞上月球。常常两翼的师都失去了联络。情报从虚空里断断续续地掉下来，多半时候我们没有它也照样苦撚。听说我们归了法国人管——起先说是福煦，后来说法约勒，我在巴黎见过他。

===Original===

But the higher command seemed a million miles away, and we were left to use our mother wits. My problem was to give ground as slowly as possible and at the same time not to delay too long, for retreat we must, with the Boche sending in brand-new divisions each morning. It was a kind of war worlds distant from the old trench battles, and since I had been taught no other I had to invent rules as I went along. Looking back, it seems a miracle that any of us came out of it. Only the grace of God and the uncommon toughness of the British soldier bluffed the Hun and prevented him pouring through the breach to Abbeville and the sea. We were no better than a mosquito curtain stuck in a doorway to stop the advance of an angry bull.
The Army Commander was right; we were hanging on with our eyelashes. We must have been easily the weakest part of the whole front, for we were holding a line which was never less than two miles and was often, as I judged, nearer five, and there was nothing in reserve to us except some oddments of cavalry who chased about the whole battlefield under vague orders. Mercifully for us the Boche blundered. Perhaps he did not know our condition, for our airmen were magnificent and you never saw a Boche plane over our line by day, though they bombed us merrily by night. If he had called our bluff we should have been done, but he put his main strength to the north and the south of us. North he pressed hard on the Third Army, but he got well hammered by the Guards north of Bapaume and he could make no headway at Arras. South he drove at the Paris railway and down the Oise valley, but there Petain’s reserves had arrived, and the French made a noble stand.
Not that he didn’t fight hard in the centre where we were, but he hadn’t his best troops, and after we got west of the bend of the Somme he was outrunning his heavy guns. Still, it was a desperate enough business, for our flanks were all the time falling back, and we had to conform to movements we could only guess at. After all, we were on the direct route to Amiens, and it was up to us to yield slowly so as to give Haig and Petain time to get up supports. I was a miser about every yard of ground, for every yard and every minute were precious. We alone stood between the enemy and the city, and in the city was Mary.
If you ask me about our plans I can’t tell you. I had a new one every hour.

===Chinese===

可统帅部像在一百万英里之外，我们只有各显娘胎里带来的机灵。我的难题是：退让要退得尽量慢，又不能拖得太久，因为退是必须退的，德国佬每天清早都投入崭新的师。这是一种同旧式堑壕战隔着一个世界的战争，而我没学过别的，只好边打边发明规矩。回头看，我们任何一个能活着出来都像奇迹。全靠上帝的恩典和英国士兵那异乎寻常的坚韧，才嗅住了德国佬，没叫他顺着突破口灌到阿布维尔和海边。我们不过是一幅挂在门口挡怒牛的蚊帐。

集团军司令说得对，我们是拿睫毛吊着。咱们准是整条战线上最弱的一段，因为我们守的一条线从来不少于两英里，我估计常常接近五英里，预备队呢，除了几支奉着含糊命令满战场乱跑的零散骑兵，什么也没有。算我们走运，德国佬看走了眼。也许他不知道我们的底，因为我们的飞行员太出色，白天你在我们线上从不见德国飞机，虽然他们夜里炸得起劲。他要是揭穿咱们的空城计，我们就完了；可他把主力摆在了我们的北面和南面。北面他猛压第三集团军，却在巴波姆以北叫近卫军狠狠捶了一顿，在阿拉斯寸步未进。南面他扑巴黎铁路、顺瓦兹河谷而下，可贝当的预备队到了，法国人打了一场堂堂的阻击战。

倒不是说他在我们中间这一段打得不起劲，只是他没摆上最好的兵，而且等我们退过索姆河的河湾，他的重炮已叫他跑过了头。话虽如此，这仍旧是够凶险的事，因为我们两翼一直在退，我们只好去凑那只能猜个大概的运动。说到底，我们正在通亚眠的直道上，我们的本分就是慢慢让，好给黑格和贝当争取调上援兵的时间。每一码地我都吝啬得像个守财奴，因为每一码、每一分钟都是宝。我们一个师挡在敌人和那座城中间，而城里有着玛丽。

你若问我们的计划，我说不上。我每个钟头都有一个新的。

===Original===

I got instructions from the Corps, but, as I have said, they were usually out of date before they arrived, and most of my tactics I had to invent myself. I had a plain task, and to fulfil it I had to use what methods the Almighty allowed me. I hardly slept, I ate little, I was on the move day and night, but I never felt so strong in my life. It seemed as if I couldn’t tire, and, oddly enough, I was happy. If a man’s whole being is focused on one aim, he has no time to worry﻿ ﻿… I remember we were all very gentle and soft-spoken those days. Lefroy, whose tongue was famous for its edge, now cooed like a dove. The troops were on their uppers, but as steady as rocks. We were against the end of the world, and that stiffens a man﻿ ﻿…
Day after day saw the same performance. I held my wavering front with an outpost line which delayed each new attack till I could take its bearings. I had special companies for counterattack at selected points, when I wanted time to retire the rest of the division. I think we must have fought more than a dozen of such little battles. We lost men all the time, but the enemy made no big scoop, though he was always on the edge of one. Looking back, it seems like a succession of miracles. Often I was in one end of a village when the Boche was in the other. Our batteries were always on the move, and the work of the gunners was past praising. Sometimes we faced east, sometimes north, and once at a most critical moment due south, for our front waved and blew like a flag at a masthead﻿ ﻿… Thank God, the enemy was getting away from his big engine, and his ordinary troops were fagged and poor in quality. It was when his fresh shock battalions came on that I held my breath﻿ ﻿… He had a heathenish amount of machine-guns and he used them beautifully. Oh, I take my hat off to the Boche performance. He was doing what we had tried to do at the Somme and the Aisne and Arras and Ypres, and he was more or less succeeding. And the reason was that he was going bald-headed for victory.
The men, as I have said, were wonderfully steady and patient under the fiercest trial that soldiers can endure. I had all kinds in the division﻿—old army, new army, Territorials﻿—and you couldn’t pick and choose between them. They fought like Trojans, and, dirty, weary, and hungry, found still some salt of humour in their sufferings.

===Chinese===

军部给我指示，可我说过，指示到了通常已经过时，战术大半得我自己发明。我的任务明明白白，为完成它，上天容我用什么法子我就用什么法子。我几乎不睡，吃得少，日夜在路上跑，可我这辈子从没这么结实过。仿佛不会累似的，说来也怪，我还很快活。一个人全副生命都钉在一个目标上，就没有工夫发愁……我记得那些日子我们大家都很温和，说话都轻。勒弗罗伊那张嘴出了名的刻毒，如今鸽子似的咕咕。部队的兵鞋子都磨穿了底，可稳得像岩石。我们在对抗世界的末日，那会叫一个人硬起来……

一天又一天，都是同一套戏码。我拿一条警戒线撑住我那摇晃的正面，每回新进攻都先被它拖住，拖到我摸清来路。我备着专门的反冲击连队，用在选定的点上，好为我赢得时间把师的其余部分撤下去。这样的小仗我们少说打了十来场。人一直在损失，可敌人从没捞着一票大的，虽然总在那一票的边上晃。回头看，像一连串的奇迹。常常我在这头村子的一端，德国佬在另一端。我们的炮兵连一直在挪，炮兵的功劳夸都夸不完。有时我们朝东，有时朝北，有一回在最吃紧的关头朝正南，因为我们的正面像桅杆顶上的旗，又摆又飘……谢天谢地，敌人离他的大机器越来越远，他的普通部队乏了，质量也差。等他崭新的突击营上来的那些时刻，我才屏住气……他的机枪多得异教徒似的，用得又漂亮。哦，我对德国佬的这一手脱帽。他干的是我们在索姆、埃纳、阿拉斯、伊普尔想干而没干成的事，而且干得差不离。原因就在于，他是豁出一切扑向胜利的。

士兵呢，我说过，在士兵受得着的顶狠的磨难底下，稳得出奇，忍得出奇。我师里什么样的兵都有——老军、新军、地方军——你挑不出彼此。他们打得像特洛伊人，又脏、又乏、又饿，还在苦难里嚼出一点幽默的盐味。

这是人性里那块压舱石的健全的证明。可我们中间有一个人不大健全。……

===Original===

It was a proof of the rock-bottom sanity of human nature. But we had one man with us who was hardly sane.﻿ ﻿…
In the hustle of those days I now and then caught sight of Ivery. I had to be everywhere at all hours, and often visited that remnant of Scots Fusiliers into which the subtlest brain in Europe had been drafted. He and his keepers were never on outpost duty or in any counterattack. They were part of the mass whose only business was to retire discreetly. This was child’s play to Hamilton, who had been out since Mons; and Amos, after taking a day to get used to it, wrapped himself in his grim philosophy and rather enjoyed it. You couldn’t surprise Amos any more than a Turk. But the man with them, whom they never left﻿—that was another matter.
“For the first wee bit,” Hamilton reported, “we thocht he was gaun daft. Every shell that came near he jumped like a young horse. And the gas! We had to tie on his mask for him, for his hands were fushionless. There was whiles when he wadna be hindered from standin’ up and talkin’ to hisself, though the bullets was spittin’. He was what ye call demoralized﻿ ﻿… Syne he got as though he didna hear or see onything. He did what we tell’t him, and when we let him be he sat down and grat. He’s aye greetin’﻿ ﻿… Queer thing, sirr, but the Gairmans canna hit him. I’m aye shakin’ bullets out o’ my claes, and I’ve got a hole in my shoulder, and Andra took a bash on his tin that wad hae felled onybody that hadna a heid like a stot. But, sirr, the prisoner taks no scaith. Our boys are feared of him. There was an Irishman says to me that he had the evil eye, and ye can see for yerself that he’s no canny.”
I saw that his skin had become like parchment and that his eyes were glassy. I don’t think he recognized me.
“Does he take his meals?” I asked.
“He doesna eat muckle. But he has an unco thirst. Ye canna keep him off the men’s water-bottles.”
He was learning very fast the meaning of that war he had so confidently played with. I believe I am a merciful man, but as I looked at him I felt no vestige of pity. He was dreeing the weird he had prepared for others. I thought of Scudder, of the thousand friends I had lost, of the great seas of blood and the mountains of sorrow this man and his like had made for the world.

===Chinese===

那些日子的忙乱里，我不时瞥见艾维里一眼。我不得不随时随地到处出现，常去看苏格兰燧发枪兵那点残余——全欧洲最精细的一颗脑子就编在里面。他和看守从不上警戒线，也不参加任何反冲击。他们是那大片人马里的一份，唯一的任务是体面地后撤。这对汉密尔顿是小孩子的把戏，他从蒙斯那会儿就上前线了；阿莫斯（Amos）呢，用一天习惯过来之后，往他那套阴沉沉的哲学里一裹，还挺享受。你没法叫阿莫斯吃惊，就像没法叫土耳其人吃惊。可是同他们在一处的、他们寸步不离的那个人——那是另一回事。

“起头一小会儿，”汉密尔顿报告，“我们当他要发疯了。每一发挨近的炮弹他都要跳，像匹小马。还有毒气！面罩得我们替他系，他两只手软得没有一点劲。有几次子弹咻咻响着，他还要站起来自言自语，拦都拦不住。他就是你们说的那个——崩溃了……后来他变得像什么都听不见、看不见了。我们叫他做什么他做什么，一不管他，他就坐下哭。他一直在哭……怪事，长官，德国佬就是打不中他。我身上天天能抖出子弹来，肩膀上一个窟窿，安德鲁的脑壳挨了一下，那一下搁谁头上谁都倒，偏他脑袋跟牲口似的铁。可这囚犯，长官，一根汗毛没伤着。弟兄们都怕他。有个爱尔兰人跟我说，他有邪眼，您自己也看得出来，他不干净。”

我看见他的皮肤变得像羊皮纸，眼睛玻璃一样。我想他不认得我了。

“他吃饭么？”我问。

“吃不了多少。可渴得邪乎。弟兄们的水壶，你怎么也拦不住他。”

他亲手摆弄过的那场战争，如今正在飞快地教他明白它的意思。我相信我是个仁慈的人，可我看着他，心里没有一丝怜悯。他在领受他为别人备下的命运。我想起斯卡德尔，想起我失去的上千个朋友，想起这个人和他的同类给世界造下的血的海、愁的山。

===Original===

Out of the corner of my eye I could see the long ridges above Combles and Longueval which the salt of the earth had fallen to win, and which were again under the hoof of the Boche. I thought of the distracted city behind us and what it meant to me, and the weak, the pitifully weak screen which was all its defence. I thought of the foul deeds which had made the German name to stink by land and sea, foulness of which he was the arch-begetter. And then I was amazed at our forbearance. He would go mad, and madness for him was more decent than sanity.
I had another man who wasn’t what you might call normal, and that was Wake. He was the opposite of shell-shocked, if you understand me. He had never been properly under fire before, but he didn’t give a straw for it. I had known the same thing with other men, and they generally ended by crumpling up, for it isn’t natural that five or six feet of human flesh shouldn’t be afraid of what can torture and destroy it. The natural thing is to be always a little scared, like me, but by an effort of the will and attention to work to contrive to forget it. But Wake apparently never gave it a thought. He wasn’t foolhardy, only indifferent. He used to go about with a smile on his face, a smile of contentment. Even the horrors﻿—and we had plenty of them﻿—didn’t affect him. His eyes, which used to be hot, had now a curious open innocence like Peter’s. I would have been happier if he had been a little rattled.
One night, after we had had a bad day of anxiety, I talked to him as we smoked in what had once been a French dugout. He was an extra right arm to me, and I told him so. “This must be a queer experience for you,” I said.
“Yes,” he replied, “it is very wonderful. I did not think a man could go through it and keep his reason. But I know many things I did not know before. I know that the soul can be reborn without leaving the body.”
I stared at him, and he went on without looking at me.
“You’re not a classical scholar, Hannay? There was a strange cult in the ancient world, the worship of Magna Mater﻿—the Great Mother. To enter into her mysteries the votary passed through a bath of blood﻿—I think I am passing through that bath. I think that like the initiate I shall be *renatus in aeternum*﻿—reborn into the eternal.”
I advised him to have a drink, for that talk frightened me.

===Chinese===

眼角里我望得见孔布勒和朗格瓦尔上头那些长长的山梁——大地上的盐为之倒下过性命才换来它们，如今又踩回德国佬蹄下。我想起我们身后那座六神无主的城市，它对我意味着什么，还有护卫它的那层可怜的、可怜的薄幕。我想起那些叫德国的名字在陆地和海上发臭的劣迹，那一切秽恶的头一个播种者就是他。随后我为我们的克制吃惊。他会疯的，而疯，在他身上比清醒更体面。

我还有一个人也不大正常，那就是韦克。他同弹震症正好相反，你明白我的意思。他从前没有正经挨过炮火，可他满不在乎。同样的事我在别人身上也见过，那些人多半末了垮掉，因为五六尺的人肉对能折磨它、毁灭它的东西不害怕，这不合天理。合天理的是永远有一点怕，像我，靠意志的力气和对活计的专心，把它忘掉。可韦克仿佛想也不想。他不是莽，只是无所谓。他走东走西，脸上总挂一个笑，一个满足的笑。连那些惨状——我们见得不少——也不动他。他那双从前火辣辣的眼睛，如今有一种古怪的、敞开的无辜，像彼得的。我倒宁愿他有点心慌。

一天夜里，熬过一个焦灼的坏日子，我们在一处从前的法军掩体里抽烟，我同他谈。他是我多出来的一条右臂，我也这么告诉了他。“这对你必是一段古怪的经历，”我说。

“是，”他答，“非常奇妙。我原想不出一个人能熬过它、还保得住理智。可我明白了许多从前不明白的事。我知道了灵魂可以不离肉身而重生。”

我瞪着他。他看也不看我，接着说。

“你不是古典学者，汉内？古代有一个奇怪的教派，崇拜大母神——玛格纳·玛特（Magna Mater）。要入她的秘仪，信徒要过一道血浴——我想我正在过那道血浴。我想，同入教的人一样，我要 renatus in aeternum——重生入永恒。”

我劝他喝口酒，那话叫我发毛。

===Original===

It looked as if he were becoming what the Scots call “fey.” Lefroy noticed the same thing and was always speaking about it. He was as brave as a bull himself, and with very much the same kind of courage; but Wake’s gallantry perturbed him. “I can’t make the chap out,” he told me. “He behaves as if his mind was too full of better things to give a damn for Boche guns. He doesn’t take foolish risks﻿—I don’t mean that, but he behaves as if risks didn’t signify. It’s positively eerie to see him making notes with a steady hand when shells are dropping like hailstones and we’re all thinking every minute’s our last. You’ve got to be careful with him, sir. He’s a long sight too valuable for us to spare.”
Lefroy was right about that, for I don’t know what I should have done without him. The worst part of our job was to keep touch with our flanks, and that was what I used Wake for. He covered country like a moss-trooper, sometimes on a rusty bicycle, oftener on foot, and you couldn’t tire him. I wonder what other divisions thought of the grimy private who was our chief means of communication. He knew nothing of military affairs before, but he got the hang of this rough-and-tumble fighting as if he had been born for it. He never fired a shot; he carried no arms; the only weapons he used were his brains. And they were the best conceivable. I never met a staff officer who was so quick at getting a point or at sizing up a situation. He had put his back into the business, and first-class talent is not common anywhere. One day a G.S.O.1 from a neighbouring division came to see me.
“Where on earth did you pick up that man Wake?” he asked.
“He’s a conscientious objector and a noncombatant,” I said.
“Then I wish to Heaven we had a few more conscientious objectors in this show. He’s the only fellow who seems to know anything about this blessed battle. My general’s sending you a chit about him.”
“No need,” I said, laughing. “I know his value. He’s an old friend of mine.”
I used Wake as my link with Corps Headquarters, and especially with Blenkiron. For about the sixth day of the show I was beginning to get rather desperate. This kind of thing couldn’t go on forever. We were miles back now, behind the old line of ’17, and, as we rested one flank on the river, the immediate situation was a little easier.

===Chinese===

他像是快要变成苏格兰人说的“凶兆缠身”了。勒弗罗伊也看出来了，老挂在嘴上。他自己勇得像头公牛，也是公牛那一类的勇；可韦克的胆气叫他不安。“我摸不透这家伙，”他对我说。“他那样子，好像心里装满了更要紧的事，德国佬的炮去他的。他不冒傻险——我不是那个意思，可他那样子，好像险不险根本不算一回事。炮弹下雹子似的落，我们个个想着这口气就是最后一口，他却稳稳地一只手做笔记，看着真叫人发毛。您得看着他点儿，长官。他金贵得很，咱们折不起。”

勒弗罗伊说得对，离了他我真不知道该怎么办。我们差事里最苦的一头，是同两翼保持联络，韦克就是干这个的。他跑起地形来像个边境骑盗，有时骑一辆锈自行车，更多时候靠腿，你怎么也累不坏他。我纳闷别的师怎么看待我们这位满身泥灰的大兵——我们的头号交通线。他从前对军事一窍不通，可这种乱拳打死老师傅的仗，他上手像天生就会。他一枪没放过；他不带武器；他唯一的兵器是脑子。而那颗脑子好得没话说。我从没见过哪个参谋军官领会上意、估量态势这么快。他把整个背脊都扑在这件事上了，而头等的天才在哪里都不多见。一天，邻师的一位一级参谋来看我。

“你到底从哪儿捡来的那个韦克？”他问。

“他是良心拒服兵役者，不上前线的，”我说。

“那上天保佑，但愿咱们这场戏里多几个良心拒服兵役的。全场子里只有他一个像是明白这场该死的大战是怎么回事。我们将军要给你写张便条夸他。”

“不必，”我笑着说。“我知道他的价值。他是我的老朋友。”

我拿韦克当我同军部、尤其同布伦基伦的联络。到这场戏大约第六天，我开始相当绝望了。这样的日子不能永远过下去。我们已经退出去许多英里，退过了1917年的旧线，一翼靠着河，眼下的态势轻松了一点。

===Original===

But I had lost a lot of men, and those that were left were blind with fatigue. The big bulges of the enemy to north and south had added to the length of the total front, and I found I had to fan out my thin ranks. The Boche was still pressing on, though his impetus was slacker. If he knew how little there was to stop him in my section he might make a push which would carry him to Amiens. Only the magnificent work of our airmen had prevented him getting that knowledge, but we couldn’t keep the secrecy up forever. Some day an enemy plane would get over, and it only needed the drive of a fresh storm-battalion or two to scatter us. I wanted a good prepared position, with sound trenches and decent wiring. Above all I wanted reserves﻿—reserves. The word was on my lips all day and it haunted my dreams. I was told that the French were to relieve us, but when﻿—when? My reports to Corps Headquarters were one long wail for more troops. I knew there was a position prepared behind us, but I needed men to hold it.
Wake brought in a message from Blenkiron. “We’re waiting for you, Dick,” he wrote, “and we’ve gotten quite a nice little home ready for you. This old man hasn’t hustled so hard since he struck copper in Montana in ’92. We’ve dug three lines of trenches and made a heap of pretty redoubts, and I guess they’re well laid out, for the Army staff has supervised them and they’re no slouches at this brand of engineering. You would have laughed to see the labour we employed. We had all breeds of Dago and Chinaman, and some of your own South African blacks, and they got so busy on the job they forgot about bedtime. I used to be reckoned a bit of a slave driver, but my special talents weren’t needed with this push. I’m going to put a lot of money into foreign missions henceforward.”
I wrote back: “Your trenches are no good without men. For God’s sake get something that can hold a rifle. My lot are done to the world.”
Then I left Lefroy with the division and went down on the back of an ambulance to see for myself. I found Blenkiron, some of the Army engineers, and a staff officer from Corps Headquarters, and I found Archie Roylance.
They had dug a mighty good line and wired it nobly. It ran from the river to the wood of La Bruyere on the little hill above the Ablain stream.

===Chinese===

可我折损了很多人，剩下的都乏得眼睛发直。敌人在南北两面的大鼓包，把整个战线拉长了，我发现自己得把单薄的队列摊得更开。德国佬还在往前压，虽然冲劲松了些。他若是知道我这一段拦他的东西有多少，一鼓作气就到亚眠了。全亏我们飞行员的漂亮活儿，才没让他得到那个知识，可保密保不了永远。哪天敌机一钻进来，只消一两个崭新的突击营一冲，我们就散了。我要一条像样的预备阵地，有结实的堑壕、过得去的铁丝网。顶要紧的是我要预备队——预备队。这词整日挂在我嘴上，夜里钻进我的梦。有人告诉我法国人要来接防，可是什么时候——什么时候？我给军部的报告是一篇长长的哭喊，喊的是再给点兵。我知道我们身后备有一条阵地，可我需要人去守它。

韦克拊来布伦基伦的信。“我们在等你，迪克，”他写道，“我们给你备好了一处挺不错的小家。这老头子打1892年在蒙大拿挖着铜矿以来，还没这么玩命赶过工。我们挖了三线堑壕，堆了一堆漂亮的小堡垒，布局我看很在行，是集团军的人督的工，这一路工程他们可不是外行。你准会笑我们雇的是什么劳工。各国的三等佬、中国人全有，还有你们南非的黑人兄弟，他们干得那么起劲，连睡觉都忘了。从前人家也算我半个奴隶监工，可这回我那点特别本事根本用不上。往后我要往国外传教事业上投一大笔钱。”

我回信：“你的堑壕没有兵就白搭。看上帝份上，弄点拿得动步枪的来。我的人已经废了。”

随后我把师交给勒弗罗伊，自己搭一辆救护车下去亲眼看一看。我找到了布伦基伦、几位集团军的工兵、军部的一位参谋，还找到了阿奇·罗伊伦斯（Archie Roylance）。

他们挖出了一条极好的防线，铁丝网架得漂亮。它从河边起，一直延到阿布兰小溪上方小山上的拉布吕耶尔树林。

===Original===

It was desperately long, but I saw at once it couldn’t well be shorter, for the division on the south of us had its hands full with the fringe of the big thrust against the French.
“It’s no good blinking the facts,” I told them. “I haven’t a thousand men, and what I have are at the end of their tether. If you put ’em in these trenches they’ll go to sleep on their feet. When can the French take over?”
I was told that it had been arranged for next morning, but that it had now been put off twenty-four hours. It was only a temporary measure, pending the arrival of British divisions from the north.
Archie looked grave. “The Boche is pushin’ up new troops in this sector. We got the news before I left squadron headquarters. It looks as if it would be a near thing, sir.”
“It won’t be a near thing. It’s an absolute black certainty. My fellows can’t carry on as they are another day. Great God, they’ve had a fortnight in hell! Find me more men or we buckle up at the next push.” My temper was coming very near its limits.
“We’ve raked the country with a small-tooth comb, sir,” said one of the staff officers. “And we’ve raised a scratch pack. Best part of two thousand. Good men, but most of them know nothing about infantry fighting. We’ve put them into platoons, and done our best to give them some kind of training. There’s one thing may cheer you. We’ve plenty of machine-guns. There’s a machine-gun school near by and we got all the men who were taking the course and all the plant.”
I don’t suppose there was ever such a force put into the field before. It was a wilder medley than Moussy’s camp-followers at First Ypres. There was every kind of detail in the shape of men returning from leave, representing most of the regiments in the army. There were the men from the machine-gun school. There were Corps troops﻿—sappers and A.S.C., and a handful of Corps cavalry. Above all, there was a batch of American engineers, fathered by Blenkiron. I inspected them where they were drilling and liked the look of them. “Forty-eight hours,” I said to myself. “With luck we may just pull it off.”
Then I borrowed a bicycle and went back to the division. But before I left I had a word with Archie. “This is one big game of bluff, and it’s you fellows alone that enable us to play it. Tell your people that everything depends on them.

===Chinese===

长得要命，可我一眼看出没法再短，因为我们南边的那个师，正被压向法国人的那股大突击的余波搅得自顾不暇。

“事实不能睁眼不看，”我对他们说。“我手下不足一千人，个个都到了强弩之末。把他们放进这些堑壕，他们站着就能睡着。法国人什么时候接防？”

我得到的答复是原定明天早晨，如今推了二十四个钟头。那不过是权宜之计，等北边的英国师调到。

阿奇面色凝重。“德国佬正往这一段往前调新部队。我离开中队部之前得了信。看样子会是一场险仗，长官。”

“不是险仗。是板上钉钉的黑仗。我的人这副样子再撑一天也不行了。老天爷，他们在地狱里已经待了半个月了！给我找兵来，不然下一次进攻我们就垮。”我的火气快到头了。

“我们拿细齿梳把这一带梳了一遍，长官，”一位参谋说。“凑起了一支杂牌队。差不多两千。都是好汉，可多半不懂步兵的仗。我们把他们编成排，尽力给了点训练。有一件事可以叫您宽心。机枪管够。附近有一所机枪学校，我们把学员全要来了，机器也全搬来了。”

我想战场上从前没摆过这样一支队伍。比第一次伊普尔穆西的随营杂役还要杂。有各式各样的掉队兵——休假归队的，几乎哪个团的人都有。有机枪学校的人。有军直属队——工兵、陆军勤务队，还有一小撮军属骑兵。顶要紧的，是布伦基伦领来的一批美国工兵。我到他们操练的地方检阅了一下，看样子喜欢。“四十八个钟头，”我对自己说。“运气好，兴许真能撑过去。”

随后我借了辆自行车回师里去。临走前我跟阿奇交代了几句。“这是一场天大的唬人把戏，全靠你们这些家伙，我们才玩得下去。告诉你们那边，一切都押在他们身上。

===Original===

They mustn’t stint the planes in this sector, for if the Boche once suspicions how little he’s got before him the game’s up.
