“我需要好几个交叉方位才敢拿稳；前天夜里，方位齐了。我这趟比格斯威克，不虚此行。”

“我不知道那两个信号是什么意思，”我说，“可我知道落在哪儿。一个是您讲奥地利社会党人那一段，艾弗里接了您的话。一个是晚饭后他引《维也纳日报》。”

“你不傻，迪克，”他说，慢吞吞地笑了。“头一枪就中的。你认识我，能跟上那几句话里的思路。艾弗里同我没那么熟，脑子里又塞满了正是那一类论调，看不出蹊跷。那几条新闻是灌给格雷森、好叫他传递的。他果然传了——传给了艾弗里。我的链条齐了。”

“可那些都是寻常事，他自己也猜得着。”

“不寻常。那是政治新闻里最尖的几口，各家疯子都伸手够过。”

“再说他引的是德国报纸。也许报纸比您想的来得早。”

“又错了。那段文字根本没在《维也纳日报》上登过。是我们伪造了一小片撕破的报纸——手艺漂亮极了——故意落到那位半个书生的格雷森手里。他传了下去。前天夜里艾弗里拿给我看。《维也纳日报》的版面上从没沾染过那样的东西。不，那是终审级别的铁证……好了，迪克，该你去追格雷森了。”

“好，”我说。“又能动弹了，我高兴。不活动，人都长膘了。我猜您是要我拿住格雷森什么黑勾当，把他同艾弗里舒舒服服一齐关起来。”

“一点也不是，”他说得又慢又清楚。“你的指令，一个字都要听仔细。我疼这两个宝贝，疼得像自己的白头儿子。

===Original===

I wouldn’t for the world interfere with their comfort and liberty. I want them to go on corresponding with their friends. I want to give them every facility.”
He burst out laughing at my mystified face.
“See here, Dick. How do we want to treat the Boche? Why, to fill him up with all the cunningest lies and get him to act on them. Now here is Moxon Ivery, who has always given them good information. They trust him absolutely, and we would be fools to spoil their confidence. Only, if we can find out Moxon’s methods, we can arrange to use them ourselves and send noos in his name which isn’t quite so genooine. Every word he dispatches goes straight to the Grand High Secret General Staff, and old Hindenburg and Ludendorff put towels round their heads and cipher it out. We want to encourage them to go on doing it. We’ll arrange to send true stuff that don’t matter, so as they’ll continue to trust him, and a few selected falsehoods that’ll matter like hell. It’s a game you can’t play forever, but with luck I propose to play it long enough to confuse Fritz’s little plans.”
His face became serious and wore the air that our corps commander used to have at the big powwow before a push.
“I’m not going to give you instructions, for you’re man enough to make your own. But I can give you the general hang of the situation. You tell Ivery you’re going north to inquire into industrial disputes at first hand. That will seem to him natural and in line with your recent behaviour. He’ll tell his people that you’re a guileless colonial who feels disgruntled with Britain, and may come in useful. You’ll go to a man of mine in Glasgow, a red-hot agitator who chooses that way of doing his bit for his country. It’s a darned hard way and darned dangerous. Through him you’ll get in touch with Gresson, and you’ll keep alongside that bright citizen. Find out what he is doing, and get a chance of following him. He must never suspect you, and for that purpose you must be very near the edge of the law yourself. You go up there as an unabashed pacifist and you’ll live with folk that will turn your stomach. Maybe you’ll have to break some of these two-cent rules the British Government have invented to defend the realm, and it’s up to you not to get caught out﻿ ﻿… Remember, you’ll get no help from me.

===Chinese===

我千万不能搅了他们的安逸同自由。我要他们继续同朋友们通信。我要给他们一切便利。”

他见我一脸糊涂，放声大笑。

“听着，迪克。我们要怎么对付德国佬？往他肚子里灌最刁的谎话，叫他照着办。如今这位莫克森·艾弗里，向来得给他们好情报。他们信他信到底；咱们要是毁了这份信任，才是傻子。只要摸清莫克森的路数，我们就能自己借用，用他的名义送不太真的消息。他发出去的每一个字，都直达德国最高机密总参谋部；老兴登堡同鲁登道夫裹上毛巾，连夜破译。我们要鼓励他们接着干。我们安排着送点无足轻重的真货，好叫他们继续信他；再夹几条挑好的假货，一条顶一条的命。这游戏不能永远玩下去；可只要运气好，我打算玩得够久，叫弗里茨的小算盘乱上一乱。”

他的脸严肃起来，摆出我们军长在大攻势前的军事会议上那种神气。

“我不给你下条条框框的指令；你是能自己立章程的人。可大局的形势可以给你交个底。你对艾弗里说，你要北上，第一手调查工潮。在他看来这自然，同你近来的行径一路。他会告诉他们的人：你是个心里对英国不满的天真殖民地人，兴许有用。你到格拉斯哥去找我的人——一个红透的鼓动家，选了这条路子替国家尽本分。那是一条顶苦、顶险的路。通过他，你去搭上格雷森，然后一步不离那位精明的公民。看他干什么，找机会跟他的梢。他万万不能疑你；为这个，你自己得贴着法律的边走。你以一个不折不扣的和平主义者身份北上，同一群要叫你反胃的人同住。说不定你还得违犯几条英国政府为保卫国土想出来的小规则；你要担待的不是叫人捉住……记住，从我这里你得不到任何帮助。

===Original===

You’ve got to wise up about Gresson with the whole forces of the British State arrayed officially against you. I guess it’s a steep proposition, but you’re man enough to make good.”
As we shook hands, he added a last word. “You must take your own time, but it’s not a case for slouching. Every day that passes Ivery is sending out the worst kind of poison. The Boche is blowing up for a big campaign in the field, and a big effort to shake the nerve and confuse the judgement of our civilians. The whole earth’s war-weary, and we’ve about reached the danger-point. There’s pretty big stakes hang on you, Dick, for things are getting mighty delicate.”
---
I purchased a new novel in the shop and reached St. Pancras in time to have a cup of tea at the buffet. Ivery was at the bookstall buying an evening paper. When we got into the carriage he seized my *Punch* and kept laughing and calling my attention to the pictures. As I looked at him, I thought that he made a perfect picture of the citizen turned countryman, going back of an evening to his innocent home. Everything was right﻿—his neat tweeds, his light spats, his spotted neckcloth, and his Aquascutum.
Not that I dared look at him much. What I had learned made me eager to search his face, but I did not dare show any increased interest. I had always been a little offhand with him, for I had never much liked him, so I had to keep on the same manner. He was as merry as a grig, full of chat and very friendly and amusing. I remember he picked up the book I had brought off that morning to read in the train﻿—the second volume of Hazlitt’s *Essays*, the last of my English classics﻿—and discoursed so wisely about books that I wished I had spent more time in his company at Biggleswick.
“Hazlitt was the academic radical of his day,” he said. “He is always lashing himself into a state of theoretical fury over abuses he has never encountered in person. Men who are up against the real thing save their breath for action.”
That gave me my cue to tell him about my journey to the North. I said I had learned a lot in Biggleswick, but I wanted to see industrial life at close quarters. “Otherwise I might become like Hazlitt,” I said.
He was very interested and encouraging. “That’s the right way to set about it,” he said. “Where were you thinking of going?”

===Chinese===

你得在英帝国官方力量明面上同你作对的情况下，自己摸清格雷森。我看这差使够陡，可你是顶得住的人。”

握手的时候他又添一句。“火候你自己拿，可不许拖沓。多过一天，艾弗里就多送一天最毒的毒。德国佬正憋着战场上的大攻势，也憋着动摇我们平民神经、搅乱他们判断的大动作。整个地球都厌战了；我们也差不多摸到危险线了。迪克，你身上押着很大的注，事情正走到针尖上。”

---

我在书店里买了一本新小说，赶回圣潘克拉斯，还在小吃部喝了杯茶。艾弗里在书报摊上买晚报。上了车厢，他一把抓过我的《笨拙》，一路笑，一路拉我看漫画。我打量他，心想：一个化装成乡下绅士的公民，傍晚回他清白的家——这幅画画得真到家。样样都对：他齐整的花呢衣、浅色的鞋罩、点花的领巾，还有他那件阿库斯库特风雨衣。

倒不是我敢多看他。学了这一番，我急着想搜他那张脸，可不敢露出分外的兴趣。我对他向来有点随随便便，因为我从没喜欢过他，这个腔调还得端住。他快活得像只蟋蟀，话多、和气、有趣。我记得他拾起我那天早上买来车上读的书——赫兹里特《随笔》的第二卷，我的英国古典里最后一部——谈起书来那样有见地，叫我后悔在比格斯威克没多同他相处。

“赫兹里特是他那个年代的学院急进派，”他说。“他永远在为一桩他本人从没遭遇的流弊，把自己鞭挞进一种理论的狂怒。真正面对真东西的人，把力气省下来做事。”

这一下给我递了话头，我对他讲起我的北方之行。我说我在比格斯威克学了许多，可我还想贴身看看工业生活。“不然，我就要变成赫兹里特了，”我说。

他极感兴趣，极受鼓舞。“这才是着手的路子，”他说。“打算去哪儿？”

===Original===

I told him that I had half thought of Barrow, but decided to try Glasgow, since the Clyde seemed to be a warm corner.
“Right,” he said. “I only wish I was coming with you. It’ll take you a little while to understand the language. You’ll find a good deal of senseless bellicosity among the workmen, for they’ve got parrot-cries about the war as they used to have parrot-cries about their labour politics. But there’s plenty of shrewd brains and sound hearts too. You must write and tell me your conclusions.”
It was a warm evening and he dozed the last part of the journey. I looked at him and wished I could see into the mind at the back of that mask-like face. I counted for nothing in his eyes, not even enough for him to want to make me a tool, and I was setting out to try to make a tool of him. It sounded a forlorn enterprise. And all the while I was puzzled with a persistent sense of recognition. I told myself it was idiocy, for a man with a face like that must have hints of resemblance to a thousand people. But the idea kept nagging at me till we reached our destination.
As we emerged from the station into the golden evening I saw Mary Lamington again. She was with one of the Weekes girls, and after the Biggleswick fashion was bareheaded, so that the sun glinted from her hair. Ivery swept his hat off and made her a pretty speech, while I faced her steady eyes with the expressionlessness of the stage conspirator.
“A charming child,” he observed as we passed on. “Not without a touch of seriousness, too, which may yet be touched to noble issues.”
I considered, as I made my way to my final supper with the Jimsons, that the said child was likely to prove a sufficiently serious business for Mr. Moxon Ivery before the game was out.

===Chinese===

我告诉他，本想过去巴罗，后来定了格拉斯哥，因为克莱德看来是个热点。

“好，”他说。“我只恨不得同你一起去。那边的土话，你得些日子才懂。工人里有一大帮没头脑的好战，战争上他们学的是鹦鹉腔，从前劳工政治上他们学的也是鹦鹉腔。可精明的脑袋、健全的心肠也多得是。有了结论务必写信告诉我。”

那天晚上天气暖，最后一段路他打起盹来。我望着他，巴不得看进那张面具脸后面的脑子。我在他眼里一文不值，值不到他想把我当件工具使；而我正出发，想把他当工具使。听着像一场没指望的营生。而这一路，一种挥不去的似曾相识老缠着我。我对自己说这是发傻：长那样一张脸的人，同一千个人都有几分相像。可这个念头一路磨我，直到我们到站。

走出车站，进到金色的黄昏里，我又看见了玛丽·拉明顿。她同威克斯家的一位姑娘在一起，照比格斯威克的规矩光着头，阳光在她头发上闪。艾弗里扫帽致意，对她说了一句漂亮话；我则以舞台密谋家的没有表情，迎住她安定的眼睛。

“迷人的孩子，”我们走过时他评论道。“也不无几分庄重，来日或可点化为高尚的用途。”

往吉姆森家去吃我在这儿的最后一顿晚饭的路上，我心里想：游戏收场之前，这位“孩子”，多半要成为莫克森·艾弗里先生一桩够正经的买卖。

## VI The Skirts of the Coolin / 六 库林山麓
===Original===

Obviously I must keep away from the railway. If the police were after me in Morvern, that line would be warned, for it was a barrier I must cross if I were to go farther north. I observed from the map that it turned up the coast, and concluded that the place for me to make for was the shore south of that turn, where Heaven might send me some luck in the boat line. For I was pretty certain that every porter and stationmaster on that tinpot outfit was anxious to make better acquaintance with my humble self.
I lunched off the sandwiches the Broadburys had given me, and in the bright afternoon made my way down the hill, crossed at the foot of a small freshwater lochan, and pursued the issuing stream through midge-infested woods of hazels to its junction with the sea. It was rough going, but very pleasant, and I fell into the same mood of idle contentment that I had enjoyed the previous morning. I never met a soul. Sometimes a roe deer broke out of the covert, or an old blackcock startled me with his scolding. The place was bright with heather, still in its first bloom, and smelt better than the myrrh of Arabia. It was a blessed glen, and I was as happy as a king, till I began to feel the coming of hunger, and reflected that the Lord alone knew when I might get a meal. I had still some chocolate and biscuits, but I wanted something substantial.
The distance was greater than I thought, and it was already twilight when I reached the coast. The shore was open and desolate﻿—great banks of pebbles to which straggled alders and hazels from the hillside scrub. But as I marched northward and turned a little point of land I saw before me in a crook of the bay a smoking cottage. And, plodding along by the water’s edge, was the bent figure of a man, laden with nets and lobster pots. Also, beached on the shingle was a boat.
I quickened my pace and overtook the fisherman. He was an old man with a ragged grey beard, and his rig was seaman’s boots and a much-darned blue jersey. He was deaf, and did not hear me when I hailed him. When he caught sight of me he never stopped, though he very solemnly returned my good evening. I fell into step with him, and in his silent company reached the cottage.
He halted before the door and unslung his burdens. The place was a two-roomed building with a roof of thatch, and the walls all grown over with a yellow-flowered creeper.

===Chinese===

显然我得躲开铁路。警察若在莫尔文追我，那条线必有关照，因为它是我再往北必过的一道关。我从地图上看出它沿海岸拐上去，便断定我该奔拐点以南的海岸，上天兴许在那儿赏我一艘船。因为我相当拿得准，那条破线路上每一个挑夫、每一个站长，都在急着同敝人进一步亲热。

我拿布罗德伯里一家给的 sandwiches 当了午饭，午后明亮的天气里下了山，在一座小小的淡水湖脚下过了水，顺着出湖的溪，穿过蚊蚋成阵的榛树林，走到它入海的地方。路难走，可极愉快，我又落进头天早晨享受过的那种闲适的满足里。一路我没遇见一个人。间或一只狍子从树丛里蹿出，或一只老黑琴鸡骂骂咧咧地吓我一跳。满地石楠开着头茬花，亮得晃眼，香气比阿拉伯的没药还好。那是一条有福的山谷，我快活得像个王，直到饥镕上来，想起下一顿饭只有老天知道。我还有一点巧克力和饼干，可我要点顶事的。

距离比我想的远，到海边时已近黄昏。海岸开阔而荒凉——一片片卵石大滩，山坡灌丛里挣扎下来的桤木榛子散拉到滩上。可我朝北走、绕过一道小小的地角后，看见前面海湾一曲里一间冒烟的农舍。还有，沿水边深一脚浅一脚的，是一个压弯的人影，驮着网和龙虾笼。此外，卵石滩上扣着一条船。

我加快脚步追上渔夫。那是个老人，一部灰胡子拉碴，一身行头是水手靴和一件补了又补的蓝汗衫。他聋，我喊他听不见。看见我，他也不停步，只极郑重地回了我一声晚上好。我与他并排走，在他沉默的陪伴下到了农舍。

他在门前站住，卸下身上的担子。那是一所两间的屋子，草顶，墙上爬满开黄花的藤。

===Original===

When he had straightened his back, he looked seaward and at the sky, as if to prospect the weather. Then he turned on me his gentle, absorbed eyes. “It will haf been a fine day, sir. Wass you seeking the road to anywhere?”
“I was seeking a night’s lodging,” I said. “I’ve had a long tramp on the hills, and I’d be glad of a chance of not going farther.”
“We will haf no accommodation for a gentleman,” he said gravely.
“I can sleep on the floor, if you can give me a blanket and a bite of supper.”
“Indeed you will not,” and he smiled slowly. “But I will ask the wife. Mary, come here!”
An old woman appeared in answer to his call, a woman whose face was so old that she seemed like his mother. In highland places one sex ages quicker than the other.
“This gentleman would like to bide the night. I wass telling him that we had a poor small house, but he says he will not be minding it.”
She looked at me with the timid politeness that you find only in outland places.
“We can do our best, indeed, sir. The gentleman can have Colin’s bed in the loft, but he will haf to be doing with plain food. Supper is ready if you will come in now.”
I had a scrub with a piece of yellow soap at an adjacent pool in the burn and then entered a kitchen blue with peat-reek. We had a meal of boiled fish, oatcakes and skim-milk cheese, with cups of strong tea to wash it down. The old folk had the manners of princes. They pressed food on me, and asked me no questions, till for very decency’s sake I had to put up a story and give some account of myself.
I found they had a son in the Argylls and a young boy in the Navy. But they seemed disinclined to talk of them or of the war. By a mere accident I hit on the old man’s absorbing interest. He was passionate about the land. He had taken part in long-forgotten agitations, and had suffered eviction in some ancient landlords’ quarrel farther north. Presently he was pouring out to me all the woes of the crofter﻿—woes that seemed so antediluvian and forgotten that I listened as one would listen to an old song. “You who come from a new country will not haf heard of these things,” he kept telling me, but by that peat fire I made up for my defective education. He told me of evictions in the year. One somewhere in Sutherland, and of harsh doings in the Outer Isles. It was far more than a political grievance.

===Chinese===

他直起腰，先望望海、望望天，像在探天气。随后把那双温存、专注的眼睛转向我。“今天天气着实好，先生。您是找去哪儿的路么？”

“我找一夜宿头，”我说。“山上走了长路，能有个不再赶的机会就欢喜。”

“我们招待不了绅士，”他郑重地说。

“睡地板也行，只要您给一条毯子、一口晚饭。”

“那可不行，”他慢慢地笑了。“不过我问问老太婆。玛丽，过来！”

一个老妇人应声出现，那张脸老得像是他母亲。在高原地带，两性里总有一性老得快些。

“这位先生想住一夜。我刚告诉他我们家小屋破，他说他不计较。”

她带着那种只有荒僻地方才有的胆怯的客气望我。

“我们尽力招待，先生。先生可以睡阁楼上科林的床，只是得吃苦茶饭。晚饭好了，这就请进吧。”

我在溪边一口水潭里拿一块黄肥皁擦洗了一通，随后进了一间蓝着泥炭烟的厨房。我们吃了一顿煮鱼、燕麦饼和脱脂奶酪，灌下几杯酽茶。两位老人的做派是王侯的做派。他们一味劝吃，一句不问，直到碍于体面，我不得不编一套话，交代了自己的来历。

我得知他们有个儿子在阿盖尔营，还有个小子在海车。可他们不愿谈儿子，也不愿谈战争。我无意间撞着了老人的痴处。他一心扑在土地上。他参加过早已被忘记的鼓动，在北边更远的地方一场老地主纠纷里遭过驱逐。不多时，他把佃农的全部苦水倒给我听——那些苦水古老得、被遗忘得，叫我听着像听一支老歌。“您从新来的地方来，不会听过这些，”他一遍遍说，可在那堆泥炭火边，我把自己残缺的教育补上了。他给我讲那几年的驱逐，一处在家瑟兰，外岛里还有种种狠事。那远远不止是一桩政治的怨屈。

===Original===

It was the lament of the conservative for vanished days and manners. “Over in Skye wass the fine land for black cattle, and every man had his bit herd on the hillside. But the lairds said it wass better for sheep, and then they said it wass not good for sheep, so they put it under deer, and now there is no black cattle anywhere in Skye.” I tell you it was like sad music on the bagpipes hearing that old fellow. The war and all things modern meant nothing to him; he lived among the tragedies of his youth and his prime.
I’m a Tory myself and a bit of a land-reformer, so we agreed well enough. So well, that I got what I wanted without asking for it. I told him I was going to Skye, and he offered to take me over in his boat in the morning. “It will be no trouble. Indeed no. I will be going that way myself to the fishing.”
I told him that after the war, every acre of British soil would have to be used for the men that had earned the right to it. But that did not comfort him. He was not thinking about the land itself, but about the men who had been driven from it fifty years before. His desire was not for reform, but for restitution, and that was past the power of any Government. I went to bed in the loft in a sad, reflective mood, considering how in speeding our newfangled plough we must break down a multitude of molehills and how desirable and unreplaceable was the life of the moles.
In brisk, shining weather, with a wind from the southeast, we put off next morning. In front was a brown line of low hills, and behind them, a little to the north, that black toothcomb of mountain range which I had seen the day before from the Arisaig ridge.
“That is the Coolin,” said the fisherman. “It is a bad place where even the deer cannot go. But all the rest of Skye wass the fine land for black cattle.”
As we neared the coast, he pointed out many places. “Look there, Sir, in that glen. I haf seen six cot houses smoking there, and now there is not any left. There were three men of my own name had crofts on the machars beyond the point, and if you go there you will only find the marks of their bit gardens. You will know the place by the gean trees.”
When he put me ashore in a sandy bay between green ridges of bracken, he was still harping upon the past.

===Chinese===

那是一个守旧者对消逝了的岁月和风度的哀歌。“斯凯那边，从前是黑牛的好地方，家家山坡上有自己一小群牛。可老爷们说放羊更划算，后来又说羊也不好，就改养鹿，如今整个斯凯一头黑牛也没有了。”我告诉您，听那老头讲，像风笛上一段悲哀的曲子。战争和一切新事物对他毫无意义；他活在他青年和壮年的悲剧里。

我自己是个托利，也算半个土地改革者，所以我们谈得拢。搰得这样搰，我没开口要，就拿到了我要的东西。我说我要去斯凯，他提出明早开船送我过去。“不麻烦。真的不。我自己也要去那边打鱼。”

我对他说，战后英属的每一亩土，都得交给挣得权利的人。这安慰不了他。他想的不是地本身，是五十年前被赶走的人。他要的不是改革，是补还，而那不是任何政府的权力办得到的。我睡在阁楼上，心境悲凉而沉思，想着我们推新式犁，免不了要铲平无数鼹鼠丘，而鼹鼠的日子多么可贵、多么无可替代。

第二天早晨，天气爽朗闪光，刮着东南风，我们出发了。前面是一线褐色低山，山后偏北，就是我前一天从阿里赛格山脊望见的那一排黑色梳齿般的山脉。

“那就是库林，”渔夫说。“一个坏地方，连鹿都去不了。可斯凯其余的地方，从前都是黑牛的好地方。”

驶近海岸时，他指点各处。“看那儿，先生，那道谷。我见过六座小茅屋在那儿冒烟，如今一座不剩。同我同姓的三个人，在岬外那些海岸草场上有佃地，您去那儿，只找得见他们小菜园的印子。您认那地方，看野樱桃树。”

他把我送上一个绿蕨山脊间的沙湾时，还在旧事上打转。

===Original===

I got him to take a pound﻿—for the boat and not for the night’s hospitality, for he would have beaten me with an oar if I had suggested that. The last I saw of him, as I turned round at the top of the hill, he had still his sail down, and was gazing at the lands which had once been full of human dwellings and now were desolate.
I kept for a while along the ridge, with the Sound of Sleat on my right, and beyond it the high hills of Knoydart and Kintail. I was watching for the *Tobermory*, but saw no sign of her. A steamer put out from Mallaig, and there were several drifters crawling up the channel and once I saw the white ensign and a destroyer bustled northward, leaving a cloud of black smoke in her wake. Then, after consulting the map, I struck across country, still keeping the higher ground, but, except at odd minutes, being out of sight of the sea. I concluded that my business was to get to the latitude of Ranna without wasting time.
So soon as I changed my course I had the Coolin for company. Mountains have always been a craze of mine, and the blackness and mystery of those grim peaks went to my head. I forgot all about Fosse Manor and the Cotswolds. I forgot, too, what had been my chief feeling since I left Glasgow, a sense of the absurdity of my mission. It had all seemed too far-fetched and whimsical. I was running apparently no great personal risk, and I had always the unpleasing fear that Blenkiron might have been too clever and that the whole thing might be a mare’s nest. But that dark mountain mass changed my outlook. I began to have a queer instinct that that was the place, that something might be concealed there, something pretty damnable. I remember I sat on a top for half an hour raking the hills with my glasses. I made out ugly precipices, and glens which lost themselves in primeval blackness. When the sun caught them﻿—for it was a gleamy day﻿—it brought out no colours, only degrees of shade. No mountains I had ever seen﻿—not the Drakensberg or the red kopjes of Damaraland or the cold, white peaks around Erzerum﻿—ever looked so unearthly and uncanny.
Oddly enough, too, the sight of them set me thinking about Ivery. There seemed no link between a smooth, sedentary being, dwelling in villas and lecture-rooms, and that shaggy tangle of precipices. But I felt there was, for I had begun to realize the bigness of my opponent.

===Chinese===

我劝他收下一镑——算船钱，不算一夜招待的钱，因为若按后者说，他会拿桨打我。我在坡顶回头望他最后一眼时，他的帆还落着，人还凝望着那片曾经人烟稠密、如今荒凉的土地。

我沿山脊走了一程，右边是斯利特海峡，海峡外是克诺伊达特和金泰尔的高山。我瞭望着托伯莫里号，不见它的影子。一艘轮船从马莱格出港，几条漂网船在航道里爬，有一回我看见白军旗，一艘驱逐舰匆匆北上，尾后拖一缕黑烟。随后我看了地图，横插乡野，仍取高地，只是除了零星几分钟，再不见海。我断定我的正事，是不误时辰地赶到兰纳的纬度上。

一改道，库林就做了我的伙伴。山向来是我的痼疾，那群黑峰的黑和神秘上了我的头。我把福斯庄园和科茨沃尔德忘了个净。我也忘了离开格拉斯哥以来的主要心事——对使命荒诞感的那个念头。这一切原本显得太牵强、太异想天开。我表面上并没有冒什么大险，心里一直有一股不痛快，怕布伦基伦过分聪明，整个儿是一场空欢喜。可那一大团黑山改变了我的眼界。我开始有一种古怪的直觉：就是那地方，那里面可能藏着什么，藏着顶坏的东西。我记得我在一座山顶上坐了半个钟头，拿望远镜把群山梳了一遍。我看出一道道狰狞的绝壁，一条条没入太初黑暗的深谷。太阳照上去——那是个闪光的天——也照不出颜色，只照出深浅的暗。我见过的山——德拉肯斯堡也好，达马兰的红石丘也好，埃尔祖鲁姆四周冷白的雪峰也好——没有一座这样不像人间、这样鬼气。

说来也怪，一见它们，我就想起艾维里。一个住惯别墅和讲堂、白白净净坐着的动物，同那片乱糟糟的绝壁之间，仿佛没有一点联系。可我觉得有，因为我已经开始明白我的对手有多大。

===Original===

Blenkiron had said that he spun his web wide. That was intelligible enough among the half-baked youth of Biggleswick, and the pacifist societies, or even the toughs on the Clyde. I could fit him in all right to that picture. But that he should be playing his game among those mysterious black crags seemed to make him bigger and more desperate, altogether a different kind of proposition. I didn’t exactly dislike the idea, for my objection to my past weeks had been that I was out of my proper job, and this was more my line of country. I always felt that I was a better bandit than a detective. But a sort of awe mingled with my satisfaction. I began to feel about Ivery as I had felt about the three devils of the Black Stone who had hunted me before the war, and as I never felt about any other Hun. The men we fought at the Front and the men I had run across in the Greenmantle business, even old Stumm himself, had been human miscreants. They were formidable enough, but you could gauge and calculate their capacities. But this Ivery was like a poison gas that hung in the air and got into unexpected crannies and that you couldn’t fight in an upstanding way. Till then, in spite of Blenkiron’s solemnity, I had regarded him simply as a problem. But now he seemed an intimate and omnipresent enemy, intangible, too, as the horror of a haunted house. Up on that sunny hillside, with the sea winds round me and the whaups calling, I got a chill in my spine when I thought of him.
I am ashamed to confess it, but I was also horribly hungry. There was something about the war that made me ravenous, and the less chance of food the worse I felt. If I had been in London with twenty restaurants open to me, I should as likely as not have gone off my feed. That was the cussedness of my stomach. I had still a little chocolate left, and I ate the fisherman’s buttered scones for luncheon, but long before the evening my thoughts were dwelling on my empty interior.
I put up that night in a shepherd’s cottage miles from anywhere. The man was called Macmorran, and he had come from Galloway when sheep were booming. He was a very good imitation of a savage, a little fellow with red hair and red eyes, who might have been a Pict. He lived with a daughter who had once been in service in Glasgow, a fat young woman with a face entirely covered with freckles and a pout of habitual discontent.

===Chinese===

布伦基伦说过，他网撒得宽。在比格尔斯维克半生不熟的年轻人中间、在和平团体里、甚至在克莱德河上的恶汉里，这都好懂，把他嵌进那幅画毫无困难。可他竟在那些神秘的黑崖之间下他的棋，这就显得他更大、更狠，整个是另一种量级的题目。我并不讨厌这个念头，因为我对前几个星期的不满，正在于我不在正行上，而这才像我的路数。我一向觉得自己做强盗比做侦探强。可满意里掺着一层敬畏。我对艾维里开始有了战前那三个黑石魔鬼追猎我时的心情，对别的德国佬我从来没有过。我们在前线打的那些人、我在绿斗篷案子里碰见的那些人，连老施图姆本人，都是人类的恶徒。他们够厉害，可你能掂量、能算计他们的斤两。而这位艾维里，像一毒气悬在空气里，钻进你想不到的缝隙，你没法同它堂堂正正地打。到那时为止，尽管布伦基伦说得郑重，我只把他当作一道难题。可如今他成了一位贴身的、无处不在的敌人，又不可捉摸，像闹鬼屋子的恐怖。在那片阳光的山坡上，海风绕着我，凤头麦鸡叫着，一想起他我脊背就发凉。

说来惭愧，我还锇得厉害。战争里有一种东西叫我胃口大开，而越没得吃我越锇得慌。倘若在伦敦，二十家饭馆任我挑，多半我还倒胃口。我的胃就这么贱。我还剩一点巧克力，中午吃掉了渔夫的奶油烤饼，可离傍晚还早得很，我的心思已整个住在我那空空的肚腹里了。

那夜我宿在一家百里无人的牧羊人小屋。那人叫麦克莫兰，是羊行景气时从加洛韦来的。他是个野蛮人的上好仿品，矮个子，红头发红眼睛，做个皮克特人也合格。同他住的是个女儿，从前在格拉斯哥做过工，胖胖的年轻妇人，一脸雀斑，一张嘴惯常撅着不满足。

===Original===

No wonder, for that cottage was a pretty mean place. It was so thick with peat-reek that throat and eyes were always smarting. It was badly built, and must have leaked like a sieve in a storm. The father was a surly fellow, whose conversation was one long growl at the world, the high prices, the difficulty of moving his sheep, the meanness of his master, and the godforsaken character of Skye. “Here’s me no seen baker’s bread for a month, and no company but a wheen ignorant Hielanders that yatter Gawlic. I wish I was back in the Glenkens. And I’d gang the morn if I could get paid what I’m awed.”
However, he gave me supper﻿—a braxy ham and oatcake, and I bought the remnants off him for use next day. I did not trust his blankets, so I slept the night by the fire in the ruins of an armchair, and woke at dawn with a foul taste in my mouth. A dip in the burn refreshed me, and after a bowl of porridge I took the road again. For I was anxious to get to some hilltop that looked over to Ranna.
Before midday I was close under the eastern side of the Coolin, on a road which was more a rockery than a path. Presently I saw a big house ahead of me that looked like an inn, so I gave it a miss and struck the highway that led to it a little farther north. Then I bore off to the east, and was just beginning to climb a hill which I judged stood between me and the sea, when I heard wheels on the road and looked back.
It was a farmer’s gig carrying one man. I was about half a mile off, and something in the cut of his jib seemed familiar. I got my glasses on him and made out a short, stout figure clad in a mackintosh, with a woollen comforter round its throat. As I watched, it made a movement as if to rub its nose on its sleeve. That was the pet trick of one man I knew. Inconspicuously I slipped through the long heather so as to reach the road ahead of the gig. When I rose like a wraith from the wayside the horse started, but not the driver.
“So ye’re there,” said Amos’s voice. “I’ve news for ye. The *Tobermory* will be in Ranna by now. She passed Broadford two hours syne. When I saw her I yoked this beast and came up on the chance of foregathering with ye.”
“How on earth did you know I would be here?” I asked in some surprise.
“Oh, I saw the way your mind was workin’ from your telegram. And says I to mysel’﻿—that man Brand, says I, is not the chiel to be easy stoppit.

===Chinese===

难怪，那小屋实在寒碜。泥炭烟浓得呛嗓子刺眼。盖得又坏，一刮风准像筛子一样漏。父亲是个阴沉的家伙，话就是一长串对世界的咆哱：物价高，羊难赶，东家小气，斯凯这地方叫上帝撇下。“我一个月没见过面包房的面包了，作伴的只有一群叮当响盖尔话的 ignorant 高地佬。我恨不得回格伦肯斯去。只要能把欠我的结清，我明天就走。”

不过他还是给了我晚饭——腌羊腿肉和燕麦饼，我又把剩下的买了下来，备第二天用。我不信他的毯子，便在炉火边一把破圈椅里过了夜，天亮醒来满嘴馊味。溪里一泳叫我精神了，喝过一碗粥，重新上路。因为我急着赶到一座能望见兰纳的山头。

晌午前，我已贴近库林东麓，走在一条与其说是路、不如说是石堆的道上。不久看见前方一座大房子像家客栈，我绕开它，在稍北一点撞上通它去的公路。随后我折向东，刚要爬一座我断定隔在我同海之间的山，听见路上有车轮声，回头看。

是一辆农夫马车，坐着一个人。离我约半英里，那人行头的裁法眼熟。我用望远镜照他，看清一个矮壮的身子，裹一件雨衣，脖子上一条羊毛围巾。正看着，那身子做了个动作，像要拿袖子蹭鼻子。这是我认得的一个人宠爱的怪癖。我不动声色地隐进长石楠，抢到马车前头的路上。我从路旁像个鬼影一样站起来时，马惊了，赶车的没惊。

“你果然在这儿，”阿莫斯的声音说。“我有消息给你。托伯莫里号这会儿该到兰纳了。两个钟头前过的布罗德福。我一见它，就套上这牲口赶上来，指望同你会合。”

“你怎么知道我会在这儿？”我相当吃惊地问。

“哦，你那份电报，我就看出你心思的走法了。我自个儿说——那个布兰德，不是容易拦得住的主。”

===Original===

But I was feared ye might be a day late, so I came up the road to hold the fort. Man, I’m glad to see ye. Ye’re younger and soopler than me, and yon Gresson’s a stirrin’ lad.”
“There’s one thing you’ve got to do for me,” I said. “I can’t go into inns and shops, but I can’t do without food. I see from the map there’s a town about six miles on. Go there and buy me anything that’s tinned﻿—biscuits and tongue and sardines, and a couple of bottles of whisky if you can get them. This may be a long job, so buy plenty.”
“Whaur’ll I put them?” was his only question.
We fixed on a cache, a hundred yards from the highway in a place where two ridges of hill enclosed the view so that only a short bit of road was visible.
“I’ll get back to the Kyle,” he told me, “and a’body there kens Andra Amos, if ye should find a way of sendin’ a message or comin’ yourself. Oh, and I’ve got a word to ye from a lady that we ken of. She says, the sooner ye’re back in Vawnity Fair the better she’ll be pleased, always provided ye’ve got over the Hill Difficulty.”
A smile screwed up his old face and he waved his whip in farewell. I interpreted Mary’s message as an incitement to speed, but I could not make the pace. That was Gresson’s business. I think I was a little nettled, till I cheered myself by another interpretation. She might be anxious for my safety, she might want to see me again, anyhow the mere sending of the message showed I was not forgotten. I was in a pleasant muse as I breasted the hill, keeping discreetly in the cover of the many gullies. At the top I looked down on Ranna and the sea.
There lay the *Tobermory* busy unloading. It would be some time, no doubt, before Gresson could leave. There was no rowboat in the channel yet, and I might have to wait hours. I settled myself snugly between two rocks, where I could not be seen, and where I had a clear view of the sea and shore. But presently I found that I wanted some long heather to make a couch, and I emerged to get some. I had not raised my head for a second when I flopped down again. For I had a neighbour on the hilltop.
He was about two hundred yards off, just reaching the crest, and, unlike me, walking quite openly. His eyes were on Ranna, so he did not notice me, but from my cover I scanned every line of him. He looked an ordinary countryman, wearing badly cut, baggy knickerbockers of the kind that gillies affect.

===Chinese===

可我怕你晚一天，就顺路赶上来守着。伙计，见到你真高兴。你比我年轻、比我活络，而那个格雷森是个能折腾的小子。”

“有一件事你得替我办，”我说。“我进不得客栈和铺子，可没吃的又不行。我看地图上六英里外有个镇子。去那儿给我买点罐头——饼干、牛舌、沙丁鱼，能弄到再弄两瓶威士忌。这活儿兴许长，多买些。”

“搁哪儿？”他只问了这一句。

我们定下一处藏点，离公路一百码，两道山梁夹着视线，只望得见短短一截路。

“我回凯尔去，”他告诉我，“那边谁都认得安德拉·阿莫斯，你要捎信或自己来的话。哦，还有，咱们都认得的那位太太有一句话给你。她说，你越早回‘虚荣市’她越高兴，前提永远是你已经翻过了‘困难山’。”

一个笑把他那张老脸拧了起来，他扬起鞭子道了别。我把玛丽的话解读为催我快跑，可快慢不由我，由格雷森。我想我有一点恼，随后又拿另一个解读宽慰自己：她兴许是担心我的安全，兴许是想再见我，总之捎话这件事本身就说明我没被忘记。我揣着愉快的遐想上了山，小心地借一道道沟壑掩着。山顶上，我俯瞰兰纳和大海。

托伯莫里号正忙着卸货。格雷森无疑得过些时辰才脱得开身。航道里还没有小船，我兴许要等上几个钟头。我把自己安顿在两块岩石间一处看不见的窨下，海和岸都望得清楚。可不久我觉得该扯些长石楠铺个铺，便探身出去拔。头抬起来不到一秒，我就又扑倒了。因为山顶上来了个邻居。

他在两百码外，正上到顶，同我不一样，走得大大方方。眼睛望着兰纳，没留意我，我从掩体里把他浑身上下扫了个透。他看着是个寻常乡下人，穿一条随猎仆役爱的那种裁坏了的宽灯笼裤。

===Original===

He had a face like a Portuguese Jew, but I had seen that type before among people with Highland names; they might be Jews or not, but they could speak Gaelic. Presently he disappeared. He had followed my example and selected a hiding-place.
It was a clear, hot day, but very pleasant in that airy place. Good scents came up from the sea, the heather was warm and fragrant, bees droned about, and stray seagulls swept the ridge with their wings. I took a look now and then towards my neighbour, but he was deep in his hidey-hole. Most of the time I kept my glasses on Ranna, and watched the doings of the *Tobermory*. She was tied up at the jetty, but seemed in no hurry to unload. I watched the captain disembark and walk up to a house on the hillside. Then some idlers sauntered down towards her and stood talking and smoking close to her side. The captain returned and left again. A man with papers in his hand appeared, and a woman with what looked like a telegram. The mate went ashore in his best clothes. Then at last, after midday, Gresson appeared. He joined the captain at the piermaster’s office, and presently emerged on the other side of the jetty where some small boats were beached. A man from the *Tobermory* came in answer to his call, a boat was launched, and began to make its way into the channel. Gresson sat in the stern, placidly eating his luncheon.
I watched every detail of that crossing with some satisfaction that my forecast was turning out right. About halfway across, Gresson took the oars, but soon surrendered them to the *Tobermory* man, and lit a pipe. He got out a pair of binoculars and raked my hillside. I tried to see if my neighbour was making any signal, but all was quiet. Presently the boat was hid from me by the bulge of the hill, and I caught the sound of her scraping on the beach.
Gresson was not a hill-walker like my neighbour. It took him the best part of an hour to get to the top, and he reached it at a point not two yards from my hiding-place. I could hear by his labouring breath that he was very blown. He walked straight over the crest till he was out of sight of Ranna, and flung himself on the ground. He was now about fifty yards from me, and I made shift to lessen the distance. There was a grassy trench skirting the north side of the hill, deep and thickly overgrown with heather.

===Chinese===

一张脸像葡萄牙犹太人，不过这种长相我在高原姓氏的人里也见过；他们也许是犹太人，也许不是，可盖尔语说得地道。不久他不见了。他学了我的样，也挑了个藏身的地方。

那是个晴热天，可那高处风凉，很殹意。海上的好气味升上来，石楠温香，蜂嗡嗡地转，零星的海鸥扫着山脊飞。我不时朝我那位邻居望一眼，他深深窝在他的洞里。大半时间我把望远镜钉在兰纳，看托伯莫里号的动静。它靠在码头上，却不急着卸。我看船长下了船，走上山坡一座屋子。随后几个闲人踱到船边，站在船旁抽烟说话。船长回来了，又走了。一个手里拿纸的人出现，一个女人拿着一封像是电报的东西。大副穿上最好的衣裳上了岸。末了，过晌午，格雷森出现了。他在码头主任办公室同船长会合，不久从码头另一侧出来，那边滩上扣着几条小船。托伯莫里号上一个水手应声过来，一条小船下了水，往航道里划。格雷森坐在艉座上，安安稳稳吃他的午饭。

那一趟渡海我盯住了每一个细节，我的预报一件件应验，颇为得意。到中途，格雷森接过桨，不久又还给托伯莫里号的水手，点上了烟斗。他掏出一副双筒镜，朝我这面山坡梳。我想看看我那位邻居是不是在打信号，可四下里静悄悄。不久小船叫山包的鼓包挡住了，我听见它擦上沙滩的声音。

格雷森不是我邻居那样的走山人。他花了足足一个钟头才到顶，到顶的地方离我藏身处不到两码。听那喘息，我知道他透不过气了。他径直翻过顶，到望不见兰纳的地方，往地上一掼。这时他离我约五十码，我设法缩短距离。山的北边有一道草沟，深，石楠密盖。

===Original===

I wound my way along it till I was about twelve yards from him, where I stuck, owing to the trench dying away. When I peered out of the cover I saw that the other man had joined him and that the idiots were engaged in embracing each other.
I dared not move an inch nearer, and as they talked in a low voice I could hear nothing of what they said. Nothing except one phrase, which the strange man repeated twice, very emphatically. “Tomorrow night,” he said, and I noticed that his voice had not the Highland inflection which I looked for. Gresson nodded and glanced at his watch, and then the two began to move downhill towards the road I had travelled that morning.
I followed as best I could, using a shallow dry watercourse of which sheep had made a track, and which kept me well below the level of the moor. It took me down the hill, but some distance from the line the pair were taking, and I had to reconnoitre frequently to watch their movements. They were still a quarter of a mile or so from the road, when they stopped and stared, and I stared with them. On that lonely highway travellers were about as rare as roadmenders, and what caught their eye was a farmer’s gig driven by a thickset elderly man with a woollen comforter round his neck.
I had a bad moment, for I reckoned that if Gresson recognized Amos he might take fright. Perhaps the driver of the gig thought the same, for he appeared to be very drunk. He waved his whip, he jiggoted the reins, and he made an effort to sing. He looked towards the figures on the hillside, and cried out something. The gig narrowly missed the ditch, and then to my relief the horse bolted. Swaying like a ship in a gale, the whole outfit lurched out of sight round the corner of hill where lay my cache. If Amos could stop the beast and deliver the goods there, he had put up a masterly bit of buffoonery.
The two men laughed at the performance, and then they parted. Gresson retraced his steps up the hill. The other man﻿—I called him in my mind the Portuguese Jew﻿—started off at a great pace due west, across the road, and over a big patch of bog towards the northern butt of the Coolin. He had some errand, which Gresson knew about, and he was in a hurry to perform it. It was clearly my job to get after him.
I had a rotten afternoon. The fellow covered the moorland miles like a deer, and under the hot August sun I toiled on his trail.

===Chinese===

我顺着沟摸到他十二码左右的地方，沟到头了，我卡住了。我从掩体里窥看，见那人已同他汇上，两个傻瓜正在互相拥抱。

我一寸也不敢再近，他们说话声音低，我一个字听不见。只听见一个短语，那陌生人着重地说了两遍。“明晚，”他说，我注意到他的嗓音没有我预想的高地腔。格雷森点头看了看表，随后两人动身下坡，朝我早晨走过的那条路去。

我尽力跟着，借一条羊踩出来的浅干沟，它托我在荒原水平线以下。它带下山去，却同那两人走的线隔着一段，我得不时侦察他们的动静。他们离公路还有四分之一英里左右时，停住盯着看，我也跟着盯。那条荒凉的大路上，行人同修路工一样稀罕，引他们注目的是一辆农夫马车，赶车一个敦实的老头，脖子上围羊毛围巾。

我捏了一把汗，因为我掂量着，格雷森若认出阿莫斯，兴许要惊。马车上的那位想必也想到了，因为他显得醉得厉害。他扬鞭子，他抖缰绳，他还努力唱歌。他朝山坡上的人影望，喊了一句什么。马车擦着沟险险过去，随后叫我松一口气的是，马惊了。整套家伙像大风里的船一样摇晃着，转过藏我东西的那个山角，没影了。阿莫斯若能勒住牲口、把货卸在那儿，那这场滑稽演得就大师级了。

两人朝这场表演笑了，随后分手。格雷森原路上山。另一个——我心里叫他葡萄牙犹太人——迈开大步正西去了，过了公路，穿过一大片沼泽，奔库林山北头的岩堡去。他有一桩差事，格雷森知道内情的差事，他急着去办。显然，我的活儿是跟上他。

那个下午我吃了大苦。那家伙过荒原像头鹿，八月毒日头底下，我一路在他的踪迹上熬。

===Original===

I had to keep well behind, and as much as possible in cover, in case he looked back; and that meant that when he had passed over a ridge I had to double not to let him get too far ahead, and when we were in an open place I had to make wide circuits to keep hidden. We struck a road which crossed a low pass and skirted the flank of the mountains, and this we followed till we were on the western side and within sight of the sea. It was gorgeous weather, and out on the blue water I saw cool sails moving and little breezes ruffling the calm, while I was glowing like a furnace. Happily I was in fair training, and I needed it. The Portuguese Jew must have done a steady six miles an hour over abominable country.
About five o’clock we came to a point where I dared not follow. The road ran flat by the edge of the sea, so that several miles of it were visible. Moreover, the man had begun to look round every few minutes. He was getting near something and wanted to be sure that no one was in his neighbourhood. I left the road accordingly, and took to the hillside, which to my undoing was one long cascade of screes and tumbled rocks. I saw him drop over a rise which seemed to mark the rim of a little bay into which descended one of the big corries of the mountains. It must have been a good half-hour later before I, at my greater altitude and with far worse going, reached the same rim. I looked into the glen and my man had disappeared.
He could not have crossed it, for the place was wider than I had thought. A ring of black precipices came down to within half a mile of the shore, and between them was a big stream﻿—long, shallow pools at the sea end and a chain of waterfalls above. He had gone to earth like a badger somewhere, and I dared not move in case he might be watching me from behind a boulder.
But even as I hesitated he appeared again, fording the stream, his face set on the road we had come. Whatever his errand was he had finished it, and was posting back to his master. For a moment I thought I should follow him, but another instinct prevailed. He had not come to this wild place for the scenery. Somewhere down in the glen there was something or somebody that held the key of the mystery. It was my business to stay there till I had unlocked it. Besides, in two hours it would be dark, and I had had enough walking for one day.

===Chinese===

我得远远坠在后面，尽量借掩护，防他回头；这就意味着他翻过一道梁，我就得加倍赶，免得落下太远；到了开阔地，我又得兜大圈子藏身。我们撞上一条公路，它翻过低口、贴着山侧走，我们顺着它走到山的西侧、望见海的地方。天气好得辉煌，蓝色海面上我看见凉凉的帆影在动，微风吹皱镜面，而我烧得像座熔炉。幸好我底子练得不坏，也真用上了。葡萄牙犹太人在那种破地形上，准保走足了每小时六英里。

五点钟左右，到了一处我不敢再跟的地方。公路平平地贴着海边走，好几英里一览无余。何况那人开始每隔几分钟回头张望。他接近什么东西了，要确信附近没人。我于是离了路，上了山坡——坏我事的山坡——那是一整条碎石和乱石的瀑布。我望见他翻下一道隆起，那像是环着一个小湾的边，山里一道大冰斗降到湾里。准是足足半个钟头后，我才凭着更高的海拔和坏得多的路，上到同一道边。我朝谷里望，我那个人不见了。

他穿不过去的，那地方比我料的宽。一圈黑绝壁一直压到离岸半英里处，中间一条大溪——近海一头是长长的浅潭，上头一串瀑布。他像只獾在哪儿钻了地，我不敢动，怕他从哪块圆石后盯着我。

可正在我迟疑，他又出现了，正涉溪，脸朝着我们来时的路。他的差事不管是什么，已办完了，正急急赶回他主人那里去。有一霎我想跟上去，可另一个本能占了上风。他到这荒地方来不是看风景的。谷里某处，有一样东西或一个人，攥着谜底。我的本分是守到解开它。再说，两个钟头后天就黑，我这一天的路也走够了。

===Original===

I made my way to the stream side and had a long drink. The corrie behind me was lit up with the westering sun, and the bald cliffs were flushed with pink and gold. On each side of the stream was turf like a lawn, perhaps a hundred yards wide, and then a tangle of long heather and boulders right up to the edge of the great rocks. I had never seen a more delectable evening, but I could not enjoy its peace because of my anxiety about the Portuguese Jew. He had not been there more than half an hour, just about long enough for a man to travel to the first ridge across the burn and back. Yet he had found time to do his business. He might have left a letter in some prearranged place﻿—in which case I would stay there till the man it was meant for turned up. Or he might have met someone, though I didn’t think that possible. As I scanned the acres of rough moor and then looked at the sea lapping delicately on the grey sand I had the feeling that a knotty problem was before me. It was too dark to try to track his steps. That must be left for the morning, and I prayed that there would be no rain in the night.
I ate for supper most of the braxy ham and oatcake I had brought from Macmorran’s cottage. It took some self-denial, for I was ferociously hungry, to save a little for breakfast next morning. Then I pulled heather and bracken and made myself a bed in the shelter of a rock which stood on a knoll above the stream. My bedchamber was well hidden, but at the same time, if anything should appear in the early dawn, it gave me a prospect. With my waterproof I was perfectly warm, and, after smoking two pipes, I fell asleep.
My night’s rest was broken. First it was a fox which came and barked at my ear and woke me to a pitch-black night, with scarcely a star showing. The next time it was nothing but a wandering hill-wind, but as I sat up and listened I thought I saw a spark of light near the edge of the sea. It was only for a second, but it disquieted me. I got out and climbed on the top of the rock, but all was still save for the gentle lap of the tide and the croak of some night bird among the crags. The third time I was suddenly quite wide awake, and without any reason, for I had not been dreaming.

===Chinese===

我摸到溪边，痛饮了一气。身后的冰斗叫西斜的太阳照亮，光秃的崖壁红一块金一块。溪的两边是草坪一样的草皮，宽约百码，再往外，长石楠和巨石一直乱到绝壁根。我从没见过更可爱的傍晚，可它的安宁我享受不到，心悬在葡萄牙犹太人身上。他在那儿前后不过半个钟头，大约刚够一个人翻过溪对面的头道山梁再回来。可他把事办成了。他也许在什么约好的地方留了封信——那样我就守到收信人露面。他也可能见了什么人，虽然我认为那不可能。我扫过一英亩一英亩的荒原，又看海在灰沙上轻轻地舐，只觉一道难题摆在面前。天太黑，跟不了他的脚印。只好留给早晨，我祷告夜里别下雨。

晚饭我吃掉了从麦克莫兰小屋带来的大半腌羊肉和燕麦饼。花了不少克己功夫，才给自己留下明早的一口，因为我饿得凶。随后我拔了石楠和蕨，在溪边小丘一块岩石的背风处给自己铺了床。我的卧室藏得好，同时，天蒙蒙亮时若有什么出现，又望得见。有雨衣裹着，我暖暖和和，抽了两斗烟就睡着了。

我这一夜睡得稀碎。先是一只狐狸来在我耳朵边叫，把我叫醒在一片漆黑里，星几乎一颗不见。第二回只是一阵游荡的山风，可我坐起来听时，觉得看见海边有一点火星。只一秒，可它搅得我不安。我爬上岩顶，一切静悄悄，只有潮水的轻舐和岩间什么夜鸟的叫。第三回我忽然彻底醒了，毫无缘由，因为我并没有做梦。

===Original===

Now I have slept hundreds of times alone beside my horse on the veld, and I never knew any cause for such awakenings but the one, and that was the presence near me of some human being. A man who is accustomed to solitude gets this extra sense which announces like an alarm-clock the approach of one of his kind.
But I could hear nothing. There was a scraping and rustling on the moor, but that was only the wind and the little wild things of the hills. A fox, perhaps, or a blue hare. I convinced my reason, but not my senses, and for long I lay awake with my ears at full cock and every nerve tense. Then I fell asleep, and woke to the first flush of dawn.
The sun was behind the Coolin and the hills were black as ink, but far out in the western seas was a broad band of gold. I got up and went down to the shore. The mouth of the stream was shallow, but as I moved south I came to a place where two small capes enclosed an inlet. It must have been a fault in the volcanic rock, for its depth was portentous. I stripped and dived far into its cold abysses, but I did not reach the bottom. I came to the surface rather breathless, and struck out to sea, where I floated on my back and looked at the great rampart of crag. I saw that the place where I had spent the night was only a little oasis of green at the base of one of the grimmest corries the imagination could picture. It was as desert as Damaraland. I noticed, too, how sharply the cliffs rose from the level. There were chimneys and gullies by which a man might have made his way to the summit, but no one of them could have been scaled except by a mountaineer.
I was feeling better now, with all the frowsiness washed out of me, and I dried myself by racing up and down the heather. Then I noticed something. There were marks of human feet at the top of the deep-water inlet﻿—not mine, for they were on the other side. The short sea-turf was bruised and trampled in several places, and there were broken stems of bracken. I thought that some fisherman had probably landed there to stretch his legs.
But that set me thinking of the Portuguese Jew. After breakfasting on my last morsels of food﻿—a knuckle of braxy and a bit of oatcake﻿—I set about tracking him from the place where he had first entered the glen. To get my bearings, I went back over the road I had come myself, and after a good deal of trouble I found his spoor.

===Chinese===

我在草原上伴着马一个人睡过几百回，这样的惊醒我从来只知道一个缘由，就是附近有一个同类。惯于独处的人，长出这样一份额外的感官，像闹钟一样，同类一近就响。

可我什么也听不见。荒原上有搡搡窸窸，那是风，是山里的小野物。狐狸，也许，或蓝野兔。我说服了理性，说服不了感官，好半天我醒着躺，耳朵支到最直，每根神经绷紧。随后睡着了，醒来时是黎明的头一线。

太阳在库林背后，山黑得像墨，可远在西边海面上，横着一带金。我起身下到岸边。溪口浅，往南走，到了两道小岬环着一个小湾的地方。那准是火山岩里的一道断层，深得骇人。我脱了衣，一头扎进它冰冷的深渊，到底没到底。浮上来时相当气短，朝海里游出去，仰面漂着，看那道巨崖的城垛。我看清了我过夜的地方，不过是想象得出的最狰狞的一道冰斗脚下，一小片绿洲。荒得同达马兰一样。我还看出崖从平地拔起得多么陡。烟囱和沟槽倒有几条，人可以爬到顶，可没有一条不是登山家就上得去的。

这会儿我觉得好些了，一身馊气洗净了，我在石楠里来回奔跑，跑干了自己。随后我注意到一样东西。深水小湾的顶上有人的脚印——不是我的，在另一边。矮矮的海滨草皮好几处叫人踩烂了，还有折断的蕨梗。我想多半是哪个渔夫上岸伸伸腿。

可这一想，又想到葡萄牙犹太人。早饭我吃掉最后一点吃食——一节羊腿骨和一角燕麦饼——随后从他头一回进谷的地方，动手追他的踪。为定方位，我先把自己来路倒走了一遍，费了好大事，找着了他的踪迹。

===Original===

It was pretty clear as far as the stream, for he had been walking﻿—or rather running﻿—over ground with many patches of gravel on it. After that it was difficult, and I lost it entirely in the rough heather below the crags. All that I could make out for certain was that he had crossed the stream, and that his business, whatever it was, had been with the few acres of tumbled wilderness below the precipices.
I spent a busy morning there, but found nothing except the skeleton of a sheep picked clean by the ravens. It was a thankless job, and I got very cross over it. I had an ugly feeling that I was on a false scent and wasting my time. I wished to Heaven I had old Peter with me. He could follow spoor like a Bushman, and would have riddled the Portuguese Jew’s track out of any jungle on earth. That was a game I had never learned, for in the old days I had always left it to my natives. I chucked the attempt, and lay disconsolately on a warm patch of grass and smoked and thought about Peter. But my chief reflections were that I had breakfasted at five, that it was now eleven, that I was intolerably hungry, that there was nothing here to feed a grasshopper, and that I should starve unless I got supplies.
It was a long road to my cache, but there were no two ways of it. My only hope was to sit tight in the glen, and it might involve a wait of days. To wait I must have food, and, though it meant relinquishing guard for a matter of six hours, the risk had to be taken. I set off at a brisk pace with a very depressed mind.
From the map it seemed that a shortcut lay over a pass in the range. I resolved to take it, and that shortcut, like most of its kind, was unblessed by Heaven. I will not dwell upon the discomforts of the journey. I found myself slithering among screes, climbing steep chimneys, and travelling precariously along razorbacks. The shoes were nearly rent from my feet by the infernal rocks, which were all pitted as if by some geological smallpox. When at last I crossed the divide, I had a horrible business getting down from one level to another in a gruesome corrie, where each step was composed of smooth boiler-plates. But at last I was among the bogs on the east side, and came to the place beside the road where I had fixed my cache.
The faithful Amos had not failed me.

===Chinese===

到溪边一段相当清楚，因为他是走——不如说跑——过一片多砾石的地面。过了溪就难了，崖下的长石楠里，我彻底丢了它。我能肯定的只有：他过了溪，他的差事——不管是什么——在绝壁下那几英亩乱荒地里。

我在那儿忙了一早晨，只找到一副叫乌鸦啐净的羊骨架。这是件没报偿的活，我越干越毛。我有一种不祥的感觉：我在一条假嗅迹上，白耗工夫。我巴望老彼得在身边。他追踪像布须曼人，葡萄牙犹太人的踪，天底下无论哪片林子他都扒得出来。那一手我从来没学，从前的日子我一概丢给我的土著。我撗了这企图，蔫蔫地躺在一块暖草皮上，抽烟，想彼得。可想得最多的还是：我五点吃的早饭，现在十一点，我锇得没顶，这地方喂不活一只蚱蜢，再搞不到补给我就得锇死。

到藏点路远，可没有第二条道。我唯一的指望是钉死在谷里，而那兴许要一等几天。等就得有吃的，虽说那意味着撗岗六个钟头，风险只好担。我快步出发，心里压得沉沉的。

从地图看，山里有一道口子抄得近。我决意走它，而那近道，同大多数近道一样，不得上天保佑。路上的苦我不多说。我在碎石坡上打滑，爬陡烟囱，在刀脊上险险地走。那一路该死的石头像害过地质天花，坑坑点点，差点把鞋从我脚上撕下来。翻过分水岭后，我费了天大的事，在一道狰狞的冰斗里从一层降到另一层，每一步都是光滑的锅炉板。可末了我终于到了东侧的沼泽，到了公路旁我定藏点的地方。

忠心的阿莫斯没有误我的事。

===Original===

There were the provisions﻿—a couple of small loaves, a dozen tins, and a bottle of whisky. I made the best pack I could of them in my waterproof, swung it on my stick, and started back, thinking that I must be very like the picture of Christian on the title-page of *Pilgrim’s Progress*.
I was liker Christian before I reached my destination﻿—Christian after he had got up the Hill Difficulty. The morning’s walk had been bad, but the afternoon’s was worse, for I was in a fever to get back, and, having had enough of the hills, chose the longer route I had followed the previous day. I was mortally afraid of being seen, for I cut a queer figure, so I avoided every stretch of road where I had not a clear view ahead. Many weary detours I made among moss-hags and screes and the stony channels of burns. But I got there at last, and it was almost with a sense of comfort that I flung my pack down beside the stream where I had passed the night.
I ate a good meal, lit my pipe, and fell into the equable mood which follows upon fatigue ended and hunger satisfied. The sun was westering, and its light fell upon the rock-wall above the place where I had abandoned my search for the spoor.
As I gazed at it idly I saw a curious thing.
It seemed to be split in two and a shaft of sunlight came through between. There could be no doubt about it. I saw the end of the shaft on the moor beneath, while all the rest lay in shadow. I rubbed my eyes, and got out my glasses. Then I guessed the explanation. There was a rock tower close against the face of the main precipice and indistinguishable from it to anyone looking direct at the face. Only when the sun fell on it obliquely could it be discovered. And between the tower and the cliff there must be a substantial hollow.
The discovery brought me to my feet, and set me running towards the end of the shaft of sunlight. I left the heather, scrambled up some yards of screes, and had a difficult time on some very smooth slabs, where only the friction of tweed and rough rock gave me a hold. Slowly I worked my way towards the speck of sunlight, till I found a handhold, and swung myself into the crack. On one side was the main wall of the hill, on the other a tower some ninety feet high, and between them a long crevice varying in width from three to six feet. Beyond it there showed a small bright patch of sea.

===Chinese===

吃的都在——两个小面包，一打罐头，一瓶威士忌。我用雨衣把它们包成尽量像样的一捆，挑在手杖上，动身往回走，心想我这模样，活像《天路历程》扉页上基督徒的画。

没到目的地，我更像基督徒了——翻过困难山以后的基督徒。上午的路坏，下午的更坏，因为我急着赶回，山也走伤了，便选了前一天走的那条远路。我怕极了叫人看见，因为我这副样子古怪，凡前方视野不清的路段我一概避开。在泥沼坑、碎石坡和石头的溪床间，我绕了一个又一个疲倦的弯。可终于到了，把那一捆撙在溪边我过夜的地方，几乎有一种到家的感觉。

我好好吃了一顿，点起烟斗，落进疲劳到头、饥镕得报之后那种平匀的心境。太阳西斜，光照在我撗下寻踪的那处上方的岩壁上。

我闲望着，看见一桩怪事。

它像是裂成两半，一束阳光从中间透过来。这错不了。我看见那束光的末端落在下面的荒原上，其余都躺在影里。我揉揉眼，掏出望远镜。随后我猜出了缘故。紧贴主绝壁的正面立着一座石塔，正对着壁看同绝壁分不出来。只有太阳斜照上去，才认得出。而塔同绝壁之间，必是一道可观的空腔。

这一发现叫我跳起来，朝那束阳光的末端奔去。出了石楠，爬上几码碎石坡，又在一大片极光滑的石板上吃了苦头，全凭呢料同粗岩的摩擦抓得住。我慢慢朝那一点阳光挪，末了摸着一个把手，把自己荡进裂缝。一边是山的主壁，一边是一座九十来英尺的塔，两壁之间一道长缝，宽窄三到六英尺不等。缝的尽头外，露出一小块亮亮的海。

===Original===

There was more, for at the point where I entered it there was an overhang which made a fine cavern, low at the entrance but a dozen feet high inside, and as dry as tinder. Here, thought I, is the perfect hiding-place. Before going farther I resolved to return for food. It was not very easy descending, and I slipped the last twenty feet, landing on my head in a soft patch of screes. At the burnside I filled my flask from the whisky bottle, and put half a loaf, a tin of sardines, a tin of tongue, and a packet of chocolate in my waterproof pockets. Laden as I was, it took me some time to get up again, but I managed it, and stored my belongings in a corner of the cave. Then I set out to explore the rest of the crack.
It slanted down and then rose again to a small platform. After that it dropped in easy steps to the moor beyond the tower. If the Portuguese Jew had come here, that was the way by which he had reached it, for he would not have had the time to make my ascent. I went very cautiously, for I felt I was on the eve of a big discovery. The platform was partly hidden from my end by a bend in the crack, and it was more or less screened by an outlying bastion of the tower from the other side. Its surface was covered with fine powdery dust, as were the steps beyond it. In some excitement I knelt down and examined it.
Beyond doubt there was spoor here. I knew the Portuguese Jew’s footmarks by this time, and I made them out clearly, especially in one corner. But there were other footsteps, quite different. The one showed the rackets of rough country boots, the others were from un-nailed soles. Again I longed for Peter to make certain, though I was pretty sure of my conclusions. The man I had followed had come here, and he had not stayed long. Someone else had been here, probably later, for the un-nailed shoes overlaid the rackets. The first man might have left a message for the second. Perhaps the second was that human presence of which I had been dimly conscious in the nighttime.
I carefully removed all traces of my own footmarks, and went back to my cave. My head was humming with my discovery. I remembered Gresson’s word to his friend: “Tomorrow night.” As I read it, the Portuguese Jew had taken a message from Gresson to someone, and that someone had come from somewhere and picked it up. The message contained an assignation for this very night.

===Chinese===

还不止，在我进入的地方，有一处悬岩，岩下构成一个顶好的洞，口低，里头却有十几英尺高，而且干得像火绒。这儿，我想，就是十全十美的藏身处。再往里探之前，我决意回去取吃的。下去不容易，末了二十英尺我滑了下来，头朝下落进一堆软碎石。在溪边我拿威士忌灌满小酒壶，往雨衣口袋里装了半个面包、一罐沙丁鱼、一罐牛舌、一包巧克力。带着这么一份重量，再爬上去花了些工夫，可我爬上去了，把家当存进洞角。随后我出发探那条缝的余下部分。

它斜下去，又升上来，到一方小小的平台。此后它以一档档容易的台阶，落到塔外的荒原。葡萄牙犹太人若来过这儿，那就是他进来的路，因为他来不及走我这条路。我走得极小心，因为我觉着自己在一桩大发现的前夜。平台从我这一头，叫缝里一个弯半掩着，从那一头，又多少叫塔身一座外伸的堡楼遮着。台面覆着一层细粉的尘，那边的台阶也一样。我压着兴奋，跪下去细看。

千真万确，这里有踪。葡萄牙犹太人的脚印我这时熟了，看得分明，尤其在一个角落。可还有另一路脚印，全然不同。一路是粗钉乡靴的钉印，另一路是没钉的底。我又一次渴望彼得来坐实，虽然我的结论已拿得八九分。我跟的那人到过这里，待的时候不长。另一个人也来过，多半晚些，因为没钉的鞋印压着钉印。第一个人也许给第二个人留了信。那第二个，兴许就是我夜里恍惚觉出的那个人。

我小心抹掉自己的全部脚印，回到洞里。我的脑子嗡嗡响着这项发现。我想起格雷森对他朋友说的那句话：“明晚。”照我的读法，葡萄牙犹太人从格雷森那儿捎信给某个人，那个人从什么地方来取走了。信里约的正是这一夜。

===Original===

I had found a point of observation, for no one was likely to come near my cave, which was reached from the moor by such a toilsome climb. There I should bivouac and see what the darkness brought forth. I remember reflecting on the amazing luck which had so far attended me. As I looked from my refuge at the blue haze of twilight creeping over the waters, I felt my pulses quicken with a wild anticipation.
Then I heard a sound below me, and craned my neck round the edge of the tower. A man was climbing up the rock by the way I had come.

===Chinese===

我找到了一处瞭望点，因为谁也不会凑近我的洞，从荒原上到它那儿得爬那样一场苦路。我就在那儿露营，看黑夜能生下什么来。我记得自己盘算着这一路追随我的惊人运气。我从藏身处望出去，蓝蒙蒙的暮霭正爬上海面，一阵狂野的预感叫我的脉搏加快。

随后我听见底下一声响，把脖子探出塔边。一个人正顺着我来过的那条路往上爬。

## XX The Storm Breaks in the West / 二十 西线风暴骤起
===Original===

The following evening﻿—it was the 20th day of March﻿—I started for France after the dark fell. I drove Ivery’s big closed car, and within sat its owner, bound and gagged, as others had sat before him on the same errand. Geordie Hamilton and Amos were his companions. From what Blenkiron had himself discovered and from the papers seized in the Pink Chalet I had full details of the road and its mysterious stages. It was like the journey of a mad dream. In a back street of a little town I would exchange passwords with a nameless figure and be given instructions. At a wayside inn at an appointed hour a voice speaking a thick German would advise that this bridge or that railway crossing had been cleared. At a hamlet among pine woods an unknown man would clamber up beside me and take me past a sentry-post. Smooth as clockwork was the machine, till in the dawn of a spring morning I found myself dropping into a broad valley through little orchards just beginning to blossom, and I knew that I was in France. After that, Blenkiron’s own arrangements began, and soon I was drinking coffee with a young lieutenant of Chasseurs, and had taken the gag from Ivery’s mouth. The bluecoats looked curiously at the man in the green ulster whose face was the colour of clay and who lit cigarette from cigarette with a shaky hand.
The lieutenant rang up a General of Division who knew all about us. At his headquarters I explained my purpose, and he telegraphed to an Army Headquarters for a permission which was granted. It was not for nothing that in January I had seen certain great personages in Paris, and that Blenkiron had wired ahead of me to prepare the way. Here I handed over Ivery and his guard, for I wanted them to proceed to Amiens under French supervision, well knowing that the men of that great army are not used to let slip what they once hold.
It was a morning of clear spring sunlight when we breakfasted in that little red-roofed town among vineyards with a shining river looping at our feet. The General of Division was an Algerian veteran with a brush of grizzled hair, whose eye kept wandering to a map on the wall where pins and stretched thread made a spider’s web.
“Any news from the north?” I asked.
“Not yet,” he said. “But the attack comes soon. It will be against our army in Champagne.” With a lean finger he pointed out the enemy dispositions.

===Chinese===

第二天傍晚——那是三月二十日——天黑以后，我动身去法国。我开着艾弗里那辆大型轿车，车里坐着车主本人，绑着堵着嘴，从前别人办同一桩差事时也是这样坐的。乔迪·汉密尔顿同埃莫斯押着他。凭布伦基伦亲自探得的东西，加上从粉红木屋里搜出的一批文件，那条路线同它神秘的各站，我掌握得清清楚楚。那简直像一场疯狂的梦。在一座小城的后街上，我同一个无名的人影对口令，领指示；在一家路边客栈的约定时辰，一个满口浓重德语的嗓音通知我，这座桥、那处铁路道口已经疏通；在松林里的一座小村，一个陌生男人爬上我旁边，带我过哨卡。那部机器走得像钟表一样顺；直到一个春日清晨的曙光里，我发现自己顺着刚刚开花的果园，降进一道宽阔的谷地——我知道，到法国了。打那以后，接手的是布伦基伦自己的安排；不多时，我已同一位年轻的猎兵中尉喝上咖啡，也扯掉了艾弗里嘴里的堵嘴布。蓝军装们好奇地打量那个穿绿大衣的人：他脸色像黏土，点烟的手一根接一根地抖。

中尉摇电话给一位对我们底细全知的师长。在他的司令部里我说明来意，他向某个集团军司令部发报请示，获准了。一月里我在巴黎会过几位大人物，布伦基伦又先我打电报铺了路，这些都不白费。在这里我把艾弗里同看守移交出去，要他们在法国人的监护下去亚眠——我很清楚，那支伟大军队的人，到手的东西从不松手。

那是一个清澈的春日早晨，我们在葡萄园间那座红瓦小城里吃早饭，一条亮晃晃的河在脚边绕了个弯。师长是位阿尔及利亚老军旅，一蓬花白硬发，眼睛不住溜向墙上那张地图——图钉同拉直的细线结成一张蛛网。

“北边有消息么？”我问。

“还没有，”他说。“可攻势快了。会打我们香巴尼的集团军。”他用一根瘦手指点着敌人的部署。

===Original===

“Why not against the British?” I asked. With a knife and fork I made a right angle and put a salt dish in the centre. “That is the German concentration. They can so mass that we do not know which side of the angle they will strike till the blow falls.”
“It is true,” he replied. “But consider. For the enemy to attack towards the Somme would be to fight over many miles of an old battleground where all is still desert and every yard of which you British know. In Champagne at a bound he might enter unbroken country. It is a long and difficult road to Amiens, but not so long to Chilons. Such is the view of Petain. Does it convince you?”
“The reasoning is good. Nevertheless he will strike at Amiens, and I think he will begin today.”
He laughed and shrugged his shoulders. “*Nous verrons.* You are obstinate, my general, like all your excellent countrymen.”
But as I left his headquarters an aide-de-camp handed him a message on a pink slip. He read it, and turned to me with a grave face.
“You have a flair, my friend. I am glad we did not wager. This morning at dawn there is great fighting around St. Quentin. Be comforted, for they will not pass. Your *Marechal* will hold them.”
That was the first news I had of the battle.
At Dijon according to plan I met the others. I only just caught the Paris train, and Blenkiron’s great wrists lugged me into the carriage when it was well in motion. There sat Peter, a docile figure in a carefully patched old R.F.C. uniform. Wake was reading a pile of French papers, and in a corner Mary, with her feet up on the seat, was sound asleep.
We did not talk much, for the life of the past days had been so hectic that we had no wish to recall it. Blenkiron’s face wore an air of satisfaction, and as he looked out at the sunny spring landscape he hummed his only tune. Even Wake had lost his restlessness. He had on a pair of big tortoiseshell reading glasses, and when he looked up from his newspaper and caught my eye he smiled. Mary slept like a child, delicately flushed, her breath scarcely stirring the collar of the greatcoat which was folded across her throat. I remember looking with a kind of awe at the curve of her young face and the long lashes that lay so softly on her cheek, and wondering how I had borne the anxiety of the last months.

===Chinese===

“为什么不打英国人？”我问。我拿刀叉摆出一个直角，角心搁一只盐碟。“这就是德国人的集结。他们能这样聚兵，叫我们到拳头落下，才知道他们打角的那一边。”

“说得对，”他答。“可你想：敌人朝索姆河进攻，就得在一片旧战场上打许多英里，那里至今一片荒凉，每一码你们英国人都熟。在香巴尼，他一蹴就可以进完好的国土。去亚眠的路又长又难，去沙隆可就不那么长。这是贝当的看法。你说服么？”

“推理很漂亮。可他要打的是亚眠，而且我看就在今天。”

他耸耸肩笑了。“咱们瞧着吧。你固执，我的将军，同你那位顶出色的同胞一个样。”

可我离开他司令部时，一名副官递给他一张粉纸条。他读完，一脸郑重地转向我。

“你有直觉，我的朋友。幸亏咱们没打赌。今儿拂晓，圣康坦周围打大了。放心，他们过不来。你们的元帅会顶住他们。”

那是我得到的关于这一仗的第一条消息。

按计划，我在第戎同其余的人会合。巴黎的火车我差一点误了，是布伦基伦那双大手在车已经开动时把我拽进车厢的。皮特坐在那儿，一个听话的身影，穿一身仔细补过的旧皇家飞行队制服。韦克在读一摞法国报纸；角落里，玛丽把脚跏上座位，睡得正香。

我们没多说话，过去几天的日子那样火热，谁也不想重提。布伦基伦一脸满意，望着窗外晴朗的春色，哼着他唯一的那支曲子。连韦克也丢了那副躁劲。他戴一副大玳琩老花镜，从报纸上抬起眼撞上我的目光，竟笑了笑。玛丽睡得像个孩子，两颊淡淡泛红，呼吸几乎吹不动折在她颈前的大衣领子。我记得我带着几分敬畏看她那张年轻脸庞的曲线，看她软软落在腮上的长睫毛，心想：过去几个月的焦灼，我居然挺过来了。

===Original===

Wake raised his head from his reading, glanced at Mary and then at me, and his eyes were kind, almost affectionate. He seemed to have won peace of mind among the hills.
Only Peter was out of the picture. He was a strange, disconsolate figure, as he shifted about to ease his leg, or gazed incuriously from the window. He had shaved his beard again, but it did not make him younger, for his face was too lined and his eyes too old to change. When I spoke to him he looked towards Mary and held up a warning finger.
“I go back to England,” he whispered. “Your little *mysie* is going to take care of me till I am settled. We spoke of it yesterday at my cottage. I will find a lodging and be patient till the war is over. And you, Dick?”
“Oh, I rejoin my division. Thank God, this job is over. I have an easy *trund* now and can turn my attention to straightforward soldiering. I don’t mind telling you that I’ll be glad to think that you and Mary and Blenkiron are safe at home. What about you, Wake?”
“I go back to my Labour battalion,” he said cheerfully. “Like you, I have an easier mind.”
I shook my head. “We’ll see about that. I don’t like such sinful waste. We’ve had a bit of campaigning together and I know your quality.”
“The battalion’s quite good enough for me,” and he relapsed into a day-old *Temps*.
Mary had suddenly woke, and was sitting upright with her fists in her eyes like a small child. Her hand flew to her hair, and her eyes ran over us as if to see that we were all there. As she counted the four of us she seemed relieved.
“I reckon you feel refreshed, Miss Mary,” said Blenkiron. “It’s good to think that now we can sleep in peace, all of us. Pretty soon you’ll be in England and spring will be beginning, and please God it’ll be the start of a better world. Our work’s over, anyhow.”
“I wonder,” said the girl gravely. “I don’t think there’s any discharge in this war. Dick, have you news of the battle? This was the day.”
“It’s begun,” I said, and told them the little I had learned from the French General. “I’ve made a reputation as a prophet, for he thought the attack was coming in Champagne. It’s St. Quentin right enough, but I don’t know what has happened. We’ll hear in Paris.”
Mary had woke with a startled air as if she remembered her old instinct that our work would not be finished without a sacrifice, and that sacrifice the best of us.

===Chinese===

韦克从报纸上抬起头，先瞟玛丽，再瞟我，眼睛里是和善的，几乎是关爱的。在群山里，他像是赢来了内心的安宁。

只有皮特同这幅画格格不入。他是个古怪、黯然的身影，不是挪动身子想让腿舒服些，就是淡漠地望着窗外。胡子又剃了，可这并不能叫他年轻：他的脸纹路太深，眼睛太老，改不动了。我对他说话，他朝玛丽那边望望，竖起一根指头。

“我回英国，”他低声说。“你那小姑娘要照看我，等我安顿下来。昨天在我小屋里我们说好了。我寻一处住处，耐心等战争打完。你呢，迪克？”

“哦，我回我的师。谢天谢地，这桩差事完了。往后是一趟轻差，我可以专心当我的兵。我不妨告诉你：想到你、玛丽、布伦基伦都平平安安在家，我就高兴。你呢，韦克？”

“我回我的劳工营，”他快活地说。“同你一样，我心里也轻省了。”

我摇头。“这个咱们再商量。我不喜欢这样罪惉地浪费。咱们一处在战场上滚过一阵，我知道你的成色。”

“那个营对我尽够好了，”他又埋进一份隔天的《时报》。

玛丽忽然醒了，坐直身子，两只拳头揉着眼睛，像个小孩子。她一只手飞快掠掠头发，眼睛把我们挨个点过，像在查我们都在。数清我们四个，她似乎放了心。

“我看你歇过来了，玛丽小姐，”布伦基伦说。“想想真髙兴，如今咱们人人都能安安稳稳睡觉了。不久你就到英国了，春天也要开头了；托上帝的福，这会是一个更好世界的开端。不管怎么说，咱们的活儿完了。”

“难说，”姑娘郑重地说。“我看这场战争里没有退伍这一说。迪克，仗上有你的消息么？就是今天。”

“开打了，”我说，把从法国将军那里听来的那一点告诉他们。“我落了个先知的名声，因为他以为攻势在香巴尼。千真万确是圣康坦；可究竟怎样了，我不知道。到巴黎就听见了。”

玛丽醒来时带着一种吃惊的神情，仿佛又想起了她那个老直觉：我们的活儿不完，少不了要交出一桩牺牲，而牺牲的是我们中间最好的那个。

===Original===

The notion kept recurring to me with an uneasy insistence. But soon she appeared to forget her anxiety. That afternoon as we journeyed through the pleasant land of France she was in holiday mood, and she forced all our spirits up to her level. It was calm, bright weather, the long curves of ploughland were beginning to quicken into green, the catkins made a blue mist on the willows by the watercourses, and in the orchards by the red-roofed hamlets the blossom was breaking. In such a scene it was hard to keep the mind sober and grey, and the pall of war slid from us. Mary cosseted and fussed over Peter like an elder sister over a delicate little boy. She made him stretch his bad leg full length on the seat, and when she made tea for the party of us it was a protesting Peter who had the last sugar biscuit. Indeed, we were almost a merry company, for Blenkiron told stories of old hunting and engineering days in the West, and Peter and I were driven to cap them, and Mary asked provocative questions, and Wake listened with amused interest. It was well that we had the carriage to ourselves, for no queerer rigs were ever assembled. Mary, as always, was neat and workmanlike in her dress; Blenkiron was magnificent in a suit of russet tweed with a pale-blue shirt and collar, and well-polished brown shoes; but Peter and Wake were in uniforms which had seen far better days, and I wore still the boots and the shapeless and ragged clothes of Joseph Zimmer, the porter from Arosa.
We appeared to forget the war, but we didn’t, for it was in the background of all our minds. Somewhere in the north there was raging a desperate fight, and its issue was the true test of our success or failure. Mary showed it by bidding me ask for news at every stopping-place. I asked gendarmes and *Permissionnaires*, but I learned nothing. Nobody had ever heard of the battle. The upshot was that for the last hour we all fell silent, and when we reached Paris about seven o’clock my first errand was to the bookstall.
I bought a batch of evening papers, which we tried to read in the taxis that carried us to our hotel. Sure enough there was the announcement in big headlines. The enemy had attacked in great strength from south of Arras to the Oise; but everywhere he had been repulsed and held in our battle-zone.

===Chinese===

这个念头一次次回到我心里，执拗得叫人不安。可不久她就仿佛忘了这份担忧。那天下午，我们的车穿行在法兰西这片可爱的土地上，她一味兴致勃勃，硬是把我们全伙的情绪拉到她的高度。天气平静晴朗，长长一弯一弯的犁地里正回出绿意，柔夷花在沟渠边的柳树上蒙起一层蓝雾，红瓦小村旁的果园里花正绽开。在这样的景色里，人很难保持一颗冷静灰暗的心，战争的阴雔从我们身上滑了下去。玛丽娇惯皮特、伺候皮特，像姐姐待一个娇弱的小弟弟。她让他把那条坏腿在座位上伸直；她给全伙沏茶时，最后一块糖饼塞给了连声抗议的皮特。说实在的，我们几乎算得上一伙快活人了：布伦基伦讲他早年在西部打猎同做工程的故事，皮特同我被逼着也吹几段，玛丽问些撩人的问题，韦克听得津津有味。幸好这节车厢归我们独占，因为再没凑过比这更奇怪的一伙。玛丽照旧穿得利落合用；布伦基伦一身赤褐花呢，浅蓝衬衫领子，棕鞋擦得锃亮，气派极了；皮特同韦克却穿着早过了好日子的军装；我还穿着阿罗萨挑夫约瑟夫·齐默尔的靴子同那身没形没状的破衣裳。

我们仿佛忘了战争，其实没有：它垫在我们每个人的心底。北方某处正打着一场拼命的仗，它的结局才是我们成败的真正考验。玛丽把这一点露出来，是催我逢站便去打听消息。我问宪兵，问休假的兵，一无所获。谁都没听说过这场仗。结果最后一点钟大家全哑了；七点来钟到巴黎，我的头一件差事就是奔报摊。

我买了一批晚报，在送我们去旅馆的出租汽车上大家传着看。果然，大标题登着呢。敌人从阿拉斯以南到瓦兹河，以优势兵力发起进攻；但处处被击退，被挡在我们的战斗地带里。

===Original===

The leading articles were confident, the notes by the various military critics were almost braggart. At last the German had been driven to an offensive, and the Allies would have the opportunity they had longed for of proving their superior fighting strength. It was, said one and all, the opening of the last phase of the war.
I confess that as I read my heart sank. If the civilians were so overconfident, might not the generals have fallen into the same trap? Blenkiron alone was unperturbed. Mary said nothing, but she sat with her chin in her hands, which with her was a sure sign of deep preoccupation.
Next morning the papers could tell us little more. The main attack had been on both sides of St. Quentin, and though the British had given ground it was only the outposts line that had gone. The mist had favoured the enemy, and his bombardment had been terrific, especially the gas shells. Every journal added the old old comment﻿—that he had paid heavily for his temerity, with losses far exceeding those of the defence.
Wake appeared at breakfast in his private’s uniform. He wanted to get his railway warrant and be off at once, but when I heard that Amiens was his destination I ordered him to stay and travel with me in the afternoon. I was in uniform myself now and had taken charge of the outfit. I arranged that Blenkiron, Mary, and Peter should go on to Boulogne and sleep the night there, while Wake and I would be dropped at Amiens to await instructions.
I spent a busy morning. Once again I visited with Blenkiron the little cabinet in the Boulevard St. Germain, and told in every detail our work of the past two months. Once again I sat in the low building beside the Invalides and talked to staff officers. But some of the men I had seen on the first visit were not there. The chiefs of the French Army had gone north.
We arranged for the handling of the Wild Birds, now safely in France, and sanction was given to the course I had proposed to adopt with Ivery. He and his guard were on their way to Amiens, and I would meet them there on the morrow. The great men were very complimentary to us, so complimentary that my knowledge of grammatical French ebbed away and I could only stutter in reply. That telegram sent by Blenkiron on the night of the 18th, from the information given me in the Pink Chalet, had done wonders in clearing up the situation.

===Chinese===

社论信心十足，各军事评论家的短评几乎近于吹牛。德国人终于被逼出来进攻了，协约国将得到他们久盼的机会，证明自己更强的战斗力。人人都说：这是战争最后一阶段的开场。

我承认，读着读着我的心往下沉。倘若文官们这样盲目乐观，将军们会不会也落进同一个陷阱？只有布伦基伦不动声色。玛丽一言不发，双手托着下巴坐着——在她，这是深心思索的可靠标志。

第二天早晨，报纸多告诉我们不了多少。主攻在圣康坦两侧；英军虽然退了，丢的只是警戒线。大雾帮了敌人，他的炮火凶猛极了，毒气弹尤其厉害。每家报纸都添上那句老而又老的评语：他为他的冒失付出了重价，损失远超过防守的一方。

韦克穿着列兵军装来吃早饭。他想领了乘车证立刻走；可我一听他的目的地是亚眠，就命令他留下，下午随我同行。我自己也换了军装，接管了这一队人马。我安排布伦基伦、玛丽同皮特先去布伦，在那儿过夜；韦克同我在亚眠下车待命。

我忙了一个上午。我同布伦基伦再一次访问圣日耳曼大道那间小机要室，把这两个月的工作逐条汇报；再一次坐在荣军院旁那排低矮的房子里，同参谋军官们谈话。可我头一回来见的有些人不在了。法国陆军的首脑们已经北上。

我们安排了野鸟们的处置——如今他们已安全押到法国——我提的对艾弗里的处置办法也获了批准。他同看守正在来亚眠的路上，我明天在那儿同他们会合。那些大人物对我们满口恭维，恭维得我那点语法法语都退了潮，只会结巴作答。布伦基伦十八日夜里依照我在粉红木屋提供的情报发的那封电报，在澄清局面上立了大功。

===Original===

But when I asked them about the battle they could tell me little. It was a very serious attack in tremendous force, but the British line was strong and the reserves were believed to be sufficient. Petain and Foch had gone north to consult with Haig. The situation in Champagne was still obscure, but some French reserves were already moving thence to the Somme sector. One thing they did show me, the British dispositions. As I looked at the plan I saw that my old division was in the thick of the fighting.
“Where do you go now?” I was asked.
“To Amiens, and then, please God, to the battle front,” I said.
“Good fortune to you. You do not give body or mind much rest, my general.”
After that I went to the *Mission Anglaise*, but they had nothing beyond Haig’s communique and a telephone message from G.H.Q. that the critical sector was likely to be that between St. Quentin and the Oise. The northern pillar of our defence, south of Arras, which they had been nervous about, had stood like a rock. That pleased me, for my old battalion of the Lennox Highlanders was there.
Crossing the Place de la Concorde, we fell in with a British staff officer of my acquaintance, who was just starting to motor back to G.H.Q. from Paris leave. He had a longer face than the people at the Invalides.
“I don’t like it, I tell you,” he said. “It’s this mist that worries me. I went down the whole line from Arras to the Oise ten days ago. It was beautifully sited, the cleverest thing you ever saw. The outpost line was mostly a chain of blobs﻿—redoubts, you know, with machine-guns﻿—so arranged as to bring flanking fire to bear on the advancing enemy. But mist would play the devil with that scheme, for the enemy would be past the place for flanking fire before we knew it﻿ ﻿… Oh, I know we had good warning, and had the battle-zone manned in time, but the outpost line was meant to hold out long enough to get everything behind in apple-pie order, and I can’t see but how big chunks of it must have gone in the first rush.﻿ ﻿… Mind you, we’ve banked everything on that battle-zone. It’s damned good, but if it’s gone﻿—” He flung up his hands.
“Have we good reserves?” I asked.
He shrugged his shoulders.
“Have we positions prepared behind the battle-zone?”
“I didn’t notice any,” he said dryly, and was off before I could get more out of him.

===Chinese===

可我问起战事，他们也说不出多少。是一次兵力极重、非常严重的进攻；可英军防线坚固，预备队据信足够。贝当同福煦已北上同黑格会商。香巴尼的形势仍不明朗，但一些法国预备队已从那边向索姆河地段调动。他们倒是给我看了一样东西：英军的部署。我一看部署图，就看见我的老师正处在打得最稠的地方。

“你现在去哪儿？”他们问我。

“去亚眠，然后，托上帝的福，上战线，”我说。

“祝你好运。你这人，身子同脑子都不大肯歇，我的将军。”

随后我到英国军事使团去，可他们除了黑格的战报，只有总司令部一条电话：危急地段多半在圣康坦同瓦兹河之间。我们防线的北支柱——阿拉斯以南那一段，他们原先担心的——像岩石一样立住了。这叫我高兴，因为我从前的伦诺克斯高地营就在那儿。

穿过协和广场时，我们撞见一位相识的英国参谋军官，他休巴黎假刚满，正要坐汽车回总司令部。他比荣军院里那些人拉着更长的脸。

“我不喜欢，告诉你吧，”他说。“揪我心的就是这场雾。十天前我从阿拉斯到瓦兹河把整条防线走了一遍。配置得漂亮极了，你从没见过那么巧的。警戒线大多是一串点——一串支撑点，带机枪——布得正好叫侧射火力扫前进的敌人。可雾能叫这套全乱套：我们还不知道，敌人已经绕过侧射的位置了……哦，我知道我们有预警，战斗地带也及时进人了；可警戒线原该撑得够久，好叫后方一切就绪。我看头一阵猛冲，大块大块准丢了……你记住，我们把一切都押在战斗地带上了。它是好得没话说；可它要是丢了——”他双手一摊。

“我们的预备队好么？”我问。

他耸耸肩。

“战斗地带后面我们备好了阵地么？”

“没瞧见有什么，”他干巴巴地说，没等我再多问就走了。

===Original===

“You look rattled, Dick,” said Blenkiron as we walked to the hotel.
“I seem to have got the needle. It’s silly, but I feel worse about this show than I’ve ever felt since the war started. Look at this city here. The papers take it easily, and the people are walking about as if nothing was happening. Even the soldiers aren’t worried. You may call me a fool to take it so hard, but I’ve a sense in my bones that we’re in for the bloodiest and darkest fight of our lives, and that soon Paris will be hearing the Boche guns as she did in 1914.”
“You’re a cheerful old Jeremiah. Well, I’m glad Miss Mary’s going to be in England soon. Seems to me she’s right and that this game of ours isn’t quite played out yet. I’m envying you some, for there’s a place waiting for you in the fighting line.”
“You’ve got to get home and keep people’s heads straight there. That’s the weak link in our chain and there’s a mighty lot of work before you.”
“Maybe,” he said abstractedly, with his eye on the top of the Vendome column.
The train that afternoon was packed with officers recalled from leave, and it took all the combined purchase of Blenkiron and myself to get a carriage reserved for our little party. At the last moment I opened the door to admit a warm and agitated captain of the R.F.C. in whom I recognized my friend and benefactor, Archie Roylance.
“Just when I was gettin’ nice and clean and comfy a wire comes tellin’ me to bundle back, all along of a new battle. It’s a cruel war, Sir.” The afflicted young man mopped his forehead, grinned cheerfully at Blenkiron, glanced critically at Peter, then caught sight of Mary and grew at once acutely conscious of his appearance. He smoothed his hair, adjusted his tie and became desperately sedate.
I introduced him to Peter and he promptly forgot Mary’s existence. If Peter had had any vanity in him it would have been flattered by the frank interest and admiration in the boy’s eyes. “I’m tremendously glad to see you safe back, sir. I’ve always hoped I might have a chance of meeting you. We want you badly now on the front. Lensch is gettin’ a bit uppish.”
Then his eye fell on Peter’s withered leg and he saw that he had blundered. He blushed scarlet and looked his apologies. But they weren’t needed, for it cheered Peter to meet someone who talked of the possibility of his fighting again.

===Chinese===

“你看着发慌了，迪克，”我们往旅馆走时布伦基伦说。

“我心里发毛。是傻，可这一仗，我打开战以来还没这样难受过。你瞧这座城。报纸拿它不当回事，人在街上溜达，仿佛天下无事。连兵都不着急。你可以骂我把事情看得太重；可我骨子里有一种感觉：咱们迎面撞上的是此生最血、最黑的一仗，不久巴黎就要像一九一四年那样听见德国佬的炮了。”

“你真是个快活的老先知。唉，玛丽小姐快要回英国了，我倒高兴。我看她说得对，咱们这盘棋还没下完。我还有点羡慕你：火线上给你留着位子呢。”

“你得回家去，把那边的脑袋扶正。那是我们链条上最弱的一环，你前头还有一大堆活。”

“也许吧，”他心不在焉地说，眼睛望着旺多姆柱的顶。

那天下午的车上挤满了被召回假期的军官；靠布伦基伦同我两人合起来的力气，才算给我们这一小伙人包下一间车厢。最后一刻，我打开门，放进来一位热得满头是汗、心神不定的皇家飞行队上尉——我认出是我的朋友兼恩人阿奇·罗兰斯。

“我刚洗得干干净净、舒舒服服，一封电报来叫我卷铺盖回去，就为一场新仗。这仗真狠心，长官。”这位遭殃的年轻人擦着脑门，冲布伦基伦咧嘴一笑，挑剔地瞟了瞟皮特，随后一眼撞见玛丽，立刻浑身上下过不去起来。他抹平头发，正一正领带，变得极其端凝。

我把他介绍给皮特，他登时忘了玛丽的 existence。皮特心里哪怕有一点虚荣，也会被小伙子眼里那份坦率的兴趣同仰慕捧上天。“又见您平安归来，我太高兴了，长官。我一直盼着有机会见您。如今前线太需要您了。伦施近来有点翘尾巴。”

随后他的眼睛落到皮特那条干瘪的腿上，明白自己失言了。他脸红到脖子，用目光道歉。可道歉是多余的：遇见一个还谈论他重返战斗可能的人，皮特反倒来了精神。

===Original===

Soon the two were deep in technicalities, the appalling technicalities of the airman. It was no good listening to their talk, for you could make nothing of it, but it was bracing up Peter like wine. Archie gave him a minute description of Lensch’s latest doings and his new methods. He, too, had heard the rumour that Peter had mentioned to me at St. Anton, of a new Boche plane, with mighty engines and stumpy wings cunningly cambered, which was a devil to climb; but no specimens had yet appeared over the line. They talked of Bali, and Rhys Davids, and Bishop, and McCudden, and all the heroes who had won their spurs since the Somme, and of the new British makes, most of which Peter had never seen and had to have explained to him.
Outside a haze had drawn over the meadows with the twilight. I pointed it out to Blenkiron.
“There’s the fog that’s doing us. This March weather is just like October, mist morning and evening. I wish to Heaven we could have some good old drenching spring rain.”
Archie was discoursing of the Shark-Gladas machine.
“I’ve always stuck to it, for it’s a marvel in its way, but it has my heart fairly broke. The General here knows its little tricks. Don’t you, sir? Whenever things get really excitin’, the engine’s apt to quit work and take a rest.”
“The whole make should be publicly burned,” I said, with gloomy recollections.
“I wouldn’t go so far, sir. The old Gladas has surprisin’ merits. On her day there’s nothing like her for pace and climbing-power, and she steers as sweet as a racin’ cutter. The trouble about her is she’s too complicated. She’s like some breeds of car﻿—you want to be a mechanical genius to understand her﻿ ﻿… If they’d only get her a little simpler and safer, there wouldn’t be her match in the field. I’m about the only man that has patience with her and knows her merits, but she’s often been nearly the death of me. All the same, if I were in for a big fight against some fellow like Lensch, where it was neck or nothing, I’m hanged if I wouldn’t pick the Gladas.”
Archie laughed apologetically. “The subject is banned for me in our mess. I’m the old thing’s only champion, and she’s like a mare I used to hunt that loved me so much she was always tryin’ to chew the arm off me. But I wish I could get her a fair trial from one of the big pilots. I’m only in the second class myself after all.”
We were running north of St.

===Chinese===

不多时，两人已扎进行话，飞行家那套吓死人的行话。他们的话旁人听不出名堂，可它给皮特提的劲像酒。阿奇把伦施近来的动向同新战法一五一十讲给他听。他也听说了皮特在圣安东对我提过的那个传闻：德国佬一种新飞机，发动机大、翅膀短而弯得刁，爬升凶得很；只是还没有样机在战线那边露过面。他们谈巴尔，谈里斯-戴维斯、毕晓普、麦卡登，谈索姆河以来所有赢得马刺的英雄，还谈英国的新机型——多半皮特都没见过，要人一样样讲给他听。

外面，一片霭随着暮色罩上了草场。我指给布伦基伦看。

“正把我们害苦的就是这场雾。三月的天气跟十月一样，早晚都是雾。上天保佑，来一场痛痛快快的春雨吧。”

阿奇正讲到鲨鱼-格拉达斯型飞机。

“我一直没撒手它，因为它自有它的神妙；可它把我的心都伤透了。将军知道它那些小脾气。是不是，长官？每逢事情真正吃紧，发动机就爱撂挑子歇工。”

“这一型号就该当众烧掉，”我说，怀着阴郁的回忆。

“我不至于说那么重，长官。老格拉达斯有惊人的长处。它状态好的日子，速度爬升没一个赶得上，转向顺得像一条赛艇。毛病出在它太复杂。它像某几个牌子的汽车——你得是个机械天才才摸得透它的脾气……他们只要把它再简化些、再稳妥些，战场上就没有能同它匹敌的了。如今差不多只有我一个人有耐心待见它、知道它的好处；可它好几回差点要了我的命。话虽如此，真要同伦施那样的人打一场大仗，拼个你死我活，我还是非挑格拉达斯不可，不挑我就是孙子。”

阿奇抱歉地笑了笑。“在我们营里，这个话题对我是个禁区。我是这老东西唯一的辩护人；它像我从前打猎骑的一匹牝马，爱我爱得老想嘴掉我一条胳膊。可我真盼哪位大飞行家能给它一场公道的试飞。说到底，我自己不过是二流人物。”

我们的车正跑过圣——

===Original===

Just when above the rattle of the train rose a curious dull sound. It came from the east, and was like the low growl of a veld thunderstorm, or a steady roll of muffled drums.
“Hark to the guns!” cried Archie. “My aunt, there’s a tidy bombardment goin’ on somewhere.”
I had been listening on and off to guns for three years.
