没拆的信里有一封彼得的，厚厚的一大摞，我坐下来从容地读。那是一封古怪的函，是他给我写过的最长的一封，那厚度叫我明白了他的寂寞。他还在德国的战俘营，可天天盼着去瑞士。他说想回英国或南非都办得到，因为他们清楚他再不能上前线了；可他想还是待在瑞士好，朋友们都在打仗，他在英国会不好受。同往常一样，他一句不抱怨，似乎还对那点小小的恩惠感激。有个医生待他好，俘虏里也有几个好人。

可彼得的信主要是沉思。他一向有点哲人味道，如今在孤独里，他往深思里扎了下去，把结果用他笨拙的笔迹，倾倒在一张张薄纸上给我。字里行间我读得出，他正同自己打一场硬仗。他在试着叫勇气不停摆，好面对他可能受的顶狠的试炼——一个瘸腿的晚年。他对圣经向来知道得不少，如今它和《天路历程》是他沉思的两大帮手。两样他都读得字字当真，当它们是最近真事的报纸报道。

===Original===

He mentioned that after much consideration he had reached the conclusion that the three greatest men he had ever heard of or met were Mr. Valiant-for-Truth, the Apostle Paul, and a certain Billy Strang who had been with him in Mashonaland in ’92. Billy I knew all about; he had been Peter’s hero and leader till a lion got him in the Blaauwberg. Peter preferred Valiant-for-Truth to Mr. Greatheart, I think, because of his superior truculence, for, being very gentle himself, he loved a bold speaker. After that he dropped into a vein of self-examination. He regretted that he fell far short of any of the three. He thought that he might with luck resemble Mr. Standfast, for like him he had not much trouble in keeping wakeful, and was also as “poor as a howler,” and didn’t care for women. He only hoped that he could imitate him in making a good end.
Then followed some remarks of Peter’s on courage, which came to me in that London room as if spoken by his living voice. I have never known anyone so brave, so brave by instinct, or anyone who hated so much to be told so. It was almost the only thing that could make him angry. All his life he had been facing death, and to take risks seemed to him as natural as to get up in the morning and eat his breakfast. But he had started out to consider the very thing which before he had taken for granted, and here is an extract from his conclusions. I paraphrase him, for he was not grammatical.

===Chinese===

他提到，经过反复掂量，他得出结论：他有生以来听说过或见过的三个最伟大的人，是忠勇先生、使徒保罗，还有一个比利·斯特兰，九二年同他在马绍纳兰的。比利我全知道；他一直是彼得的英雄和头领，直到一头狮子在布劳伯格要了他。彼得觉得忠勇先生比好心先生强，我想是因为他那份更足的狠劲，因为彼得自己太温存，偏爱说话硬气的人。随后他转入一段自我省察。他憾恨自己离那三个都差得远。他想，运气好的话他也许像得上斯坦法斯特先生，因为同那位一样，他夜里不愁睡不着，也一样“穷得叮当响”，也不近女色。他只盼能学他的样，得一个好收场。

随后是彼得关于勇气的一番议论，在这间伦敦的屋子里，像他活着的声音说给我听。我从没见过谁这样勇敢、这样天生勇敢，也没见过谁这样恨人这么说。那几乎是唯一能叫他动怒的事。他一辈子都在面对死亡，冒风险在他同早晨起床吃早饭一样自然。可这一回，他动笔去想他从前从不放在心上的那件事，下面抄他结论的一段。我替他顺了顺句子，因为他的文法不成章法。

===Original===

> It’s easy enough to be brave if you’re feeling well and have food inside you. And it’s not so difficult even if you’re short of a meal and seedy, for that makes you inclined to gamble. I mean by being brave playing the game by the right rules without letting it worry you that you may very likely get knocked on the head. It’s the wisest way to save your skin. It doesn’t do to think about death if you’re facing a charging lion or trying to bluff a lot of savages. If you think about it you’ll get it; if you don’t, the odds are you won’t. That kind of courage is only good nerves and experience﻿ ﻿… Most courage is experience. Most people are a little scared at new things﻿ ﻿…
> You want a bigger heart to face danger which you go out to look for, and which doesn’t come to you in the ordinary way of business. Still, that’s pretty much the same thing﻿—good nerves and good health, and a natural liking for rows. You see, Dick, in all that game there’s a lot of fun. There’s excitement and the fun of using your wits and skill, and you know that the bad bits can’t last long. When Arcoll sent me to Makapan’s kraal I didn’t altogether fancy the job, but at the worst it was three parts sport, and I got so excited that I never thought of the risk till it was over﻿ ﻿…
> But the big courage is the cold-blooded kind, the kind that never lets go even when you’re feeling empty inside, and your blood’s thin, and there’s no kind of fun or profit to be had, and the trouble’s not over in an hour or two but lasts for months and years. One of the men here was speaking about that kind, and he called it “Fortitude.” I reckon fortitude’s the biggest thing a man can have﻿—just to go on enduring when there’s no guts or heart left in you. Billy had it when he trekked solitary from Garungoze to the Limpopo with fever and a broken arm just to show the Portugooses that he wouldn’t be downed by them. But the head man at the job was the Apostle Paul﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

> 一个人身上没病、肚里有食，勇敢容易。就算缺一顿饭、身上发软，也不太难，因为那反倒叫你想赌一把。我说的勇敢，是照正经规矩玩这场游戏，又不住心里去——虽然脑袋很可能叫人敲碎。这是保命顶聪明的法子。迎面碰上扑来的狮子，或者要唬住一群蛮子的时候，想死的事没好处。你想它，它就来；你不想，多半不来。那一路勇气不过是好神经加老经验……勇气大半是经验。多数人对新东西都有点怕……
> 你出去自找的那种险，不在日常买卖路上送上门来的那种险，要更大的心去迎。不过那也还是一路货——好神经，好身体，天生爱吵架。你瞧，迪克，那套把戏里有的是乐子。有刺激，有使心眼使手艺的乐子，你也知道坏日子长不了。阿科尔派我去马卡潘的寨子那回，我不大乐意那差事，可往坏里说也是七分玩，我兴奋得过了事才想起险来……
> 可大的勇气是冷血的那一种，是肚里空了、血也稀了、一点乐子和油水都没有、熬头也不是一两个钟头而是几个月几年，也决不撒手的那一种。这儿有个人谈起过这一种，管它叫“坚忍”。我看坚忍是一个人能有的顶大的东西—— guts 和 heart 都没了的时候，照旧熬下去。比利独个儿从加伦戈泽走到林波波河，发着热、断着一条胳膊，就为给那些葡萄牙人看看压不垮他，凭的就是这个。可这门行的头一号人物，是使徒保罗……

===Original===

Peter was writing for his own comfort, for fortitude was all that was left to him now. But his words came pretty straight to me, and I read them again and again, for I needed the lesson. Here was I losing heart just because I had failed in the first round and my pride had taken a knock. I felt honestly ashamed of myself, and that made me a far happier man. There could be no question of dropping the business, whatever its difficulties. I had a queer religious feeling that Ivery and I had our fortunes intertwined, and that no will of mine could keep us apart. I had faced him before the war and won; I had faced him again and lost; the third time or the twentieth time we would reach a final decision. The whole business had hitherto appeared to me a trifle unreal, at any rate my own connection with it. I had been docilely obeying orders, but my real self had been standing aside and watching my doings with a certain aloofness. But that hour in the Tube station had brought me into the serum, and I saw the affair not as Bullivant’s or even Blenkiron’s, but as my own. Before I had been itching to get back to the Front; now I wanted to get on to Ivery’s trail, though it should take me through the nether pit. Peter was right; fortitude was the thing a man must possess if he would save his soul.
The hours passed, and, as I expected, there came no word from Macgillivray. I had some dinner sent up to me at seven o’clock, and about eight I was thinking of looking up Blenkiron. Just then came a telephone call asking me to go round to Sir Walter Bullivant’s house in Queen Anne’s Gate.
Ten minutes later I was ringing the bell, and the door was opened to me by the same impassive butler who had admitted me on that famous night three years before. Nothing had changed in the pleasant green-panelled hall; the alcove was the same as when I had watched from it the departure of the man who now called himself Ivery; the telephone book lay in the very place from which I had snatched it in order to ring up the First Sea Lord. And in the back room, where that night five anxious officials had conferred, I found Sir Walter and Blenkiron.
Both looked worried, the American feverishly so. He walked up and down the hearthrug, sucking an unlit black cigar.
“Say, Dick,” he said, “this is a bad business. It wasn’t no fault of yours. You did fine. It was us﻿—me and Sir Walter and Mr.

===Chinese===

彼得这信是写给他自己宽心的，因为坚忍如今是他剩下的全部。可他的话对着我心口来，我一遍又一遍读，因为我需要这一课。瞧我，只因头一个回合败了、脸面挨了一下，就要泄气。我老老实实为自己害臊，这一臊倒把我臊成一个快活得多的人。这桩事再难，也谈不上撂下了。我有一种古怪的宗教感：艾维里和我的气数缠在一起，凭我的意愿解不开。战前我同他对过阵，赢了；后来又对过，输了；第三回也好第二十回也好，我们总得见个最后的分晓。这桩事到此为止，在我眼里总有点不实，至少我自己同它的牵连不实。我一直听话地奉命行事，可我那个真我站在一边，隔着一层看我的所作所为。可地铁车站里那一小时把我卷进了血里，我看这桩事不再是比较文特的、甚至不再是布伦基伦的，是我自己的。先前我急着回前线；如今我要上艾维里的踪，哪怕它通到十八层地狱。彼得说得对；人要救自己的灵魂，必须有的一样就是坚忍。

几个钟头过去，不出所料，麦吉利夫雷没有消息。七点钟我让人把饭送上来，八点来钟正打算去看布伦基伦，来了个电话，请我去安妮女王门的沃尔特·比利文特爵士府上。

十分钟后我在按铃，开门的还是那位不动声力的管家，三年前那个著名的夜晚放我进门的也是他。绿护壁的悦目门厅一样没变；那处壁龛还是老样，当年我就从那儿看着如今自称艾维里的人离去；电话簿还在老地方，我正是从那儿一把抓起它、给第一海务大臣打电话的。而在后间——那夜五位焦灼的官员议事的屋子——我找到了沃尔特爵士和布伦基伦。

两人都愁容满面，美国人愁得发着烧。他在炉前毯上走来走去，咂着一支没点的黑雪茄。

“听我说，迪克，”他说，“这事坏了。不怪你。你干得漂亮。是我们——我，沃尔特爵士，还有麦吉利夫雷先生——当了逃兵。”

===Original===

Macgillivray that were the quitters.”
“Any news?” I asked.
“So far the cover’s drawn blank,” Sir Walter replied. “It was the devil’s own work that our friend looked your way today. You’re pretty certain he saw that you recognized him?”
“Absolutely. As sure as that he knew I recognized him in your hall three years ago when he was swaggering as Lord Alloa.”
“No,” said Blenkiron dolefully, “that little flicker of recognition is just the one thing you can’t be wrong about. Land alive! I wish Mr. Macgillivray would come.”
The bell rang, and the door opened, but it was not Macgillivray. It was a young girl in a white ball-gown, with a cluster of blue cornflowers at her breast. The sight of her fetched Sir Walter out of his chair so suddenly that he upset his coffee cup.
“Mary, my dear, how did you manage it? I didn’t expect you till the late train.”
“I was in London, you see, and they telephoned on your telegram. I’m staying with Aunt Doria, and I cut her theatre party. She thinks I’m at the Shandwick’s dance, so I needn’t go home till morning﻿ ﻿… Good evening, General Hannay. You got over the Hill Difficulty.”
“The next stage is the Valley of Humiliation,” I answered.
“So it would appear,” she said gravely, and sat very quietly on the edge of Sir Walter’s chair with her small, cool hand upon his.
I had been picturing her in my recollection as very young and glimmering, a dancing, exquisite child. But now I revised that picture. The crystal freshness of morning was still there, but I saw how deep the waters were. It was the clean fineness and strength of her that entranced me. I didn’t even think of her as pretty, any more than a man thinks of the good looks of the friend he worships.
We waited, hardly speaking a word, till Macgillivray came. The first sight of his face told his story.
“Gone?” asked Blenkiron sharply. The man’s lethargic calm seemed to have wholly deserted him.
“Gone,” repeated the newcomer. “We have just tracked him down. Oh, he managed it cleverly. Never a sign of disturbance in any of his lairs. His dinner ordered at Biggleswick and several people invited to stay with him for the weekend﻿—one a member of the Government. Two meetings at which he was to speak arranged for next week. Early this afternoon he flew over to France as a passenger in one of the new planes.

===Chinese===

“有消息么？”我问。

“到这会儿收网收了个空，”沃尔特爵士答。“咱们那位朋友今天偏偏朝你看，真是魔鬼的安排。他看见你认出了他，这一点你拿得准？”

“绝对准。同三年前他在你门厅里冒充阿洛阿勋爵、我知道我认出了他，一样地准。”

“是，”布伦基伦惨兮兮地说，“认没认出来这一闪，是天下唯一不会看错的东西。老天爷！麦吉利夫雷先生怎么还不来。”

门铃响，门开了，进来的不是麦吉利夫雷。是一位穿白色舞裙的年轻姑娘，胸前簪着一簇蓝色矢车菊。一见她，沃尔特爵士霍地离了椅，带翻了咖啡杯。

“玛丽，亲爱的，你怎么赶来的？我当你坐夜车才到呢。”

“我这就在伦敦呀，你的电报一挂过来。我住多丽亚姑姑家，我把她的戏班子给翘了。她只当我在桑德威克家的舞会上，不到早晨不用回家……晚上好，汉内将军。你翻过困难山了。”

“下一站是屈辱谷，”我答。

“看样子是，”她郑重地说，安安静静坐在沃尔特爵士椅子的扶手上，一只凉凉的小手搭在他手上。

在我记忆里，我一直把她想成很年轻、闪闪发光，一个跳舞的、精致的孩子。此刻我修正了这幅画。清晨水晶般的新鲜还在，可我看见了那水有多深。叫我着迷的，是她那份干净的精细和力量。我甚至不想到她漂亮，就像人不想到自己崇拜的朋友的相貌。

我们几乎一言不发地等着，直到麦吉利夫雷来。一看他的脸，故事就讲完了。

“走了？”布伦基雷厉声问。那人一贯的懒散的镇定像丢了个干净。

“走了，”新来的人重复。“我们刚追出他的踪。哦，他办得漂亮。哪一处巢都没有一点惊动。比格尔斯维克的晚饭订好了，周末还邀了几位客人——其中一位是政府成员。下周安排了两场他演讲的集会。今天下午一早，他化名换脸几个月混进了航空委员会那些人里——坐一架新式飞机，以乘客身份飞过了法国。

拉明顿小姐发现这一点，晚了一步。那架飞机偏了航线，落在诺曼底。这会儿我们的人在巴黎，或者更远。”

He had been mixed up with the Air Board people for months﻿—of course as another man with another face. Miss Lamington discovered that just too late. The bus went out of its course and came down in Normandy. By this time our man’s in Paris or beyond it.”
Sir Walter took off his big tortoiseshell spectacles and laid them carefully on the table.
“Roll up the map of Europe,” he said. “This is our Austerlitz. Mary, my dear, I am feeling very old.”
Macgillivray had the sharpened face of a bitterly disappointed man. Blenkiron had got very red, and I could see that he was blaspheming violently under his breath. Mary’s eyes were quiet and solemn. She kept on patting Sir Walter’s hand. The sense of some great impending disaster hung heavily on me, and to break the spell I asked for details.
“Tell me just the extent of the damage,” I asked. “Our neat plan for deceiving the Boche has failed. That is bad. A dangerous spy has got beyond our power. That’s worse. Tell me, is there still a worst? What’s the limit of mischief he can do?”
Sir Walter had risen and joined Blenkiron on the hearthrug. His brows were furrowed and his mouth hard as if he were suffering pain.
“There is no limit,” he said. “None that I can see, except the long-suffering of God. You know the man as Ivery, and you knew him as that other whom you believed to have been shot one summer morning and decently buried. You feared the second﻿—at least if you didn’t, I did﻿—most mortally. You realized that we feared Ivery, and you knew enough about him to see his fiendish cleverness. Well, you have the two men combined in one man. Ivery was the best brain Macgillivray and I ever encountered, the most cunning and patient and long-sighted. Combine him with the other, the chameleon who can blend himself with his environment, and has as many personalities as there are types and traits on the earth. What kind of enemy is that to have to fight?”
“I admit it’s a steep proposition. But after all how much ill can he do? There are pretty strict limits to the activity of even the cleverest spy.”
“I agree. But this man is not a spy who buys a few wretched subordinates and steals a dozen private letters. He’s a genius who has been living as part of our English life. There’s nothing he hasn’t seen. He’s been on terms of intimacy with all kinds of politicians. We know that. He did it as Ivery.

===Chinese===

沃尔特爵士摘下他那副大玳瑁眼镜，小心地摆在桌上。

“把欧洲的地图卷起来吧，”他说。“这是我们的奥斯特里茨。玛丽，亲爱的，我觉着自己很老了。”

麦吉利夫雷绷着一张大失所望的人的脸。布伦基伦涨得通红，我看得出他在不出声地暴骂。玛丽的眼睛安静而庄重。她一下一下拍着沃尔特爵士的手。一场大祸將临的感觉沉沉压着我，为破这个咒，我要细节。

“给我说说损失到底多大，”我说。“我们那套骗德国佬的漂亮计划泡了汤。这是坏。一个危险的间谍脱出了我们的手心。这更坏。告诉我，还有没有更坏的？他闯祸的限度在哪儿？”

沃尔特爵士起身，到炉前毯上同布伦基伦并了肩。他眉头拧着，嘴抿得硬，像忍着疼。

“没有限度，”他说。“我看不出限度，除非算上帝的长期忍耐。你们认识那个人，叫他艾维里；也认识另一个他，你们本以为某个夏天早晨已经毙了、体面埋了的那个。你们怕那第二个——至少你们不怕的话，我怕——怕得要死。你们也明白我们怕艾维里，你们对他的鬼精也知道得够多。好，如今这两个人合在了一个人身上。艾维里是我和麦吉利夫雷碰见过的最好的脑子，最狡猾、最有耐心、最看得远。再把他同另一个合起来——那个能随环境变色的变色龙，天底下有多少种人有多少种性子他就有多少副人格。这样的敌人，叫人怎么打？”

“我认这笔账不小。可他到底能作多大的恶？再精的间谍，活动也有挺严的界限。”

“我同意。可这人不是那种买几个 miserable 下属、偷十几封私信的间谍。他是个天才，多少年来就活在我们英国生活里头。他没有没见过的。他同各式各样的政客都是过从。这我们知道，他以艾维里的身份干的。

他们还挺喜欢他，因为他聪明、会捧，同他讲事情。可他在另外那些人格里看见的、听见的，天知道。保不准他在唐宁街吃过早饭，揣着威尔逊总统的介绍信，或者以中立国名流的身份参观过大舰队。再想想那些女人；她们多能说。我们是世上最漏风的社会，我们保平安靠的是把危险分子挡在门外。我们指望外层弹幕。可谁真钻进了里面，就有一百万个机会。而这一个，记住，是千万人里挑一的，脑子一刻不睡，一点风就抓，十几句闲话能拼出一盘棋。这就像——就像情报部长忽然叛逃去了敌营……寻常间谍只握着一把互不相干的碎事实。这个人握着我们的生活、我们的思路、我们的一切。”

===Original===

They rather liked him, for he was clever and flattered them, and they told him things. But God knows what he saw and heard in his other personalities. For all I know he may have breakfasted at Downing Street with letters of introduction from President Wilson, or visited the Grand Fleet as a distinguished neutral. Then think of the women; how they talk. We’re the leakiest society on earth, and we safeguard ourselves by keeping dangerous people out of it. We trust to our outer barrage. But anyone who has really slipped inside has a million chances. And this, remember, is one man in ten millions, a man whose brain never sleeps for a moment, who is quick to seize the slightest hint, who can piece a plan together out of a dozen bits of gossip. It’s like﻿—it’s as if the Chief of the Intelligence Department were suddenly to desert to the enemy﻿ ﻿… The ordinary spy knows only bits of unconnected facts. This man knows our life and our way of thinking and everything about us.”
“Well, but a treatise on English life in time of war won’t do much good to the Boche.”
Sir Walter shook his head. “Don’t you realize the explosive stuff that is lying about? Ivery knows enough to make the next German peace offensive really deadly﻿—not the blundering thing which it has been up to now, but something which gets our weak spots on the raw. He knows enough to wreck our campaign in the field. And the awful thing is that we don’t know just what he knows or what he is aiming for. This war’s a packet of surprises. Both sides are struggling for the margin, the little fraction of advantage, and between evenly matched enemies it’s just the extra atom of foreknowledge that tells.”
“Then we’ve got to push off and get after him,” I said cheerfully.
“But what are you going to do?” asked Macgillivray. “If it were merely a question of destroying an organization it might be managed, for an organization presents a big front. But it’s a question of destroying this one man, and his front is a razor edge. How are you going to find him? It’s like looking for a needle in a haystack, and such a needle! A needle which can become a piece of straw or a tin-tack when it chooses!”
“All the same we’ve got to do it,” I said, remembering old Peter’s lesson on fortitude, though I can’t say I was feeling very stouthearted.
Sir Walter flung himself wearily into an armchair.

===Chinese===

“可一篇战时英国生活的论文，对德国佬也帮不了多少忙。”

沃尔特爵士摇头。“你不明白那遍地堆着的炸药么？艾维里知道的东西，够把德国人下一场和平攻势变得真致命——不再是至今那种瞎撞的东西，而是样样捝在我们的软肋上。他知道的东西，够毁掉我们战场上的战局。而可怕的是，我们不知道他到底知道什么、图什么。这场仗是一包意外。两边争的都是那点边际，那一小分便宜，而势均力敌的对手之间，起作用的恰恰是先见的那一个原子。”

“那咱们就得赶紧动身追他，”我欢欢地说。

“可你们打算怎么追？”麦吉利夫雷问。“若只是捣毁一个组织，倒好办，因为组织摆着大正面。可这是收拾这一个人，他的正面是一道剃刀刃。你们怎么找他？这等于草垛里找针，还是那样一根针！一根想变草棍就变草棍、想变图钉就变图钉的针！”

“再难也得干，”我说，心里记着老彼得的坚忍课，虽然不能说自己底气很足。

沃尔特爵士疲惫地跌进扶手椅。

===Original===

“I wish I could be an optimist,” he said, “but it looks as if we must own defeat. I’ve been at this work for twenty years, and, though I’ve been often beaten, I’ve always held certain cards in the game. Now I’m hanged if I’ve any. It looks like a knockout, Hannay. It’s no good deluding ourselves. We’re men enough to look facts in the face and tell ourselves the truth. I don’t see any ray of light in the business. We’ve missed our shot by a hairsbreadth and that’s the same as missing by miles.”
I remember he looked at Mary as if for confirmation, but she did not smile or nod. Her face was very grave and her eyes looked steadily at him. Then they moved and met mine, and they seemed to give me my marching orders.
“Sir Walter,” I said, “three years ago you and I sat in this very room. We thought we were done to the world, as we think now. We had just that one miserable little clue to hang on to﻿—a dozen words scribbled in a notebook by a dead man. You thought I was mad when I asked for Scudder’s book, but we put our backs into the job and in twenty-four hours we had won out. Remember that then we were fighting against time. Now we have a reasonable amount of leisure. Then we had nothing but a sentence of gibberish. Now we have a great body of knowledge, for Blenkiron has been brooding over Ivery like an old hen, and he knows his ways of working and his breed of confederate. You’ve got something to work on now. Do you mean to tell me that, when the stakes are so big, you’re going to chuck in your hand?”
Macgillivray raised his head. “We know a good deal about Ivery, but Ivery’s dead. We know nothing of the man who was gloriously resurrected this evening in Normandy.”
“Oh, yes we do. There are many faces to the man, but only one mind, and you know plenty about that mind.”
“I wonder,” said Sir Walter. “How can you know a mind which has no characteristics except that it is wholly and supremely competent? Mere mental powers won’t give us a clue. We want to know the character which is behind all the personalities. Above all we want to know its foibles. If we had only a hint of some weakness we might make a plan.”
“Well, let’s set down all we know,” I cried, for the more I argued the keener I grew. I told them in some detail the story of the night in the Coolin and what I had heard there.
“There’s the two names Chelius and Bommaerts.

===Chinese===

“但愿我乐得起来，”他说，“可看来只好认输。这行我干了二十年，虽常吃败仗，手里总还拤着几张牌。如今我一张也没有。这像是被击倒了，汉内。骗自己没好处。咱们是男人，敢正视事实，敢对自己说真话。这事里我看不到一线光。我们差一根头发丝没打着，而差一丝同差一里是一回事。”

我记得他望着玛丽，像等她作保，可她没笑也没点头。她一脸郑重，眼睛定定望着他。随后那双眼移过来，遇上了我的，像给了我行军的号令。

“沃尔特爵士，”我说，“三年前，你和我坐在这间屋里。我们当自己输定了，同今晚一样。那时我们只攤着一个可怜的小小线索——一个死人记事本里涂的十几个字。我要斯卡德尔的本子时，你当我疯了，可我们把命压上去，二十四个钟头就赢了。记住，那时我们同时间赛跑。如今我们有一份从容。那时我们只有一句胡话。如今我们有一大宗知识，因为布伦基伦像只老母鸡那样把艾维里喋在翅膀底下，他的行事路数、他那一路的同党，他都摸得。你们如今有东西可下手了。你们要告诉我，赌注这样大，你们倒要撂牌？”

麦吉利夫雷抬起头。“艾维里我们知道得不少，可艾维里死了。今晚在诺曼底光荣复活的那一个，我们一无所知。”

“不，我们知道。那人有许多张脸，可只有一个脑子，那个脑子你们知道得不少。”

“难说，”沃尔特爵士说。“一个除了完完全全、登峰造极的能干以外没有任何特征的脑子，你怎么认识它？光凭智力给不了线索。我们要认识的是那一堆人格背后的品性。顶要紧是认识它的癖病。哪怕给我们一点弱点的影子，我们就能定计。”

“好，那就把咱们知道的全摊出来，”我叫道，因为越争我越起劲。我把库林山上那一夜的事，相当详细地讲给他们，还有我在那儿听见的。

“有切利乌斯和博马茨这两个名字。

===Original===

The man spoke them in the same breath as Elfenbein, so they must be associated with Ivery’s gang. You’ve got to get the whole Secret Service of the Allies busy to fit a meaning to these two words. Surely to goodness you’ll find something! Remember those names don’t belong to the Ivery part, but to the big game behind all the different disguises﻿ ﻿… Then there’s the talk about the Wild Birds and the Cage Birds. I haven’t a guess at what it means. But it refers to some infernal gang, and among your piles of records there must be some clue. You set the intelligence of two hemispheres busy on the job. You’ve got all the machinery, and it’s my experience that if even one solitary man keeps chewing on at a problem he discovers something.”
My enthusiasm was beginning to strike sparks from Macgillivray. He was looking thoughtful now, instead of despondent.
“There might be something in that,” he said, “but it’s a far-out chance.”
“Of course it’s a far-out chance, and that’s all we’re ever going to get from Ivery. But we’ve taken a bad chance before and won﻿ ﻿… Then you’ve all that you know about Ivery here. Go through his dossier with a small-tooth comb and I’ll bet you find something to work on. Blenkiron, you’re a man with a cool head. You admit we’ve a sporting chance.”
“Sure, Dick. He’s fixed things so that the lines are across the track, but we’ll clear somehow. So far as John S. Blenkiron is concerned he’s got just one thing to do in this world, and that’s to follow the yellow dog and have him neatly and cleanly tidied up. I’ve got a stack of personal affronts to settle. I was easy fruit and he hasn’t been very respectful. You can count me in, Dick.”
“Then we’re agreed,” I cried. “Well, gentlemen, it’s up to you to arrange the first stage. You’ve some pretty solid staff work to put in before you get on the trail.”
“And you?” Sir Walter asked.
“I’m going back to my brigade. I want a rest and a change. Besides, the first stage is office work, and I’m no use for that. But I’ll be waiting to be summoned, and I’ll come like a shot as soon as you hoick me out. I’ve got a presentiment about this thing. I know there’ll be a finish and that I’ll be in at it, and I think it will be a desperate, bloody business too.”
I found Mary’s eyes fixed upon me, and in them I read the same thought.

===Chinese===

那个人说这两个名字同 Elfenbein（象牙）是一口气说的，所以它们必同艾维里那一伙有牵连。你们得叫协约国全部特工忙起来，给这两个词配出意思来。老天爷在上，总能找出点什么！记住，这两个名字不属于艾维里这一面，而属于一切伪装背后的大局……还有那套野鸟和笼中鸟的议论。它是什么意思我猜不出。可它指的是某支地狱队伍，你们那一摞摞档案里必有线索。你们把两个半球的智力都开动起来。机器你们全有，而照我的经验，哪怕孤零零一个人对着难题不停地嚼，也能嚼出东西来。”

我的热劲开始从麦吉利夫雷身上击出火星。他这时显出思索，不垂头丧气了。

“这里头兴许有点东西，”他说，“不过是一步远棋。”

“当然是远棋，而对艾维里，咱们能拿到的从来只有远棋。可咱们赌过更坏的运气，赢了……再说，你们还有艾维里在这边的全部旧账。拿细齿梳把他的档案梳一遍，我打赌你们找得出可下手的东西。布伦基伦，你是个冷静的人。你认咱们还有一搏的机会。”

“认，迪克。他把横木摆上了道，可咱们总会翻过去。就约翰·S·布伦基伦而言，他在这世上只剩一件事要办，就是盯住那条黄狗，把他干干净净、利利索索地收抬了。我私人的旧账也攒了一大堆。我是块送到嘴边的肥肉，他对我可不大客气。算我一个，迪克。”

“那咱们一致了，”我叫。“好，先生们，头一阶段归你们排。上道之前，你们有一阵结结实实的参谋活要干。”

“那你呢？”沃尔特爵士问。

“我回我的旅。我要歇歇，换换气。再说头一阶段是案头活，我插不上手。可我等着传唤，你们一拏我，我立刻就到。这件事我有预感。我知道它有收场，收场时我必在场，而且我看那还是一场玩命的、见血的营生。”

我发现玛丽的眼睛定着我，我在里面读出同一个念头。

===Original===

She had not spoken a word, but had sat on the edge of a chair, swinging a foot idly, one hand playing with an ivory fan. She had given me my old orders and I looked to her for confirmation of the new.
“Miss Lamington, you are the wisest of the lot of us. What do you say?”
She smiled﻿—that shy, companionable smile which I had been picturing to myself through all the wanderings of the past month.
“I think you are right. We’ve a long way to go yet, for the Valley of Humiliation comes only halfway in the *Pilgrim’s Progress*. The next stage was Vanity Fair. I might be of some use there, don’t you think?”
I remember the way she laughed and flung back her head like a gallant boy.
“The mistake we’ve all been making,” she said, “is that our methods are too *terre-à-terre*. We’ve a poet to deal with, a great poet, and we must fling our imaginations forward to catch up with him. His strength is his unexpectedness, you know, and we won’t beat him by plodding only. I believe the wildest course is the wisest, for it’s the most likely to intersect his﻿ ﻿… Who’s the poet among us?”
“Peter,” I said. “But he’s pinned down with a game leg in Germany. All the same we must rope him in.”
By this time we had all cheered up, for it is wonderful what a tonic there is in a prospect of action. The butler brought in tea, which it was Bullivant’s habit to drink after dinner. To me it seemed fantastic to watch a slip of a girl pouring it out for two grizzled and distinguished servants of the State and one battered soldier﻿—as decorous a family party as you would ask to see﻿—and to reflect that all four were engaged in an enterprise where men’s lives must be reckoned at less than thistledown.
After that we went upstairs to a noble Georgian drawing-room and Mary played to us. I don’t care two straws for music from an instrument﻿—unless it be the pipes or a regimental band﻿—but I dearly love the human voice. But she would not sing, for singing to her, I fancy, was something that did not come at will, but flowed only like a bird’s note when the mood favoured. I did not want it either. I was content to let “Cherry Ripe” be the one song linked with her in my memory.
It was Macgillivray who brought us back to business.
“I wish to Heaven there was one habit of mind we could definitely attach to him and to no one else.” (At this moment “He” had only one meaning for us.)

===Chinese===

她一句话没说，只坐在椅子边上，一只脚闲闲地晃，一只手玩一把象牙扇。旧号令是她下的，新的我也望着她认可。

“拉明顿小姐，你是我们中间最明白的人。你说呢？”

她笑了——那羞涩的、伙伴式的笑，过去一个月的漂泊里我一直这样想着它。

“我想你是对的。路还长着呢，《天路历程》里，屈辱谷才走到一半。下一站是虚荣市。在那儿我兴许有点用处，你们说呢？”

我记得她笑起来、头往后一甩的样子，像个勇敢的男孩。

“咱们大家一直犯的错，”她说，“是法子太实、太就事论事。咱们对付的是一个诗人，一个大诗人，咱们得把想象力拋到前头去追他。他的力量在于出人意料，你们知道的，光靠磨性子赢不了他。我看最狂的一路就是最聪明的一路，因为最可能同他那一道交叉……咱们中间谁是诗人？”

“彼得，”我说。“可他拖着条坏腿钉在德国。也得把他拉进来。”

这时大家的精神都起来了，前景里有一股行动的药力，真叫人想不到。管家端上茶——比利文特有饭后喝茶的习惯。我看一个 slender 的姑娘给两位鬓发斑白的国家重臣和一个打过仗的军人斟茶——一个再体面不过的家常场面——而心里转着：这四个人正办的是一桩人命贱过蓟绒的事业。那光景真像一場梦。

饭后我们上楼到一间堂皇的乔治式客厅，玛丽为我们弹琴。乐器的音乐我一向不在乎两根草——除非是风笛或军乐队——可我深爱人的嗓子。她不肯唱，我想，唱歌于她不是随叫随来的东西，只在兴致凑巧时像鸟声一样自己流。我也不要。我甘愿让《樱桃熟了》做记忆里同她相连的唯一一支歌。

把我们拉回正事的又是麦吉利夫雷。

“我巴不得有一副思维习惯，能一口咬定是他的、不是别人的。”（此刻“他”在我们中间只有一个意思。）

===Original===

“You can’t do nothing with his mind,” Blenkiron drawled. “You can’t loose the bands of Orion, as the Bible says, or hold Leviathan with a hook. I reckoned I could and made a mighty close study of his de-vices. But the darned cuss wouldn’t stay put. I thought I had tied him down to the double bluff, and he went and played the triple bluff on me. There’s nothing doing that line.”
A memory of Peter recurred to me.
“What about the ‘blind spot’?” I asked, and I told them old Peter’s pet theory. “Every man that God made has his weak spot somewhere, some flaw in his character which leaves a dull patch in his brain. We’ve got to find that out, and I think I’ve made a beginning.”
Macgillivray in a sharp voice asked my meaning.
“He’s in a funk﻿ ﻿… of something. Oh, I don’t mean he’s a coward. A man in his trade wants the nerve of a buffalo. He could give us all points in courage. What I mean is that he’s not clean white all through. There are yellow streaks somewhere in him﻿ ﻿… I’ve given a good deal of thought to this courage business, for I haven’t got a great deal of it myself. Not like Peter, I mean. I’ve got heaps of soft places in me. I’m afraid of being drowned for one thing, or of getting my eyes shot out. Ivery’s afraid of bombs﻿—at any rate he’s afraid of bombs in a big city. I once read a book which talked about a thing called agoraphobia. Perhaps it’s that﻿ ﻿… Now if we know that weak spot it helps us in our work. There are some places he won’t go to, and there are some things he can’t do﻿—not well, anyway. I reckon that’s useful.”
“Ye-es,” said Macgillivray. “Perhaps it’s not what you’d call a burning and a shining light.”
“There’s another chink in his armour,” I went on. “There’s one person in the world he can never practise his transformations on, and that’s me. I shall always know him again, though he appeared as Sir Douglas Haig. I can’t explain why, but I’ve got a feel in my bones about it. I didn’t recognize him before, for I thought he was dead, and the nerve in my brain which should have been looking for him wasn’t working. But I’m on my guard now, and that nerve’s functioning at full power. Whenever and wherever and howsoever we meet again on the face of the earth, it will be ‘Dr. Livingstone, I presume’ between him and me.”
“That is better,” said Macgillivray.

===Chinese===

“他的脑子你下不了手，”布伦基伦拖长腔说。“你解不开猎户的带子，圣经上说的，也拿钩子钓不住利维坦。我原以为能，把他那套器具做了顶细的研究。可那该死的家伙不肯待在原地。我刚把他钉死在双重欺骗上，他一转身对我玩起三重的。那条道没戏。”

一段彼得的记忆浮上来。

“‘盲点’怎么说？”我问，随后把老彼得的宝贝理论讲给他们。“上帝造的每个人身上都有一处弱点，品性上的一道裂缝，在脑里留下一块暗斑。咱们得把那块找出来，我想我已经开了个头。”

麦吉利夫雷厉声问我什么意思。

“他怕……怕一样东西。哦，我不是说他胆小。干他那一行的要有野牛的神经。论胆量他给咱们所有人让子都赢。我是说，他不是通体的纯白。他里头什么地方有黄的条纹……这份勇气的事我想得很多，因为我自己没多少勇气。不像彼得，我是说。我身上软地方一堆。比方我怕淹死，怕眼睛叫枪打瞎。艾维里怕炸弹——至少他怕大城市里的炸弹。我读过一本书，讲一种叫广场恐怖的病。兴许就是那个……咱们知道了这个弱点，活儿就好干了。有些地方他不去，有些事他干不了——至少干不好。我看这就有用。”

“是——啊，”麦吉利夫雷说。“算不上什么燃着的、放光的灯。”

“他铠甲上还有一道缝，”我接着说。“这世上有一个人，他永远玩不成变身，那就是我。他哪怕扮成道格拉斯·黑格爵士，我也认得出他。道理我说不清，可我骨头里有这个感觉。先前我没认出他，因为我当他死了，脑子里那根该盯着他的神经没上班。如今我上了岗，那根神经火力全开。往后不管在天涯海角、什么场合再碰上，我和他之间就是一句‘我猜，您是利文斯通博士吧’。”

“这就好些了，”麦吉利夫雷说。

===Original===

“If we have any luck, Hannay, it won’t be long till we pull you out of His Majesty’s Forces.”
Mary got up from the piano and resumed her old perch on the arm of Sir Walter’s chair.
“There’s another blind spot which you haven’t mentioned.” It was a cool evening, but I noticed that her cheeks had suddenly flushed.
“Last week Mr. Ivery asked me to marry him,” she said.

===Chinese===

“运气好，汉内，用不了多久我们就把你从陛下军队里拉出来了。”

玛丽从琴边起身，回到她那老位子，沃尔特爵士的椅子扶手上。

“还有一个盲点，你没提。”那晚天凉，我却看见她的两颊忽然红了。

“上礼拜，艾维里先生向我求婚了，”她说。

## II “The Village Named Morality” / 二 名叫“道德”的村子
===Original===

Up on the high veld our rivers are apt to be strings of pools linked by muddy trickles﻿—the most stagnant kind of watercourse you would look for in a day’s journey. But presently they reach the edge of the plateau and are tossed down into the flats in noble ravines, and roll thereafter in full and sounding currents to the sea. So with the story I am telling. It began in smooth reaches, as idle as a millpond; yet the day soon came when I was in the grip of a torrent, flung breathless from rock to rock by a destiny which I could not control. But for the present I was in a backwater, no less than the Garden City of Biggleswick, where Mr. Cornelius Brand, a South African gentleman visiting England on holiday, lodged in a pair of rooms in the cottage of Mr. Tancred Jimson.
The house﻿—or “home” as they preferred to name it at Biggleswick﻿—was one of some two hundred others which ringed a pleasant Midland common. It was badly built and oddly furnished; the bed was too short, the windows did not fit, the doors did not stay shut; but it was as clean as soap and water and scrubbing could make it. The three-quarters of an acre of garden were mainly devoted to the culture of potatoes, though under the parlour window Mrs. Jimson had a plot of sweet-smelling herbs, and lines of lank sunflowers fringed the path that led to the front door. It was Mrs. Jimson who received me as I descended from the station fly﻿—a large red woman with hair bleached by constant exposure to weather, clad in a gown which, both in shape and material, seemed to have been modelled on a chintz curtain. She was a good kindly soul, and as proud as Punch of her house.
“We follow the simple life here, Mr. Brand,” she said. “You must take us as you find us.”
I assured her that I asked for nothing better, and as I unpacked in my fresh little bedroom with a west wind blowing in at the window I considered that I had seen worse quarters.
I had bought in London a considerable number of books, for I thought that, as I would have time on my hands, I might as well do something about my education. They were mostly English classics, whose names I knew but which I had never read, and they were all in a little flat-backed series at a shilling apiece. I arranged them on top of a chest of drawers, but I kept the *Pilgrim’s Progress* beside my bed, for that was one of my working tools and I had got to get it by heart.

===Chinese===

在我们高原草原上，河流多半是一串水潭，中间由泥浆细流连着——你走一天路也找不出比这更死气的水道。可它们终于到了高原的边上，被抖进深谷，摔成壮丽的瀑布，此后便以充实喧响的水流滚滚入海。我要讲的故事也是这样。它开端在平缓的河段，懒同磨坊池塘一样；可那一天很快就来了：我落进一股激流的掌心，叫一股我掌管不了的命运，喘不上气地从一个岩石甩向另一个岩石。可眼下我还待在一段回水里——不比那座名叫比格斯威克的花园城更差的地方：科尼利厄斯·布兰德先生，一位来英国休假的南非绅士，租住在坦克雷德·吉姆森先生小屋的两间房里。

这座房子——或者照比格斯威克人喜欢的说法，这个“家”——是环着一片宜人的中部公地的大约两百座房子中的一座。造得糟糕，家具古怪：床太短，窗关不严，门关不住；可是肥皂、水同刷子能造出的干净，它都有了。四分之三英亩的园子主要种土豆，只是客厅留下，吉姆森太太有一小畦香草，几株瘦长的向日葵排在通向前门的小路两旁。我下站车时接待我的就是吉姆森太太——一个大块头、红脸膛的女人，头发叫风吹日晒裉了色，身上那件袍子，无论样子还是料子，都像照着一幅印花布窗帘裁的。她是个好人，心肠和善，为她那座房子骄傲得不得了。

“我们在这儿过筒朴日子，布兰德先生，”她说。“您只好将就我们这个样。”

我向她保证再好没有了；我在新崭崭的小卧室里拆行李，西风从窗口灌进来，我心想：比这更糟的住处我也住过。

我在伦敦买了不少书，想着闲的时候多，不妨给自己补一补教育。多半是英国古典，名字都知道，从没读过；全是一套平脊小丛书，一先令一本。我把它们排在五斗柜顶上，只有《天路历程》放在床头——那是我的一件家伙什，我得把它读熟。

===Original===

Mrs. Jimson, who came in while I was unpacking to see if the room was to my liking, approved my taste. At our midday dinner she wanted to discuss books with me, and was so full of her own knowledge that I was able to conceal my ignorance.
“We are all labouring to express our personalities,” she informed me. “Have you found your medium, Mr. Brand? is it to be the pen or the pencil? Or perhaps it is music? You have the brow of an artist, the frontal ‘bar of Michelangelo,’ you remember!”
I told her that I concluded I would try literature, but before writing anything I would read a bit more.
It was a Saturday, so Jimson came back from town in the early afternoon. He was a managing clerk in some shipping office, but you wouldn’t have guessed it from his appearance. His city clothes were loose dark-grey flannels, a soft collar, an orange tie, and a soft black hat. His wife went down the road to meet him, and they returned hand-in-hand, swinging their arms like a couple of schoolchildren. He had a skimpy red beard streaked with grey, and mild blue eyes behind strong glasses. He was the most friendly creature in the world, full of rapid questions, and eager to make me feel one of the family. Presently he got into a tweed Norfolk jacket, and started to cultivate his garden. I took off my coat and lent him a hand, and when he stopped to rest from his labours﻿—which was every five minutes, for he had no kind of physique﻿—he would mop his brow and rub his spectacles and declaim about the good smell of the earth and the joy of getting close to Nature.
Once he looked at my big brown hands and muscular arms with a kind of wistfulness. “You are one of the doers, Mr. Brand,” he said, “and I could find it in my heart to envy you. You have seen Nature in wild forms in far countries. Some day I hope you will tell us about your life. I must be content with my little corner, but happily there are no territorial limits for the mind. This modest dwelling is a watchtower from which I look over all the world.”
After that he took me for a walk. We met parties of returning tennis-players and here and there a golfer. There seemed to be an abundance of young men, mostly rather weedy-looking, but with one or two well-grown ones who should have been fighting. The names of some of them Jimson mentioned with awe.

===Chinese===

吉姆森太太进来查看屋子是否中意时看见了我的书，很是称赞。午饭时她要同我谈书，她自己的学问讲得那样满，我的无知正好藏得住。

“我们人人都在为表现个性而奋斗，”她开导我。“您找到您的媒介了么，布兰德先生？是笔，还是铅笔？要不，是音乐？您有一副艺术家的额头，‘米开朗基罗的额梁’，您记得！”

我告诉她，我打算试试文学；只是动笔之前，还要多读一点。

那天是星期六，吉姆森下午早早从城里回来。他在一家航运公司当主任 clerk，可看他的外表你猜不出。他的城里衣裳是宽大的深灰法兰绒裤、软领、一条橙红领带、一顶软塌塌的黑帽。他太太迎下坡去接他；两人手幸手回来，胳膊甩得像一对小学生。他一撇稀稀的红色胡子，夹着灰，厚镜片后一双温和的蓝眼睛。他是世上顶顶和气的人，问话又急又多，一心要把我当自家人。不多时他换上粗花呢的诺福克上衣，下园子侍弄；我脱了外套帮他搭手。他每千五分钟就得停下来喘气——他一点体格也没有——擦着脑门，擦着眼镜，朗诵泥土的香气、亲近自然的快乐。

有一回，他有点羡慕地看我这双棕色大手同粗壮的胳膊。“您是动手做事的那一类人，布兰德先生，”他说，“我真有点嫉妒您。您在远方见过野态的自然。哪天望您给我们讲讲您的生活。我只好守着我这个小角落；好在思想是没有疆界的。这座寒酸的小屋，是我的一座瞭望塔，从这里我看得见全世界。”

随后他带我去散步。我们遇见一队队打完网球回来的人，间或一个打高尔夫的。年轻人好像极多，多数瘦骨嶙峋，可也有一两个壮实的、本该去打仗的。有几个的名字，吉姆森提起来带着敬畏。

===Original===

An unwholesome youth was Aronson, the great novelist; a sturdy, bristling fellow with a fierce moustache was Letchford, the celebrated leader-writer of the *Critic*. Several were pointed out to me as artists who had gone one better than anybody else, and a vast billowy creature was described as the leader of the new Orientalism in England. I noticed that these people, according to Jimson, were all “great,” and that they all dabbled in something “new.” There were quantities of young women, too, most of them rather badly dressed and inclining to untidy hair. And there were several decent couples taking the air like householders of an evening all the world over. Most of these last were Jimson’s friends, to whom he introduced me. They were his own class﻿—modest folk, who sought for a coloured background to their prosaic city lives and found it in this odd settlement.
At supper I was initiated into the peculiar merits of Biggleswick.
“It is one great laboratory of thought,” said Mrs. Jimson. “It is glorious to feel that you are living among the eager, vital people who are at the head of all the newest movements, and that the intellectual history of England is being made in our studies and gardens. The war to us seems a remote and secondary affair. As someone has said, the great fights of the world are all fought in the mind.”
A spasm of pain crossed her husband’s face. “I wish I could feel it far away. After all, Ursula, it is the sacrifice of the young that gives people like us leisure and peace to think. Our duty is to do the best which is permitted to us, but that duty is a poor thing compared with what our young soldiers are giving! I may be quite wrong about the war﻿ ﻿… I know I can’t argue with Letchford. But I will not pretend to a superiority I do not feel.”
I went to bed feeling that in Jimson I had struck a pretty sound fellow. As I lit the candles on my dressing-table I observed that the stack of silver which I had taken out of my pockets when I washed before supper was top-heavy. It had two big coins at the top and sixpences and shillings beneath. Now it is one of my oddities that ever since I was a small boy I have arranged my loose coins symmetrically, with the smallest uppermost. That made me observant and led me to notice a second point. The English classics on the top of the chest of drawers were not in the order I had left them.

===Chinese===

阿龙森，那位大小说家，是个不健康的青年；莱奇福德，《批评家》的名主笔，是个结实、毛发倒竖的家伙，一撇凶猛的小胡子。还有几位被指点给我，说是比谁都高一头的艺术家；还有一个大浪头似的人物，被说成英格兰新东方主义的领袖。我注意到，据吉姆森说，这些人个个“伟大”，个个都摘一点“新”东西。年轻女人也多得是，多半衣衫穿得不当，头发趋于散乱。也有几对体面的夫妇在户外透气，同全世界的有房人家一个傍晚一个样。这后一批多半是吉姆森的朋友，他把我一一引见。他们是他的同类——谦卑的人家，给他们平淡的城里日子找一点彩色的背景，就找到了这个古怪的定居点。

晚饭时，我领教了比格斯威克的种种特别的好处。

“这是一座思想的巨大实验室，”吉姆森太太说。“同这些热切、有生命力的人住在一起——他们站在一切新运动的前头，英国的思想史正在我们的书房同园子里写出来——想想都光荣。战争，在我们看来是件遥远、次要的事。像有人说过的：世界上伟大的战斗，全是在头脑里打的。”

一丝痛楚掠过她丈夫的脸。“但愿我能觉得它远。说到底，厄休拉，正是因为年轻人牺牲，才给了我们这样的人安闲去思想。我们的本分是把许可我们做的事做到最好；可同我们年轻的士兵付出的相比，这点本分算得了什么！关于战争，我也许全错了……我知道我辩不过莱奇福德。可我不愿假装一种我感受不到的优越。”

上床时我觉得，吉姆森这人我算撞着个相当扎实的。我在梳妆台上点蜡烛时，发现晚饭前盟洗时从口袋里掏出的那摞银币头重脚轻：顶上是两枚大币，底下全是六便士同先令。我这人有个怪癖：从小时候起，零钱总要码得对称，最小的高在上。这叫我眼尖，又发现了第二点：五斗柜顶上那摞英国古典，不是我原先摆的次序。

===Original===

Izaak Walton had got to the left of Sir Thomas Browne, and the poet Burns was wedged disconsolately between two volumes of Hazlitt. Moreover a receipted bill which I had stuck in the *Pilgrim’s Progress* to mark my place had been moved. Someone had been going through my belongings.
A moment’s reflection convinced me that it couldn’t have been Mrs. Jimson. She had no servant and did the housework herself, but my things had been untouched when I left the room before supper, for she had come to tidy up before I had gone downstairs. Someone had been here while we were at supper, and had examined elaborately everything I possessed. Happily I had little luggage, and no papers save the new books and a bill or two in the name of Cornelius Brand. The inquisitor, whoever he was, had found nothing﻿ ﻿… The incident gave me a good deal of comfort. It had been hard to believe that any mystery could exist in this public place, where people lived brazenly in the open, and wore their hearts on their sleeves and proclaimed their opinions from the rooftops. Yet mystery there must be, or an inoffensive stranger with a kit-bag would not have received these strange attentions. I made a practice after that of sleeping with my watch below my pillow, for inside the case was Mary Lamington’s label.
---
Now began a period of pleasant idle receptiveness. Once a week it was my custom to go up to London for the day to receive letters and instructions, if any should come. I had moved from my chambers in Park Lane, which I leased under my proper name, to a small flat in Westminster taken in the name of Cornelius Brand. The letters addressed to Park Lane were forwarded to Sir Walter, who sent them round under cover to my new address. For the rest I used to spend my mornings reading in the garden, and I discovered for the first time what a pleasure was to be got from old books. They recalled and amplified that vision I had seen from the Cotswold ridge, the revelation of the priceless heritage which is England. I imbibed a mighty quantity of history, but especially I liked the writers, like Walton, who got at the very heart of the English countryside. Soon, too, I found the *Pilgrim’s Progress* not a duty but a delight. I discovered new jewels daily in the honest old story, and my letters to Peter began to be as full of it as Peter’s own epistles.

===Chinese===

艾萨克·沃尔顿挤到了托马斯·布朗爵士左边；诗人彭斯凄凄地夹在赫兹里特的两卷中间。还有，我夹在《天路历程》里当书签的一张付讫账单，也挪了地方。有人翻过我的东西。

想了一想，断定不会是吉姆森太太。她没有仆人，家务自己做；可我下楼吃晚饭前东西没动过——她在我下楼前已经收拾过屋子了。是有人趁我们吃晚饭的当儿进来，把我所有的东西细细查了一遍。幸好我行李少，除了那几本新书同一两张开给科尼利厄斯·布兰德的账单，没有片纸只字。那位检查官，不管是谁，一无所获……这件事倒给了我不小的安慰。在这样一个地方，人人明明白白住在光天化日下，心事挑在袖子上，主张站在屋顶上喊——要说这里还藏着什么神秘，本来很难信。可神秘必定有，不然一个提着行囊、与世无争的外乡人，不会受到这种奇怪的关照。打那以后，我养成习惯：睡觉把手表压在枕头底下——表壳里贴着玛丽·拉明顿的封缄。

---

现在开始了一段舒服的、闲散的、只管吸收的日子。每星期我照例上伦敦过一天，收信，等指令——如果有指令的话。我已从帕克巷以本名租的寓所搬出，搬进威斯敏斯特一套以科尼利厄斯·布兰德名义租下的小公寓。寄往帕克巷的信转给沃尔特爵士，他再设法转到我的新址。其余的日子，我上午在园子里读书；我头一回发现旧书里有多少乐趣。它们把我从科茨沃尔德山梁上望见的那幅景象召回、放大——英格兰这份无价遭产的启示。我灌下了大量的历史，尤其喜欢沃尔顿那样能攥住英国乡村心脏的作家。不久我也发现，《天路历程》不再是差事，而是快乐。在那本老实的老故事里我天天发现新的宝石；我给皮特的信，也开始像皮特自己的书信一样，满纸都是它。

===Original===

I loved, also, the songs of the Elizabethans, for they reminded me of the girl who had sung to me in the June night.
In the afternoons I took my exercise in long tramps along the good dusty English roads. The country fell away from Biggleswick into a plain of wood and pastureland, with low hills on the horizon. The Place was sown with villages, each with its green and pond and ancient church. Most, too, had inns, and there I had many a draught of cool nutty ale, for the inn at Biggleswick was a reformed place which sold nothing but washy cider. Often, tramping home in the dusk, I was so much in love with the land that I could have sung with the pure joy of it. And in the evening, after a bath, there would be supper, when a rather fagged Jimson struggled between sleep and hunger, and the lady, with an artistic mutch on her untidy head, talked ruthlessly of culture.
Bit by bit I edged my way into local society. The Jimsons were a great help, for they were popular and had a nodding acquaintance with most of the inhabitants. They regarded me as a meritorious aspirant towards a higher life, and I was paraded before their friends with the suggestion of a vivid, if Philistine, past. If I had any gift for writing, I would make a book about the inhabitants of Biggleswick. About half were respectable citizens who came there for country air and low rates, but even these had a touch of queerness and had picked up the jargon of the place. The younger men were mostly Government clerks or writers or artists. There were a few widows with flocks of daughters, and on the outskirts were several bigger houses﻿—mostly houses which had been there before the garden city was planted. One of them was brand-new, a staring villa with sham-antique timbering, stuck on the top of a hill among raw gardens. It belonged to a man called Moxon Ivery, who was a kind of academic pacificist and a great god in the place. Another, a quiet Georgian manor house, was owned by a London publisher, an ardent Liberal whose particular branch of business compelled him to keep in touch with the new movements. I used to see him hurrying to the station swinging a little black bag and returning at night with the fish for dinner.
I soon got to know a surprising lot of people, and they were the rummiest birds you can imagine.

===Chinese===

我还爱伊丽莎白时代的歌谣，因为它们叫我想起六月夜里为我唱歌的那个姑娘。

下午我的锻炼是长途跋涉，沿着英国那些很好的、尘土飞扬的大路。地势从比格斯威克落下去，是一片树林同草场的平原，地平线上是低低的山丘。这一带撒满了村庄，村村有草地、池塘同古老的教堂。多半还有酒馆；在那儿我喝过许多大杯凉丝丝、带果仁味的麦酒——比格斯威克本村的酒馆是个改良派的地方，只卖淡而无味的苹果酒。常常，在暮色里往回走，我爱这片土地爱得要唱歌，为它纯净的欢喜。晚上，洗过澡，吃晚饭：一个累得半死的吉姆森在睡意同饥饿中间挣扎；太太头上歪戴一顶艺术小睡帽，无情地大谈文化。

一点一点，我挤进了本地社会。吉姆森夫妇帮了大忙：他们人缘好，同大半住户都点头之交。他们当我是一个向上等生活努力的可造之才，带着我遍见他们的朋友，还暗示我有一段生动的——虽然俗气的——过去。我要是有写书的本事，真想写写比格斯威克的居民。约有一半是体面市民，图这里的乡间空气同低税率来的；可就连这些也沾了点怪气，学了几句本地的黑话。年轻人多半是政府书记、写字的、画画的。有几户寡妇，拖着一群女儿；外围还有几座大宅——多半是花园城种下之前就有的老宅。其中一座崭新，一座瞪眼的山庄，假古董的木骨架，戳在一座小山顶的生土园子中间。它属于一个叫莫克森·艾弗里的人——一位学院式的和平主义者，本地的头号神明。另一座安静的乔治时代庄园，属于一位伦敦出版商——一位热心的自由党人，他那门生意逼着他随时跟上新运动。我常见他拎一只小黑包急急奔车站，晚上拎着晚饭吃的鱼回来。

我很快认得了多得惊人的一群人，他们是你能想象的最古怪的一群鸟。

===Original===

For example, there were the Weekeses, three girls who lived with their mother in a house so artistic that you broke your head whichever way you turned in it. The son of the family was a conscientious objector who had refused to do any sort of work whatever, and had got quodded for his pains. They were immensely proud of him and used to relate his sufferings in Dartmoor with a gusto which I thought rather heartless. Art was their great subject, and I am afraid they found me pretty heavy going. It was their fashion never to admire anything that was obviously beautiful, like a sunset or a pretty woman, but to find surprising loveliness in things which I thought hideous. Also they talked a language that was beyond me. This kind of conversation used to happen.﻿—**Miss Weekes**: “Don’t you admire Ursula Jimson?” **Self**: “Rather!” **Miss W.**: “She is so John-esque in her lines.” **Self**: “Exactly!” **Miss W.**: “And Tancred, too﻿—he is so full of nuances.” **Self**: “Rather!” **Miss W.**: “He suggests one of Degousse’s countrymen.” **Self**: “Exactly!”
They hadn’t much use for books, except some Russian ones, and I acquired merit in their eyes for having read Leprous Souls. If you talked to them about that divine countryside, you found they didn’t give a rap for it and had never been a mile beyond the village. But they admired greatly the sombre effect of a train going into Marylebone station on a rainy day.
But it was the men who interested me most. Aronson, the novelist, proved on acquaintance the worst kind of blighter. He considered himself a genius whom it was the duty of the country to support, and he sponged on his wretched relatives and anyone who would lend him money. He was always babbling about his sins, and pretty squalid they were. I should like to have flung him among a few good old-fashioned full-blooded sinners of my acquaintance; they would have scared him considerably. He told me that he sought “reality” and “life” and “truth,” but it was hard to see how he could know much about them, for he spent half the day in bed smoking cheap cigarettes, and the rest sunning himself in the admiration of half-witted girls. The creature was tuberculous in mind and body, and the only novel of his I read, pretty well turned my stomach. Mr. Aronson’s strong point was jokes about the war.

===Chinese===

比方说威克斯家，三姐妹同母亲住在一座艺术得叫你随便朝哪边转都碰破头的房子里。家里的儿子是个良心拒服兵役者，什么活都拒绝干，因此蹲了监狱。她们为他骄傲得了不得，讲他在达特穆尔的受难，讲得津津有味——我觉得有点狠心。艺术是她们的大题目；我怕她们觉得我这个人很闷。她们的时尚是从不夸显然美的东西——落日、漂亮女人——偏要在我觉得丑的东西里寻出惊人的美。她们说的话我也听不懂。这一类谈话是家常便饭。——威克斯小姐：“你不赞赏厄休拉·吉姆森么？”我：“颇赞赏！”威：“她的线条多么约翰式。”我：“正是！”威：“还有坦克雷德——他浑身都是微妙。”我：“颇是！”威：“他叫人想起德古斯的一位同胞。”我：“正是！”

她们不大用得着书，除了几本俄国的；我因为读过《癞皮的灵魂》，在她们眼里得了几分体面。你要是同她们谈那神圣的乡野，就会发现她们毫不为它动心，从没出过村子一英里。可她们极口赞赏雨天一列火车开进马里勒波恩车站的那种阴沉效果。

但最叫我感兴趣的还是男人们。小说家阿龙森，一交往才知道是个最恶劣的坯子。他当自己是天才，国家有义务供养；他揩他那点穷亲戚的油，以及一切肯借钱给他的人。他永远喋喋不休他的罪，而那些罪也真够脏的。我真想把他扔到我的几位老派的、血气旺盛的罪人朋友中间去，保管吓他个半死。他对我说他追求“真实”、“生活”、“真理”；可他怎么能认得这三样，很难明白：他一天半天赖在床上抽廉价烟卷，剩下半天晒在傻姑娘们的崇拜里。这东西身心都是痨病性的；他那本我读过的唯一的小说，差点把我胃翻过来。阿龙森先生的强项是拿战争开玩笑。

===Original===

If he heard of any acquaintance who had joined up or was even doing war work his merriment knew no bounds. My fingers used to itch to box the little wretch’s ears.
Letchford was a different pair of shoes. He was some kind of a man, to begin with, and had an excellent brain and the worst manners conceivable. He contradicted everything you said, and looked out for an argument as other people look for their dinner. He was a double-engined, high-speed pacificist, because he was the kind of cantankerous fellow who must always be in a minority. If Britain had stood out of the war he would have been a raving militarist, but since she was in it he had got to find reasons why she was wrong. And jolly good reasons they were, too. I couldn’t have met his arguments if I had wanted to, so I sat docilely at his feet. The world was all crooked for Letchford, and God had created him with two left hands. But the fellow had merits. He had a couple of jolly children whom he adored, and he would walk miles with me on a Sunday, and spout poetry about the beauty and greatness of England. He was forty-five; if he had been thirty and in my battalion I could have made a soldier out of him.
There were dozens more whose names I have forgotten, but they had one common characteristic. They were puffed up with spiritual pride, and I used to amuse myself with finding their originals in the *Pilgrim’s Progress*. When I tried to judge them by the standard of old Peter, they fell woefully short. They shut out the war from their lives, some out of funk, some out of pure levity of mind, and some because they were really convinced that the thing was all wrong. I think I grew rather popular in my role of the seeker after truth, the honest colonial who was against the war by instinct and was looking for instruction in the matter. They regarded me as a convert from an alien world of action which they secretly dreaded, though they affected to despise it. Anyhow they talked to me very freely, and before long I had all the pacifist arguments by heart. I made out that there were three schools. One objected to war altogether, and this had few adherents except Aronson and Weekes, C.O., now languishing in Dartmoor. The second thought that the Allies’ cause was tainted, and that Britain had contributed as much as Germany to the catastrophe. This included all the adherents of the L.D.A.

===Chinese===

听说哪个熟人入伍了，或者哪怕在做战时工作，他的乐子就没边了。我的手指头好几次发痒，想掌这小无赖的嘴。

莱奇福德是另一码事。首先，他好歹算个男子汉，脑子一流，礼貌却坏得没法想。你说的每一句话他都要驳，找岔同人吵，像旁人找饭吃。他是个双引擎、高转速的和平主义者，因为他是那种不居于少数就活不下的禽牌气。英国当年若不参战，他会是咆哮的军国主义者；既然参了，他就得找出英国错的理由来——而且理由找得真不坏。我就是想驳也驳不动，只好乖乖坐他脚边听。对莱奇福德，世界全歪了，上帝造他时给了他一双左手。可这人有他的好处。他有两个可爱的孩子，他疼得心尖发颤；星期天他肯陪我走许多英里路，朗诵咏英格兰之美、之伟大的诗。他四十五岁；他要只有三十岁，又在我营里，我能把他练成一个兵。

还有几十个名字我忘了，可他们有一个共同点：全都叫灵性的骄傲撑着。我拿他们到《天路历程》里去找原型，自娱自乐。拿老皮特的尺子一量，他们短得可怜。他们把战争关在生活外头：有的出于怕，有的出于心性轻浮，有的真信这件事从头错起。我呢，扮的是一个求真理的人——一个凭本能反对战争的、诚实的殖民地人，正在寻人开导——扮着扮着，人倒真有点喜欢我了。他们当我是从一个他们暗中害怕、表面上又看不起的行动世界里来的归化者。总之他们对我说话很随便；不久，和平主义的全套论调我都背熟了。我盘算出三派。一派根本反对一切战争，信徒寥寥，只有阿龙森同那位现在达特穆尔坐牢的威克斯（拒服兵役者）。第二派认为协约国的事业不干净，论这场大灾，英国同德国出力一样多。这一派网罗了反侵略民主主义者同盟——

===Original===

﻿—or League of Democrats against Aggression﻿—a very proud body. The third and much the largest, which embraced everybody else, held that we had fought long enough and that the business could now be settled by negotiation, since Germany had learned her lesson. I was myself a modest member of the last school, but I was gradually working my way up to the second, and I hoped with luck to qualify for the first. My acquaintances approved my progress. Letchford said I had a core of fanaticism in my slow nature, and that I would end by waving the red flag.
Spiritual pride and vanity, as I have said, were at the bottom of most of them, and, try as I might, I could find nothing very dangerous in it all. This vexed me, for I began to wonder if the mission which I had embarked on so solemnly were not going to be a fiasco. Sometimes they worried me beyond endurance. When the news of Messines came nobody took the slightest interest, while I was aching to tooth every detail of the great fight. And when they talked on military affairs, as Letchford and others did sometimes, it was difficult to keep from sending them all to the devil, for their amateur cocksureness would have riled Job. One had got to batten down the recollection of our fellows out there who were sweating blood to keep these fools snug. Yet I found it impossible to be angry with them for long, they were so babyishly innocent. Indeed, I couldn’t help liking them, and finding a sort of quality in them. I had spent three years among soldiers, and the British regular, great fellow that he is, has his faults. His discipline makes him in a funk of red-tape and any kind of superior authority. Now these people were quite honest and in a perverted way courageous. Letchford was, at any rate. I could no more have done what he did and got hunted off platforms by the crowd and hooted at by women in the streets than I could have written his leading articles.
All the same I was rather low about my job. Barring the episode of the ransacking of my effects the first night, I had not a suspicion of a clue or a hint of any mystery. The place and the people were as open and bright as a Y.M.C.A. hut. But one day I got a solid wad of comfort. In a corner of Letchford’s paper, the *Critic*, I found a letter which was one of the steepest pieces of invective I had ever met with.

===Chinese===

简称L.D.A.——的全部信徒，一个很自负的团体。第三派，人数多得多，其余的人全算上：认为我们打够了，如今尽可谈判了结，因为德国已经受了教训。我自己是末一派里一个谦卑的成员，正一步步往第二派爬，运气好，还盼着够上第一派。熟人们都赞赏我的进步。莱奇福德说，我慢吞吞的天性里有一根狂热的芯子，早晚要打红旗。

我方才说了，灵性的骄傲同虚荣是他们大多数的底子；我怎么看，也看不出其中有多大的危险。这叫我发愁：我如此郑重接下的使命，别要变成一场空。有时候他们烦得我受不了。梅森的消息传来，没有一个人有丝毫兴趣，我却痒得恨不得把那场大战的每个细节都听全。他们一谈军事——莱奇福德几个偶尔也谈——我很难不叫他们统统见鬼去：他们那股业余的自作聪明，约伯也要被激怒。人只好把咱们那些在远方流血流汗、好叫这班傻瓜安生的弟兄的死记硬压下去。可我又没法长期生他们的气，他们天真得像婴孩。说真的，我不由得喜欢他们，觉得他们身上有一种质地。我在军人堆里住了三年；英国正规军是好汉，可有他的短处：纪律叫他怕文牍、怕一切上级权威。而这些人相当诚实，还有一副走了样的勇敢。莱奇福德至少是。他那种事——叫人群赶下讲台、叫街上女人喝倒彩——我做不到，正如我写不出他的社论。

话虽如此，我对我的差事相当消沉。除了头一晚东西被搜那一段，我没有一丝线索、一点神秘的影子。这地方这些人，亮堂得像一座基督教青年会小屋。可有一天，我拿到了一大块安慰。在莱奇福德那份《批评家》报的角落里，我读到一封信，一篇我平生仅见的顶刻毒的骂文。

===Original===

The writer gave tongue like a beagle pup about the prostitution, as he called it, of American republicanism to the vices of European aristocracies. He declared that Senator La Follette was a much-misunderstood patriot, seeing that he alone spoke for the toiling millions who had no other friend. He was mad with President Wilson, and he prophesied a great awakening when Uncle Sam got up against John Bull in Europe and found out the kind of standpatter he was. The letter was signed “John S. Blenkiron” and dated “London, 3 July.”
The thought that Blenkiron was in England put a new complexion on my business. I reckoned I would see him soon, for he wasn’t the man to stand still in his tracks. He had taken up the role he had played before he left in December 1915, and very right too, for not more than half a dozen people knew of the Erzerum affair, and to the British public he was only the man who had been fired out of the Savoy for talking treason. I had felt a bit lonely before, but now somewhere within the four corners of the island the best companion God ever made was writing nonsense with his tongue in his old cheek.
There was an institution in Biggleswick which deserves mention. On the south of the common, near the station, stood a redbrick building called the Moot Hall, which was a kind of church for the very undevout population. Undevout in the ordinary sense, I mean, for I had already counted twenty-seven varieties of religious conviction, including three Buddhists, a Celestial Hierarch, five Latter-day Saints, and about ten varieties of Mystic whose names I could never remember. The hall had been the gift of the publisher I have spoken of, and twice a week it was used for lectures and debates. The place was managed by a committee and was surprisingly popular, for it gave all the bubbling intellects a chance of airing their views. When you asked where somebody was and were told he was “at Moot,” the answer was spoken in the respectful tone in which you would mention a sacrament.
I went there regularly and got my mind broadened to cracking point. We had all the stars of the New Movements. We had Doctor Chirk, who lectured on “God,” which, as far as I could make out, was a new name he had invented for himself. There was a woman, a terrible woman, who had come back from Russia with what she called a “message of healing.”

===Chinese===

写信人像一只小猎犬那样开腔，大骂所谓美利坚共和主义卖身给欧洲贵族的恶德。他断言拉福莱特参议员是一位深受误解的爱国者，因为只有他一个人替那没有别的朋友的、千辛万苦的劳苦大众说话。他气得要死威尔逊总统，还预言：等山姆大叔在欧洲同约翰牛较上了劲、看清楚了他是哪一号圆滑的老顽固，就会有一场大觉醒。信的署名是“约翰·S. 布伦基伦”，日期“伦敦，七月三日”。

布伦基伦在英国的这个消息，给我的差事换了一副面孔。我盘算很快会见着他，他不是站在原地不动的人。他又抬起了一九一五年十二月离英前扮演的那个角色，而且抬得对：知道埃尔祖鲁姆那件事的，拢共不过五六个人；在英国公众眼里，他只是那个因为在萨伏伊饭店里大谈叛国话被撵出来的人。先前我有点孤单，如今，在这座岛的四角之内某处，上帝造下的最好的一位伙伴，正鼓着腮帮子写他的胡话。

比格斯威克有一处机构值得一提。公地南边、车站附近，立着一座红砖房子，叫议事堂，是为这群极不虔诚的居民设的一座教堂。我说的是寻常意义的不虔诚：我早已数出二十七种宗教信仰，包括三名佛教徒、一位天界等级派、五名末日圣徒，同大约十种我永远记不住名字的神秘派。这座堂子是我说过的那位出版商捐的，每星期两次用于演讲同辩论。管理它的是一个委员会；它出奇地受欢迎，因为它给所有这些沸腾的头脑一个亮相的机会。你问某某人在哪儿，人家告诉你“在议事会呢”，那口气恭敬得像提到一件圣事。

我常去，把脑子撑到裂缝。新运动的明星们我们听了个遍。有奇克博士，讲“神”——据我听明白的，那是他给自己新起的名字。有一位太太，一位可怕的太太，从俄国回来，带着她所谓的“疗愈的音信”。

===Original===

And to my joy, one night there was a great buck nigger who had a lot to say about “Africa for the Africans.” I had a few words with him in Sesutu afterwards, and rather spoiled his visit. Some of the people were extraordinarily good, especially one jolly old fellow who talked about English folk songs and dances, and wanted us to set up a maypole. In the debates which generally followed I began to join, very coyly at first, but presently with some confidence. If my time at Biggleswick did nothing else it taught me to argue on my feet.
The first big effort I made was on a full-dress occasion, when Launcelot Wake came down to speak. Mr. Ivery was in the chair﻿—the first I had seen of him﻿—a plump middle-aged man, with a colourless face and nondescript features. I was not interested in him till he began to talk, and then I sat bolt upright and took notice. For he was the genuine silver-tongue, the sentences flowing from his mouth as smooth as butter and as neatly dovetailed as a parquet floor. He had a sort of man-of-the-world manner, treating his opponents with condescending geniality, deprecating all passion and exaggeration and making you feel that his urbane statement must be right, for if he had wanted he could have put the case so much higher. I watched him, fascinated, studying his face carefully; and the thing that struck me was that there was nothing in it﻿—nothing, that is to say, to lay hold on. It was simply nondescript, so almightily commonplace that that very fact made it rather remarkable.
Wake was speaking of the revelations of the Sukhomhnov trial in Russia, which showed that Germany had not been responsible for the war. He was jolly good at the job, and put as clear an argument as a first-class lawyer. I had been sweating away at the subject and had all the ordinary case at my fingers’ ends, so when I got a chance of speaking I gave them a long harangue, with some good quotations I had cribbed out of the *Vossische Zeitung*, which Letchford lent me. I felt it was up to me to be extra violent, for I wanted to establish my character with Wake, seeing that he was a friend of Mary and Mary would know that I was playing the game. I got tremendously applauded, far more than the chief speaker, and after the meeting Wake came up to me with his hot eyes, and wrung my hand. “You’re coming on well, Brand,” he said, and then he introduced me to Mr. Ivery.

===Chinese===

叫我喜欢的是，有一晚来了一位了不得的黑人绅士，就“非洲人的非洲”大发宏论。会后我用塞苏陀语同他讲了几句，把他的来访搅得颇败兴。有些人非常之好，尤其一位快活的老先生，讲英国的民歌民舞，主张我们竖一根五朔节花柱。随后照例举行的辩论里，我也开始加入，起初怯生生的，后来有了几分自信。我在比格斯威克的日子要是没干别的，至少教会了我站着说话。

我头一回大出风头，是一个隆重的场合：朗斯洛·韦克下来演讲。艾弗里先生做主席——这是我头一回见他——一位胖胖的中年人，一张没有颜色的脸，五官没有特征。起初我不在意他，等他一开口，我立刻坐直了。因为他是真正的银舌头，句子从他嘴里流出来，滑得像黄油，咬合得像拼花地板。他有一副阅世老手的派头：对对手带着屈尊的和气，痛惜一切激愤同夸大，叫你觉着他这文雅的说法必定不错——因为他若愿意，本可以把这道理讲得高得多。我入迷地看着他，细细研究他的脸；打动我的是：那张脸里什么也没有——也就是说，没有一处抓得住。它简直是“无特征”，平常到可怕，而这份平常本身，反倒叫它相当不平常。

韦克讲的是俄国苏霍姆利诺夫审判的披露，说那表明德国对战争并无责任。他干这一行干得很漂亮，论证清楚得像一位头等律师。这题目我下过死功夫，寻常的案子倒背如流；轮到我发言，我给了他们一篇长篇大论，还引了几段好引文，是从莱奇福德借我的《福斯报》上剽的。我自觉必须格外激烈，因为我要在韦克面前立住人设——他是玛丽的朋友，玛丽会知道我在守规矩。我赢得了雷动的掌声，远多过主讲人；会后韦克走来，一双眼睛冒着火，攥住我的手。“你有长进，布兰德，”他说；随后把我介绍给艾弗里先生。

===Original===

“Here’s a second and a better Smuts,” he said.
Ivery made me walk a bit of the road home with him. “I am struck by your grip on these difficult problems, Mr. Brand,” he told me. “There is much I can tell you, and you may be of great value to our cause.” He asked me a lot of questions about my past, which I answered with easy mendacity. Before we parted he made me promise to come one night to supper.
Next day I got a glimpse of Mary, and to my vexation she cut me dead. She was walking with a flock of bareheaded girls, all chattering hard, and though she saw me quite plainly she turned away her eyes. I had been waiting for my cue, so I did not lift my hat, but passed on as if we were strangers. I reckoned it was part of the game, but that trifling thing annoyed me, and I spent a morose evening.
The following day I saw her again, this time talking sedately with Mr. Ivery, and dressed in a very pretty summer gown, and a broad-brimmed straw hat with flowers in it. This time she stopped with a bright smile and held out her hand. “Mr. Brand, isn’t it?” she asked with a pretty hesitation. And then, turning to her companion﻿—“This is Mr. Brand. He stayed with us last month in Gloucestershire.”
Mr. Ivery announced that he and I were already acquainted. Seen in broad daylight he was a very personable fellow, somewhere between forty-five and fifty, with a middle-aged figure and a curiously young face. I noticed that there were hardly any lines on it, and it was rather that of a very wise child than that of a man. He had a pleasant smile which made his jaw and cheeks expand like indiarubber. “You are coming to sup with me, Mr. Brand,” he cried after me. “On Tuesday after Moot. I have already written.” He whisked Mary away from me, and I had to content myself with contemplating her figure till it disappeared round a bend of the road.
Next day in London I found a letter from Peter. He had been very solemn of late, and very reminiscent of old days now that he concluded his active life was over. But this time he was in a different mood. “I think,” he wrote, “that you and I will meet again soon, my old friend. Do you remember when we went after the big black-maned lion in the Rooirand and couldn’t get on his track, and then one morning we woke up and said we would get him today?﻿—and we did, but he very near got you first.

===Chinese===

“这是第二个、也是更好的一个斯穆茨，”他说。

艾弗里要我陪他走一段回家的路。“你把握这些难题的功夫叫我吃惊，布兰德先生，”他对我说。“有许多我能告诉你，你也许对我们的事业大有用处。”他问了我许多我的过去，我用撒谎的娴熟一一作答。分手前，他要我答应哪天晚上去他家吃晚饭。

第二天我望见了玛丽一眼，叫我恼的是，她把我当空气。她同一群光头的姑娘走着，人人唧唧喳喳；她明明看见了我，却把眼睛掉开。我一直在等我的戏眼，便也不脱帽，像陌路人一样走过去。我盘算这是戏里的一手，可这件小事硌了我一下，我闷闷地过了一个晚上。

第二天我又见到她，这一回正同艾弗里先生斯斯文文地谈话，穿一件顶漂亮的夏裙，戴一顶插花的宽边草帽。这一回她带着明亮的笑站住，伸出手来。“是布兰德先生吧？”她问，漂亮地顿了一顿。随后转向同伴——“这是布兰德先生。上个月他在格洛斯特郡住过我们家。”

艾弗里先生声明，他同我早已认识。大天光里看，他是位相当体面的家伙，四十五到五十之间，中年的身段，一张奇怪地年轻的脸。我注意到那脸上几乎没有纹路，与其说是一张男人的脸，不如说是一个极聪明的孩子的脸。他有一个愉快的微笑，笑起来下巴同腮帮像橡皮一样张开。“您要来我家吃晚饭，布兰德先生，”他在我身后喊。“星期二，议事会之后。信我已经写了。”他把玛丽从我身边带走了，我只好望着她的背影，直到它在路拐弯处消失。

第二天在伦敦，我收到皮特的信。近来他变得很郑重，认定他的活跃岁月结束了，便频频回忆旧日。可这一回他换了一种心情。“我想，”他写道，“你我很快又要见面了，我的老朋友。还记得咱们在鲁伊兰德追那头黑鬃大狮，怎么也踩不着它的踪，后来一天早晨咱们醒来说：今天拿下它——也真拿下了，可它差一点先拿下你。

===Original===

I’ve had a feel these last days that we’re both going down into the Valley to meet with Apolyon, and that the devil will give us a bad time, but anyhow we’ll be together.”
I had the same kind of feel myself, though I didn’t see how Peter and I were going to meet, unless I went out to the Front again and got put in the bag and sent to the same Boche prison. But I had an instinct that my time in Biggleswick was drawing to a close, and that presently I would be in rougher quarters. I felt quite affectionate towards the place, and took all my favourite walks, and drank my own health in the brew of the village inns, with a consciousness of saying goodbye. Also I made haste to finish my English classics, for I concluded I wouldn’t have much time in the future for miscellaneous reading.
The Tuesday came, and in the evening I set out rather late for the Moot Hall, for I had been getting into decent clothes after a long, hot stride. When I reached the place it was pretty well packed, and I could only find a seat on the back benches. There on the platform was Ivery, and beside him sat a figure that thrilled every inch of me with affection and a wild anticipation. “I have now the privilege,” said the chairman, “of introducing to you the speaker whom we so warmly welcome, our fearless and indefatigable American friend, Mr. Blenkiron.”
It was the old Blenkiron, but almightily changed. His stoutness had gone, and he was as lean as Abraham Lincoln. Instead of a puffy face, his cheekbones and jaw stood out hard and sharp, and in place of his former pasty colour his complexion had the clear glow of health. I saw now that he was a splendid figure of a man, and when he got to his feet every movement had the suppleness of an athlete in training. In that moment I realized that my serious business had now begun. My senses suddenly seemed quicker, my nerves tenser, my brain more active. The big game had started, and he and I were playing it together.
I watched him with strained attention. It was a funny speech, stuffed with extravagance and vehemence, not very well argued and terribly discursive. His main point was that Germany was now in a fine democratic mood and might well be admitted into a brotherly partnership﻿—that indeed she had never been in any other mood, but had been forced into violence by the plots of her enemies.

===Chinese===

这几天我有一种感觉：咱们俩都要下到谷底去会亚玻伦，那魔鬼要给咱们一顿苦头；可不管怎样，咱们是在一处的。”

我自己也有同一种感觉，虽然我看不出皮特同我怎么个见面法，除非我再上前线、叫人家装了口袋、送进同一座德国战俘营。可我有一种直觉：我在比格斯威克的日子快到头了，不久我就要住更粗粝的 quarters。我对这地方竟生出感情来，把心爱的散步路线统统走了一遍，在各村酒馆里用自己的酒祝自己的健康，带着道别的心思。我还赶着读完我的英国古典：往后的日子，怕是没有多少闲功夫杂读了。

星期二到了。晚上我出发得挺晚——先狠狠出一身汗，再换上体面衣裳。到议事堂时已挤得满满，我只在后排寻着一个座。台上是艾弗里；他旁边坐着一个人影，叫我从头到脚每一寸都热起来——那是爱，是狂喜的期待。“我现在有幸，”主席说，“向诸位介绍我们热烈欢迎的演讲人：我们无畏的、不懈的美国朋友，布伦基伦先生。”

是老布伦基伦，可大大地换了一个人。他的胖没有了，瘦得像亚伯拉罕·林肯。浮肿的脸没有了，颧骨同下颚又硬又利地凸出；从前那副浆糊气色没有了，换上健康的清亮的光。我这才看清他是条出色的汉子；他站起来，一举一动都有在训运动员的柔韧。那一刻我明白：我的正经买卖开场了。我的感官忽然变快，神经绷紧，脑子转得更急。大猎开局了，他同我在一伙。

我屏着气看他演讲。那是一篇古怪的演说，满纸夸张同激烈，论证不怎么样，又散得厉害。他的主旨是：德国如今心情极好、极民主，尽可以接纳进兄弟的伙伴关系——其实她从来不是别的心情，全是叫敌人的阴谋逼得动了粗。

===Original===

Much of it, I should have thought, was in stark defiance of the Defence of the Realm Acts, but if any wise Scotland Yard officer had listened to it he would probably have considered it harmless because of its contradictions. It was full of a fierce earnestness, and it was full of humour﻿—long-drawn American metaphors at which that most critical audience roared with laughter. But it was not the kind of thing that they were accustomed to, and I could fancy what Wake would have said of it. The conviction grew upon me that Blenkiron was deliberately trying to prove himself an honest idiot. If so, it was a huge success. He produced on one the impression of the type of sentimental revolutionary who ruthlessly knifes his opponent and then weeps and prays over his tomb.
Just at the end he seemed to pull himself together and to try a little argument. He made a great point of the Austrian socialists going to Stockholm, going freely and with their Government’s assent, from a country which its critics called an autocracy, while the democratic western peoples held back. “I admit I haven’t any real watertight proof,” he said, “but I will bet my bottom dollar that the influence which moved the Austrian Government to allow this embassy of freedom was the influence of Germany herself. And that is the land from which the Allied Pharisees draw in their skirts lest their garments be defiled!”
He sat down amid a good deal of applause, for his audience had not been bored, though I could see that some of them thought his praise of Germany a bit steep. It was all right in Biggleswick to prove Britain in the wrong, but it was a slightly different thing to extol the enemy. I was puzzled about his last point, for it was not of a piece with the rest of his discourse, and I was trying to guess at his purpose. The chairman referred to it in his concluding remarks. “I am in a position,” he said, “to bear out all that the lecturer has said. I can go further. I can assure him on the best authority that his surmise is correct, and that Vienna’s decision to send delegates to Stockholm was largely dictated by representations from Berlin. I am given to understand that the fact has in the last few days been admitted in the Austrian press.”
A vote of thanks was carried, and then I found myself shaking hands with Ivery while Blenkiron stood a yard off, talking to one of the Misses Weekes.

===Chinese===

其中多半，我看是公然以身试《国土防卫法》；可哪位精明的苏格兰场警官要听了，多半会因为它自相矛盾，反而认为无害。它充满凶猛的诚恳，也充满幽默——长长的美式比喻，叫那最挑剔的听众哄堂大笑。可这不是他们听惯的东西，我想象得出韦克会怎么评它。一个信念在我心里长成：布伦基伦在故意证明自己是个诚实的傻子。若是这样，成绩辉煌。他给人的印象，是那种感情用事的革命家：一刀捅死对手，再对着坟头流泪祷告。

临结束，他似乎定了一定神，试着论证了一下。他大讲奥地利社会党人去斯德哥尔摩——自由地、得政府同意地去——而那是个被批评家称为专制国的国家；民主的西方各国反倒缩着手。这一点他大做文章。“我承认我没有真正滴水不漏的证据，”他说，“可我拿我最后一块钱打赌：叫奥地利政府放行这趟自由使团的势力，正是德国自己的势力。而协约国的法利赛人，正提起衣角不敢沾那块地界，生怕脏了袍子！”

他在一片掌声中坐下。他的听众不曾无聊，可我看得出，有人觉得他对德国的赞美有点过头。在比格斯威克，证明英国错了尽可以；捧敌人，就稍微是另一回事了。我为最后那一点纳闷：它同全篇演说不成一体；我正想猜他的用意，主席在结束词里替我解了。“我可以证实，”他说，“演讲人所说的一切。我还能更进一步。我可以拿最可靠的消息向他保证：他的猜测不错，维也纳派代表赴斯德哥尔摩的决定，多半出自柏林的授意。据我所知，奥地利报纸近几天已经承认了这一事实。”

表决了谢意；随后我发现自己在同艾弗里握手，布伦基伦站在一码开外，同威克斯家的一位小姐说话。

===Original===

The next moment I was being introduced.
“Mr. Brand, very pleased to meet you,” said the voice I knew so well. “Mr. Ivery has been telling me about you, and I guess we’ve got something to say to each other. We’re both from noo countries, and we’ve got to teach the old nations a little horse-sense.”
Mr. Ivery’s car﻿—the only one left in the neighbourhood﻿—carried us to his villa, and presently we were seated in a brightly-lit dining-room. It was not a pretty house, but it had the luxury of an expensive hotel, and the supper we had was as good as any London restaurant. Gone were the old days of fish and toast and boiled milk. Blenkiron squared his shoulders and showed himself a noble trencherman.
“A year ago,” he told our host, “I was the meanest kind of dyspeptic. I had the love of righteousness in my heart, but I had the devil in my stomach. Then I heard stories about the Robson Brothers, the star surgeons way out west in White Springs, Nebraska. They were reckoned the neatest hands in the world at carving up a man and removing devilments from his intestines. Now, sir, I’ve always fought pretty shy of surgeons, for I considered that our Maker never intended His handiwork to be reconstructed like a bankrupt Dago railway. But by that time I was feeling so almighty wretched that I could have paid a man to put a bullet through my head. ‘There’s no other way,’ I said to myself. ‘Either you forget your religion and your miserable cowardice and get cut up, or it’s you for the Golden Shore.’ So I set my teeth and journeyed to White Springs, and the Brothers had a look at my duodenum. They saw that the darned thing wouldn’t do, so they sidetracked it and made a noo route for my noo-trition traffic. It was the cunningest piece of surgery since the Lord took a rib out of the side of our First Parent. They’ve got a mighty fine way of charging, too, for they take five percent of a man’s income, and it’s all one to them whether he’s a Meat King or a clerk on twenty dollars a week. I can tell you I took some trouble to be a very rich man last year.”
All through the meal I sat in a kind of stupor. I was trying to assimilate the new Blenkiron, and drinking in the comfort of his heavenly drawl, and I was puzzling my head about Ivery. I had a ridiculous notion that I had seen him before, but, delve as I might into my memory, I couldn’t place him.

===Chinese===

下一刻，我被介绍过去。

“布兰德先生，幸会幸会，”那副我再熟不过的嗓音说。“艾弗里先生刚同我谈起您；我想咱们有话要谈。咱们都是从新国家来的，得教教老牌国家一点马匹的常识。”

艾弗里先生的汽车——附近仅剩的一辆——把我们载到他的山庄；不多时，我们已在灯烛通明的饭厅里落座。房子不好看，却有豪华旅馆的奢侈；那顿晚饭，赶得上伦敦任何一家馆子。鱼、吐司同煮牛奶的旧日子，一去不返了。布伦基伦抡起肩膀，显出一位高贵的饕餮的本色。

“一年前，”他对主人说，“我是最没出息的那种胃病汉子。心里装着对正义的爱，肚子里却住着魔鬼。后来我听说了罗布森兄弟——远在西边内布拉斯加白泉的明星外科医生。世上最会开膛破肚、从人肠子里摘魔鬼的一双手，数他们。呃，先生，我一辈子最躲的就是外科医生，因为我认为，造物主从没打算叫他的手艺像一条破产的达戈铁路那样翻修。可到那时，我难受得情愿出钱请人给我脑袋来一枪。‘没有别的路了，’我对自己说。‘要么你忘了你的宗教、忘了你这可怜的胆小，挨那一刀；要么你就上黄金岸去。’于是我咬紧牙关上了白泉；罗布森兄弟瞧了瞧我的十二指肠。他们看出那玩意儿不成了，就把它撇到岔道上，给我的营养运输另开了一条新路。这是自打主从咱们始祖肋边抽出一根肋骨以来最巧的一刀。他们的收费办法也漂亮：抽一个人收入的百分之五，管他是肉类大王还是二十块钱一礼拜的书记，一视同仁。告诉您，去年我着实费了些劲，才做成一个非常有钱的人。”

整顿饭，我坐在那里发懵。我在努力消化这个新的布伦基伦，畅饮他那天堂般的拖腔；同时为艾弗里绞脑汁。我有个荒唐的念头：我从前见过他。可我在记忆里怎么挖，也放不准他。

===Original===

He was the incarnation of the commonplace, a comfortable middle-class sentimentalist, who patronized pacificism out of vanity, but was very careful not to dip his hands too far. He was always damping down Blenkiron’s volcanic utterances. “Of course, as you know, the other side have an argument which I find rather hard to meet﻿ ﻿…” “I can sympathize with patriotism, and even with jingoism, in certain moods, but I always come back to this difficulty.” “Our opponents are not ill-meaning so much as ill-judging,”﻿—these were the sort of sentences he kept throwing in. And he was full of quotations from private conversations he had had with every sort of person﻿—including members of the Government. I remember that he expressed great admiration for Mr. Balfour.
Of all that talk, I only recalled one thing clearly, and I recalled it because Blenkiron seemed to collect his wits and try to argue, just as he had done at the end of his lecture. He was speaking about a story he had heard from someone, who had heard it from someone else, that Austria in the last week of July 1914 had accepted Russia’s proposal to hold her hand and negotiate, and that the Kaiser had sent a message to the Tsar saying he agreed. According to his story this telegram had been received in Petrograd, and had been rewritten, like Bismarck’s Ems telegram, before it reached the Emperor. He expressed his disbelief in the yarn. “I reckon if it had been true,” he said, “we’d have had the right text out long ago. They’d have kept a copy in Berlin. All the same I did hear a sort of rumour that some kind of message of that sort was published in a German paper.”
Mr. Ivery looked wise. “You are right,” he said. “I happen to know that it has been published. You will find it in the *Wiener Zeitung*.”
“You don’t say?” he said admiringly. “I wish I could read the old tombstone language. But if I could they wouldn’t let me have the papers.”
“Oh yes they would.” Mr. Ivery laughed pleasantly. “England has still a good share of freedom. Any respectable person can get a permit to import the enemy press. I’m not considered quite respectable, for the authorities have a narrow definition of patriotism, but happily I have respectable friends.”
Blenkiron was staying the night, and I took my leave as the clock struck twelve.

===Chinese===

他是“平凡”的化身：一个舒舒服服的中产阶级感情家，出于虚荣偏爱和平主义，又万分小心，不肯把手伸得太深。他不住给布伦基伦的火山喷发泼水。“当然，您知道，对方有一条论证，我觉得颇难应对……”“我同情爱国心，甚至同情沙文主义——在某些心境里——可我总回到这个难处上。”“我们的对手与其说存心不良，不如说判断不良。”——他撒进来的全是这一路句子。他还满口他同各种人物——包括政府阁员——私下谈话的引文。我记得他对贝尔福先生表示了极大的仰慕。

那一席话里，我只清清楚楚记住了一件事；之所以记住，是因为布伦基伦似乎又定了定神、认真论证起来——像他讲演结尾那样。他讲一个他从某人听来、某人又从另一人听来的故事：说一九一四年七月最后一周，奥地利接受了俄国的提议——停手、谈判；德皇也给沙皇发了电报，表示同意。照这故事，这封电报到了彼得格勒之后、送到沙皇之前，被人改写了——同俾斯麦那封埃姆斯电报一个路数。他表示不信这个传闻。“我看若真有这回事，”他说，“原文早该出来了。柏林会留一份抄件。话虽如此，我倒确实听过一点风声，说德国有家报纸登过类似的一封信件。”

艾弗里先生显出行家的神气。“你说得对，”他说。“我碰巧知道确实登过。你去查《维也纳日报》。”

“不至于吧？”他佩服地说。“真想读读那门老碑文的语言。可就算我读得懂，他们也不会让报纸进来。”

“哦，会让的。”艾弗里先生愉快地笑。“英国还保有很大一份自由。任何体面人都可以领到进口敌国报刊的许可。我么，人家觉得我不大体面——当局对爱国的定义很窄——好在有的是体面的朋友。”

布伦基伦留下过夜；钟敲十二点时，我起身告辞。

===Original===

They both came into the hall to see me off, and, as I was helping myself to a drink, and my host was looking for my hat and stick, I suddenly heard Blenkiron’s whisper in my ear. “London﻿ ﻿… the day after tomorrow,” he said. Then he took a formal farewell. “Mr. Brand, it’s been an honour for me, as an American citizen, to make your acquaintance, sir. I will consider myself fortunate if we have an early reunion. I am stopping at Claridge’s Ho-tel, and I hope to be privileged to receive you there.”

===Chinese===

两人都送到门厅。我正自斟一杯酒，主人替我找帽子手杖，忽然，布伦基伦的耳语落进我耳朵：“伦敦……后天。”随后他一本正经地告别：“布兰德先生，作为一个美国公民，能结识您是我的荣幸，先生。若能早日重逢，我当引为幸事。我下榻克拉里奇饭店，盼有福气在那里恭候您。”

## I The Wicket-Gate / 一 窄门
===Original===

I spent one-third of my journey looking out of the window of a first-class carriage, the next in a local motorcar following the course of a trout stream in a shallow valley, and the last tramping over a ridge of downland through great beech-woods to my quarters for the night. In the first part I was in an infamous temper; in the second I was worried and mystified; but the cool twilight of the third stage calmed and heartened me, and I reached the gates of Fosse Manor with a mighty appetite and a quiet mind.
As we slipped up the Thames valley on the smooth Great Western line I had reflected ruefully on the thorns in the path of duty. For more than a year I had never been out of khaki, except the months I spent in hospital. They gave me my battalion before the Somme, and I came out of that weary battle after the first big September fighting with a crack in my head and a D.S.O. I had received a C.B. for the Erzerum business, so what with these and my Matabele and South African medals and the Legion of Honour, I had a chest like the High Priest’s breastplate. I rejoined in January, and got a brigade on the eve of Arras. There we had a star turn, and took about as many prisoners as we put infantry over the top. After that we were hauled out for a month, and subsequently planted in a bad bit on the Scarpe with a hint that we would soon be used for a big push. Then suddenly I was ordered home to report to the War Office, and passed on by them to Bullivant and his merry men. So here I was sitting in a railway carriage in a grey tweed suit, with a neat new suitcase on the rack labelled C. B. The initials stood for Cornelius Brand, for that was my name now. And an old boy in the corner was asking me questions and wondering audibly why I wasn’t fighting, while a young blood of a second lieutenant with a wound stripe was eyeing me with scorn.
The old chap was one of the cross-examining type, and after he had borrowed my matches he set to work to find out all about me. He was a tremendous fire-eater, and a bit of a pessimist about our slow progress in the west. I told him I came from South Africa and was a mining engineer.
“Been fighting with Botha?” he asked.
“No,” I said. “I’m not the fighting kind.”
The second lieutenant screwed up his nose.
“Is there no conscription in South Africa?”

===Chinese===

旅程的头一段，我在头等车厢里望窗外；第二段坐一辆本地汽车，顺着一条浅谷里的鳟鱼溪走；末一段靠两条腿，翻一道白垩冈，穿过大片山毛榉林，到我过夜的宿处。第一段路上我火气坏透；第二段心里又烦又疑；可第三段清凉的暮色叫我定下神、鼓起劲，到福斯庄园（Fosse Manor）大门口时，我胃口极好，心境极静。

在大西部线的滑轨上沿泰晤士河谷北去时，我悻悻地想者本分路上的刺。一年多来我没脱过卡其布，住医院的几个月除外。索姆河之前他们给了我一个营，我在那场疲惫的大战里熬过九月头一场大仗，出来时脑袋里一条裂缝，外加一枚杰出服务勋章。埃尔祖鲁姆那件事我得过渡斯式的巴思勋章，加上马塔贝列和南非的战役章和荣誉军团勋章，我前胸挂得像大祭司的胸牌。一月里我回队，阿拉斯前夜得了一个旅。我们在那里露了一手漂亮戏，抓的俘虏差不多同我们送过顶的步兵一样多。随后拉下来休整一个月，再往后栽在斯卡普河一段坏地上，风声说快有大仗用我们了。忽然一道令调我回国，报到陆军部，又转给比利文特和他那伙快活人。于是我就坐在了这儿，一节车厢里，一身灰色呢衣，行李架上 一只崭新的小提箱，标签上 C.B. 两个字母。那是科尼利厄斯·布兰德（Cornelius Brand）的缩写，如今我就叫这个。角落里一位老先生正盘问我，大声纳闷我怎么不去打仗；一个带伤条儿的年轻少尉军官斜着眼看我。

老头是盘问型的那路人，借了我的火柴，就动手查我的底。他是个天大的主战派，又对西线的慢劲有点悲观。我说我从南非来，是个采矿工程师。

“同博塔打过仗？”他问。

“没有，”我说。“我不是打仗那路人。”

少尉皱了皱鼻子。

“南非没有征兵么？”

===Original===

“Thank God there isn’t,” I said, and the old fellow begged permission to tell me a lot of unpalatable things. I knew his kind and didn’t give much for it.
